### arXiv 2611.03647 - Pixel-UMI: Learning Environment-Aware Whole-Body Manipulation from Sensorized Human Demonstrations

- categories: cs.RO, cs.AI, cs.CV, cs.LG, eess.SY
- published: 2026-11-02T18:59:32Z
- authors: Jason Jingzhou Liu, Tony Tao, Deepak Pathak, Ruslan Salakhutdinov
- query-set: cs.AI
- abs: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.03647

Sensorized human demonstrations offer scalable data for mobile manipulation, but embodiment differences complicate policy learning. We present Pixel-UMI, a pipeline for learning environment-aware whole-body policies from human data. We first introduce Environment-Aware Retargeting (EAR), which combines reconstructed scene geometry with demonstrated head and gripper trajectories to determine robot posture and generate collision-aware joint and base targets. Delay-calibrated action assignment converts these trajectories into actions. However, kinematic differences can leave residual pose errors, creating viewpoint mismatches between human demonstrations and robot observations. We therefore introduce Retargeting-Error-Aware Masking (REAM), which uses these errors during training to reduce reliance on mismatched views at deployment. Finally, because estimated actions cannot fully capture contact-rich interactions, we collect on-policy human corrections through DAgger to improve performance. We evaluate Pixel-UMI on five challenging tasks spanning articulated-object manipulation, deformable-object handling, constrained-space reaching, whole-body collision avoidance, and contact-rich bimanual coordination, achieving 87.0% average task progress. Video results are available at https://pixel-robot.com/pixel-umi


### arXiv 2611.03648 - Pixel: A Co-Designed Full-Stack Robot System for Learning General-Purpose Mobile Manipulation

- categories: cs.RO, cs.AI, cs.CV, cs.LG, eess.SY
- published: 2026-11-02T18:59:32Z
- authors: Tony Tao, Jason Jingzhou Liu, Ruslan Salakhutdinov, Deepak Pathak
- query-set: cs.AI
- abs: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.03648

General-purpose mobile manipulation requires a combination of capable robot hardware, scalable demonstration collection, expressive whole-body control, and effective policy learning. Yet these components are often developed independently, creating mismatches that limit the capabilities of the complete system. We present Pixel, a co-designed full-stack system for learning general-purpose mobile manipulation. Pixel combines a low-cost wheeled humanoid with human-aligned kinematics and compliant grippers, multiple teleoperation and robot-free human data-collection interfaces, a unified whole-body controller, and a common policy learning pipeline across human and robot demonstrations. A hierarchical learning stack further enables language-conditioned visuomotor control and long-horizon task execution. We evaluate the system across hardware, data collection, and policy learning, and demonstrate mobile-manipulation behaviors requiring dexterity, active perception, navigation, and whole-body coordination. We hope that Pixel provides an integrated and extensible foundation for studying how robot hardware, data, control, and learning can be designed together for robot learning. More results at https://pixel-robot.com/pixel


### arXiv 2611.03641 - From Natural Language to Exact Cover: A Neuro-Symbolic Approach to Zebra Puzzles

- categories: cs.AI
- published: 2026-11-02T18:58:21Z
- authors: Paulius Skaisgiris, Thomas Pammer, Veronika Semmelrock, Mykyta Ielanskyi, Maximilian Heisinger, Erich Kobler
- query-set: cs.AI
- abs: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.03641

Chain-of-Thought (CoT) generation has substantially improved the performance of Large Language Models (LLMs) on complex reasoning tasks, including code generation, data analysis, and exam-style question answering. Despite these advances, purely neural LLMs continue to struggle with elementary logical reasoning problems and lack the determinism, soundness, and reliability characteristic of symbolic reasoning systems. Conversely, classical symbolic methods such as SAT solving and exact cover guarantee correctness and completeness, but require problems to be expressed in highly specialized formal encodings, limiting their applicability to natural language inputs. In this work, we present a synergistic neuro-symbolic framework that bridges this gap by combining neural semantic parsing with deterministic exact cover reasoning. Given a Zebra-style logic puzzle in natural language, an LLM translates entities and constraints into structured tool calls that construct an exact cover instance. A symbolic solver then computes the solution and provides feedback to iteratively refine invalid, under- or overconstrained encodings, exploiting the uniqueness property of Zebra puzzles. We conduct a comprehensive empirical evaluation across multiple parameter scales, post-training paradigms, and LLM families. The results demonstrate that our hybrid approach consistently outperforms strong plain neural baselines, including CoT prompting, as well as recent neuro-symbolic methods.


### arXiv 2611.03634 - WebHarbor: Docking Real Websites for Evolving GUI Agent Environments

- categories: cs.AI, cs.CL, cs.LG
- published: 2026-11-02T18:57:36Z
- authors: Zhaoyang Wang, Qianhui Wu, Boyu Gou, Demi Wang, Jiacheng Zhang, Peichun Hua
- query-set: cs.AI
- abs: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.03634

GUI web agents require realistic and interactive environments that remain stable during both evaluation and training. Live website evaluation suffers from changing content, access restrictions, and limited coverage of authenticated workflows, while existing synthetic environments for training often lack functional depth and visual fidelity. We propose WebHarbor, a suite of 153 executable and dockerized website environments that faithfully reconstruct the visual interfaces, content, and functional workflows of real-world web services under deterministic control. We build WebHarbor through task-driven human-agent collaboration, where environments evolve as new tasks expand their functional scope, coding agents implement the required changes, and human contributors audit visual fidelity, functional correctness, and task validity. The resulting suite comprises 3,518 tasks spanning multi-step and post-authentication workflows, where existing works often fail to cover. Beyond reliable evaluation, WebHarbor provides high-fidelity synthetic web environments for cost-efficient reinforcement learning (RL). We demonstrate that a Qwen 4B-level GUI agent trained with RL in WebHarbor generalizes to unseen tasks and performs competitively with leading open-source GUI web agents across four benchmarks. The code and data are publicly available at https://github.com/aiming-lab/WebHarbor.


### arXiv 2611.03622 - D&A: Decoupling Description and Assessment Enables VLMs as Zero-Shot Robotic Reward Models

- categories: cs.RO, cs.AI, cs.CL, cs.LG, eess.SY
- published: 2026-11-02T18:52:11Z
- authors: Marius Memmel, Shawn Yin, Urvi Rutia, Angad Wadhwa, Eldon Li, Gavin Angeloff
- query-set: cs.AI
- abs: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.03622

Vision-Language Models (VLMs) hold promise for zero-shot video task-progress estimation in robot learning, yet existing frameworks frequently suffer from confirmation bias, hallucinating progress in failed executions due to implicit success assumptions or relative temporal ranking designs. Naive prompting of frontier models collapses into a binary success detector that fails to capture nuanced, non-linear progress and failures. While post-training approaches can mitigate this through explicit training, they demand expensive real-world robotics data and require computationally intensive post-training for every individual base model. To resolve these limitations, we introduce D&A (Describe & Assess): our approach decouples progress estimation into an objective, task-grounded textual description of scene dynamics followed by an assessment phase using explicit rubrics. By compelling the VLM to synthesize an explicit, text-based definition of absolute success before scoring, the framework anchors evaluations to an external, global criterion rather than the video's internal timeline. Empirical results demonstrate that our method calibrates zero-shot estimates and mitigates confirmation bias, providing fine-grained, partial progress metrics that accurately penalize failed executions while correctly interpolating successful runs. Being purely prompt-based, D&A generalizes across tasks, embodiments, and benchmarks---matching or surpassing post-trained reward models---and its reward estimates enable efficient downstream reinforcement learning.


### arXiv 2611.03618 - Strategist: Evolving Reasoning with Agents

- categories: cs.AI, cs.CL
- published: 2026-11-02T18:51:04Z
- authors: Taiming Lu, Qi Xu, Zhigang Hua, Zhuang Liu
- query-set: cs.AI
- abs: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.03618

A model is only half of an AI agent system. The other half is the inference strategy: the reasoning procedure that turns model calls into a response. Today that second half is written by hand, and the experience of building one strategy never carries to the next. We introduce Strategist, a meta-agent that evolves inference strategies across tasks. Each strategy is written as a blueprint,executable code that other strategies can reuse, and together the blueprints form a library. Given a task and a small development set, Strategist retrieves related blueprints, writes candidate strategies, tests them on real samples, and revises them from the results. Every candidate then enters the library, so later tasks build on earlier discoveries. On 30 benchmarks, Strategist beats the strongest baseline on 24 and matches its average accuracy (74.7% vs.74.0%) at 39% lower inference cost. Its strategies also transfer to unseen tasks and other models without further search. The part of an AI system that is not improved on its own now does.


### arXiv 2611.03617 - Tracing the Scientific Discovery Capacity of LLMs to Their Training Data

- categories: cs.CL, cs.AI
- published: 2026-11-02T18:50:54Z
- authors: Arda Uzunoglu, Mahler Revsine, Michael Schatz, Benjamin Van Durme, Mehrdad Farajtabar, Daniel Khashabi
- query-set: cs.AI
- abs: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.03617

Large language models (LLMs) show growing promise for scientific discovery, yet basic questions remain about what enables reliable discovery. How can we trace the scientific discovery capacity of LLMs to their training data? We study this question as one of missing probability mass and develop a theoretical account in which monofacts-the facts observed once during training-set the discovery capacity. In materials science and regulatory genomics, we directly manipulate monofact rates in training sets and find that more monofacts consistently yield more discoveries across two open-weight model families, sampling strategies, and reasoning budgets. Further experiments show the relationship cannot be explained by training-set diversity alone and can transfer across scientific domains when paired with in-domain data. Together, our results identify monofact rate in training data as a key factor governing discoveries in language models.


### arXiv 2611.03601 - DiabAgent: Toward Agentic Physiological Control

- categories: cs.AI, cs.CL
- published: 2026-11-02T18:41:02Z
- authors: Chengxi Li, Shaoxuan Li, Deyao Hong, Qingle Liu, Yi Huang, Puheng Bian
- query-set: cs.AI
- abs: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.03601

As pretraining scales, medical language models have advanced rapidly in clinical question answering. Their ability to achieve effective physiological control through sustained intervention still lacks systematic quantitative evaluation. Type 1 diabetes provides a concrete setting: impaired insulin secretion creates dependence on exogenous insulin, with dosing continually adjusted using information about meals, exercise, illness, and glucose measurements. We introduce DiabAgent, a research suite for agentic physiological control comprising the Agentic Physiological Control (APC) framework, the DiabetSim simulator, and the SweetBench benchmark. APC controls glucose through medical-device tools, using ongoing observations to infer individual states, accumulate personal experience, and choose treatment actions. We build DiabetSim to simulate different patients, events, and device conditions for scalable virtual testing and training. Using DiabetSim, we construct SweetBench by instantiating 32 scenario designs as 212 glucose-control tasks. With Claude Opus 5.5 and a fixed decision interval of one hour, APC achieves a COGI of 73.5, compared with 71.2 for the open-source closed-loop baseline OpenAPS. Ablations further show that individual adaptation and contextual information improve glucose control. Combining high-frequency rule-based control with lower-frequency agent decisions shows potential for balancing control performance and computational cost. Our work provides a framework, simulator, and evaluation foundation for agents to participate in human physiological control.


### arXiv 2611.03600 - Four Bridges: Measuring Deception Propensity in a Multi-Agent Game

- categories: cs.AI, cs.MA
- published: 2026-11-02T18:40:47Z
- authors: Raphael Casey, Michael Simeone, Alberto Tosato, Florian Ernst, Kemal El Moujahid, James Tamplin
- query-set: cs.AI
- abs: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.03600

AI agents are increasingly deployed in settings where they interact and coordinate with other agents, yet honesty is mostly measured in single-turn, single-agent benchmarks that pressure the model to deceive. We study deception that emerges in multi-agent interaction without any instruction to deceive. We introduce Kradle, a platform for evaluating AI models, and Four Bridges, an information asymmetry game in which four agents distribute across four rooms: one is the death room, the others hold food worth more if not shared. One agent, the informed agent, knows which room is the death room and can withhold or disclose that information. We evaluate eight frontier models as the informed agent. Deceptive behavior appears in every model and in 73% of all runs (6% to 100% per model), and does not decrease with capability tier within a model family. When the informed agent does disclose, the warning is often challenged, and agents that challenge it enter the death room twice as often as those that do not. Telling all agents that one of them knows the death room collapses deception in six of eight models; telling the informed agent that the room is permanently fatal moves only one. On an open-weight model, an honesty steering vector reduces deception to 0% and adversarial honesty training to 6%. The platform and game provide a testbed for measuring deceptive behavior and comparing interventions.


### arXiv 2611.03596 - Spacetime Emergence in the Memory of a Learning Agent

- categories: quant-ph, cs.AI, cs.LG, gr-qc
- published: 2026-11-02T18:39:52Z
- authors: Philip A. LeMaitre, T. Rick Perche
- query-set: cs.AI
- abs: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.03596

We show that the causal structure of spacetime can emerge within the memory of a learning agent performing a quantum information task. We train a model-based reinforcement-learning agent to maximize the quantum mutual information between localized quantum probes interacting with a quantum field in Minkowski spacetime, without directly providing it with information regarding the spacetime metric or causal structure. In learning to accomplish this task, the reinforcement-learning agent constructs an internal predictive model whose planner develops the characteristic null structure of spacetime, allowing us to reconstruct the causal structure of the Minkowski metric with high accuracy. Within this task, spacetime geometry is obtained from an agent's successful predictions and actions, providing a first step toward showing that spacetime concepts emerge from operational quantum experience.


### arXiv 2611.03592 - Evaluating Uncertainty Calibration in Probabilistic Time Series Foundation Models

- categories: cs.LG, cs.AI
- published: 2026-11-02T18:34:50Z
- authors: Thomas Decker, Volker Tresp
- query-set: cs.AI
- abs: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.03592

Time series foundation models (TSFMs) are increasingly used for probabilistic forecasting across diverse domains, yet the reliability of their uncertainty estimates remains poorly understood. In this work, we study uncertainty calibration in probabilistic TSFMs from a broader methodological and empirical perspective. We introduce a taxonomy of how TSFMs represent predictive uncertainty and develop a fine-grained evaluation framework for calibration in multi-horizon forecasting that distinguishes pooled, horizon-wise, and stronger conditional notions of calibration. Using this framework, we conduct a comprehensive empirical evaluation of popular TSFMs across diverse datasets and show that current models exhibit distinct and systematic patterns of miscalibration that standard metrics often obscure. We further analyze common post-hoc recalibration methods and characterize which forms of calibration they can improve and which tend to persist. Our results provide practical guidance for evaluating and improving uncertainty calibration in TSFMs and highlight important limitations of current practice.


### arXiv 2611.03586 - FedReady: Bounded Agents for Federation Formation

- categories: cs.AI, cs.MA
- published: 2026-11-02T18:32:43Z
- authors: Ziyue Xu, Holger R. Roth, Chester Chen, Peter Cnudde
- query-set: cs.AI
- abs: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.03586

Federated learning (FL) runtimes standardize aggregation and deployment, but each new study still requires federation formation: selecting task-eligible sites and preparing their local data and task code for federated training or analysis. This preparation recurs when the target changes, while raw records and site-private mappings are excluded from cross-site FL messages. We present FedReady, a system that performs federation formation with bounded LLM agents. Agents propose shared contracts, local adapters, and task code; the runtime owns workflow state and accepts artifacts only after checks tied to their digests. Candidate site discovery prioritizes recall; local eligibility verification checks task support before site admission. Once participating sites and artifacts are fixed, conventional federated training or analysis proceeds without readiness agents. Across 39 simulated retinal sites and four tasks, discovery reaches recall 1.00 at precision 0.65; local eligibility verification removes all 17 false positives without losing an eligible site-task pair. The participating sites supply 45,575 task-record instances and complete four 100-round jobs, including one with a runtime-generated contract. Across ten patient cohorts, preparation supports seven patient populations and holds endpoints at three sources as unsupported. Comparison with manually prepared reference populations and analysis (the manual reference) gives a maximum survival-curve difference of 0.001133. We also analyze 19 development failures, targeted classification ablations, and standalone and heterogeneous agent configurations. These results establish the feasibility of task-specific data preparation before ordinary FL training or analysis.


### arXiv 2611.03577 - Sonar-MoE: In-Backward Expert Updates for Memory-Efficient Mixture-of-Experts Training

- categories: cs.LG, cs.AI, cs.CL, cs.PF
- published: 2026-11-02T18:30:00Z
- authors: Avni Mittal, Avinash Anand, Ashutosh Kumar, Dikshant Kukreja, Kritarth Prasad, Chaitanya Lakhchaura
- query-set: cs.AI
- abs: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.03577

Most parameters of a Mixture-of-Experts (MoE) model belong to its experts, and standard training keeps every expert gradient in GPU memory until the optimizer step. This memory grows with the number and size of the experts, so it rises as MoE models scale. We introduce Sonar-MoE, which updates each expert tensor during the backward pass and frees its gradient right away. It brings optimizer-in-backward from dense models to batched MoE expert tensors, so the GPU never holds more than one layer of expert gradients. When we fine-tune OLMoE-1B-7B and Granite-3.1-3B-A800M in bf16 with two expert-kernel backends, Sonar-MoE lowers peak memory by 19% to 22%. Its updates are bitwise identical to standard training without expert gradient clipping, and held-out loss stays within 0.003 of all baselines. Each step also takes 17% to 23% less time than standard training with global-norm clipping. Updating whole batched tensors rather than individual experts is what keeps Sonar-MoE fast, as it avoids a 20% to 72% slowdown while using the same memory. Sonar-MoE matches optimizer-in-backward over all parameters in memory and speed while still clipping the dense parameters.


### arXiv 2611.03557 - PlantVLM-Edge: Quantized Vision-Language Models for On-Device Crop Health Diagnosis

- categories: cs.CV, cs.AI
- published: 2026-11-02T18:19:19Z
- authors: Gaurab Wagle, Bishal Adhikari, Jiajia Li, Xiaolei Huang, Dong Chen
- query-set: cs.AI
- abs: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.03557

Timely and accurate crop health diagnosis is essential for sustainable agricultural production, yet accurate diagnosis often requires expert knowledge that is not readily available. Recent vision-language models (VLMs) have demonstrated remarkable capabilities in visual understanding, reasoning, and natural-language interaction, creating new opportunities for intelligent crop health assessment. However, deploying VLMs on resource-constrained devices remains challenging due to their substantial computational and memory requirements. This paper presents PlantVLM-Edge, a quantized vision-language framework for conversational crop health diagnosis that can run entirely on resource-constrained edge devices. PlantVLM-Edge combines agricultural domain adaptation, post-training quantization, and on-device serving with an offline chat interface, allowing growers to ask open-ended questions and receive diagnoses, symptom explanations, and management guidance without cloud connectivity. The framework is backbone-agnostic: we validate it with two different VLM types with varying scale and architecture (Qwen3-VL-2B and LLaVA-1.5-7B), four quantization schemes, and two diagnostic domains (plant diseases and insect pests), and we deploy all variants on an NVIDIA Jetson AGX Orin. Eight-bit quantization affects answer quality minimally relative to the full-precision model, and 4-bit activation-aware weight quantization (AWQ) retains 98.6% of full-precision answer quality on both domains while reducing the weight memory of the 2B backbone by 49%. In its most compact form, PlantVLM-Edge requires 2.01 GiB of weight memory, returns its first token in about 0.12 s, and completes a 64-token diagnostic response in 0.78 s. These results demonstrate that timely and accurate crop health diagnosis is practical on edge devices, providing a foundation for next-generation crop monitoring and decision support systems.


### arXiv 2611.03555 - MCP Tool Poisoning in Agentic AI Frameworks: A Cross-Framework Measurement Study

- categories: cs.CR, cs.AI
- published: 2026-11-02T18:17:08Z
- authors: Farooq Shaikh, Biagio Montaruli
- query-set: cs.AI
- abs: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.03555

The Model Context Protocol (MCP) lets agentic AI frameworks connect language models to external tool providers, but it carries attacker-controlled content (tool descriptions, schema fields, and response data) into model context without validation. Prior benchmarks measure model compliance with poisoned instructions without confirming real execution. We present the first cross-framework measurement of MCP tool poisoning with real tool execution. A 25-class root-cause taxonomy unifies prior classifications, and the open-source tool mcpvenom drives a 9,720-cell susceptibility matrix across 12 frameworks, 18 models, and 45 attack scenarios, with a dual oracle that confirms exfiltration via canary HTTP receipt. The overall attack success rate is 22.9% (2,113 vulnerable cells). Of these, 64.9% dispatch an attacker tool call, 23.0% contact the attacker endpoint, and 12.1% verifiably exfiltrate a secret. Resistance is model-level: nine core frameworks cluster within 23 to 26% (chi-squared = 3.30, p = 0.91), and no framework implements intentional defense. Incidental framework properties mitigate in specific cases, and a regex description scanner rejects 20 of 45 scenarios at a 7.2% false-positive rate, so metadata scanning alone is a partial control. Model ASR ranges from 0.4% to 49.7%. Claude Opus 4.8 is vulnerable in 8 of 530 cells while issuing 223 explicit refusals. A code-level audit finds no intentional defense across twelve frameworks, and all five coordinated disclosures across four vendors were rejected.


### arXiv 2611.03551 - Can LLM Agents Be Forward-Deployed Engineers? Requirement Discovery and Delivery for Enterprise Text-to-SQL

- categories: cs.CL, cs.AI
- published: 2026-11-02T18:13:38Z
- authors: Marianne Menglin Liu, Rongguang Wang, Zheng Wang, Yewen Wang, Leonid Boytsov, Tao Sheng
- query-set: cs.AI
- abs: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.03551

LLM coding agents can increasingly automate model post-training, but whether they can act as forward-deployed engineer (FDE) agents, discovering customer requirements and turning them into deployment decisions, remains unclear. We study this on a real enterprise text-to-SQL engagement: a simulated customer, grounded in the real use case's requirements, starts with an ambiguous request. An agent must discover acceptance criteria, data permissions, and hardware constraints in the discovery phase, then plan and execute a post-training recipe in the delivery phase. Across 15 models, the strongest discover over 80% of withheld requirements and open-weight models discover 40-50%; in the delivery phase, only 25% of runs across eight coding-agent configurations pass all acceptance criteria. We identify four bottlenecks in this engagement: (1) incomplete discovery, with 40% of withheld requirements missing or misrepresented; (2) unreliable execution; (3) underused compute budgets and a lack of systematic comparisons across base models and post-training methods; and (4) benchmark accuracy does not guarantee production readiness. We suggest human review of discovered requirements, monitoring of the agent's plan during execution, and an explicit experiment-environment specification covering hardware, budget scope, and production checks.


### arXiv 2611.03534 - An Agentic Framework for Automated Power System Studies and Grid Connection Assessment

- categories: cs.AI
- published: 2026-11-02T18:00:52Z
- authors: MaoXun Huang, Kshitij Kapoor, Eli Louise
- query-set: cs.AI
- abs: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.03534

Connecting new generation and demand to the grid depends on two scarce skills: checking applications against a large, changing body of regulation, and running the network studies that decide where and how a project connects. We present Grid Agents, an AI system that brings both together, and the methodology we use to evaluate it. The Grid Ontology is its knowledge system. It represents the regulation, standards and engineering knowledge that grid work depends on, makes it searchable, and evolves as the agents learn from use. Every answer drawn from it rests on evidence the agents cite. Grid Studio is an agent system specialised for power-system engineering. It works in its own engineering environment with domain tools and a study framework, draws on the Grid Ontology, and relies on established solvers for every number it reports. In both, engineers make the decisions. We bring eight published power-system benchmark families, previously scattered across separate papers, into one suite of 519 tasks, and we verify not only each answer but the work behind it, by replaying the agent's tool calls. The Grid Ontology cites the right regulatory evidence more often than a general coding agent or retrieval pipelines, leads on LegalBench-RAG and ranks first on the public leaderboards of the Enterprise RAG Challenge and ViDoRe V3. Grid Studio scores highest on the 519 power-system tasks and is the fastest of all the systems we compare, both by a statistically significant margin. With an optimiser in the loop, its reinforcement packages cost 33-67% less on average than those the agent designs alone.


### arXiv 2611.03528 - Contrastive Adapter Training and Consensus Knowledge Distillation for Multi-Source-Free Domain Adaptation in Skin Cancer Diagnosis

- categories: cs.CV, cs.AI
- published: 2026-11-02T17:58:16Z
- authors: Ali Abedi, Q. M. Jonathan Wu, Ning Zhang, Farhad Pourpanah
- query-set: cs.AI
- abs: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.03528

Skin cancer diagnosis, particularly differentiating melanoma from benign nevi, is challenging due to the visual similarity between lesions. Although convolutional neural networks (CNNs) and vision transformers (ViTs) have shown promising performance, their effectiveness often deteriorates on data from heterogeneous clinical sources. Conventional domain adaptation methods address domain shift but require access to source data during adaptation, which is often infeasible due to privacy regulations. Multi-source-free unsupervised domain adaptation (MSFDA) addresses this limitation by leveraging multiple labeled source domains to generalize to an unlabeled target domain without requiring access to source data. However, existing MSFDA methods rely on full backbone fine-tuning, leading to catastrophic forgetting and overfitting on small clinical datasets, and address domain shift at the aggregation stage without establishing a shared domain-invariant feature space. Furthermore, their reliance on hard pseudo-labels or confidence-weighted aggregation introduces noisy supervision signals under domain shift. To address these limitations, we propose CAT-CKD, consisting of two components: (1) contrastive adapter training (CAT), which trains lightweight ConvPass adapters within a frozen ViT backbone using supervised contrastive learning (SCL) to establish a shared domain-invariant feature space before source-specific model training, and (2) consensus knowledge distillation (CKD), which aggregates logits from multiple source models into a consensus supervisory signal and adapts a student model on unlabeled target data using KL divergence. Experiments on five publicly available skin lesion datasets show that CAT-CKD achieves an average AUROC of 86.1%, outperforming existing MSFDA methods while requiring only 4.3M trainable parameters. The code is available at https://github.com/A-Abedi/CAT_CKD.


### arXiv 2611.03526 - Library of Layers (LoL): Adaptively Ordering Transformer Layers at Runtime

- categories: cs.LG, cs.AI, cs.CL
- published: 2026-11-02T17:57:53Z
- authors: Abdellah Rahmani, Sean McLeish, Ang Li, Sanae Lotfi, Pascal Frossard, Micah Goldblum
- query-set: cs.AI
- abs: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.03526

Computer programmers chain together the same reusable computational primitives, for example, conditional statements or loops, but in different orders to implement different algorithms. In contrast, existing language model architectures always execute their layers in the same order. We introduce Library of Layers (LoL), an architecture that dynamically orders its layers during inference to solve the problem at hand. LoL has a single hyperparameter, the size of its global library of transformer blocks, that allows it to transition seamlessly between parameter reuse and sparsity, generalizing the parameter-efficiency and FLOPs-efficiency tradeoffs of dense, depth-recurrent, and mixture of experts architectures in one unified family. We show that LoL achieves competitive performance with all three above baselines when pretrained on text data. On synthetic sudoku and maze data, we observe that the model employs distinct layer orderings to implement different solution strategies, and we can intervene on routing to force a model to apply a specific solution strategy to a problem instance. In a multilingual natural language setting, we find that LoL chooses different layer order patterns based on language and script. Similarly, we train LoL on safety data and fit probes to its router outputs, finding that these probes distinguish safe from unsafe text with high AUROC. LoL provides a path toward a future in which models dynamically compose their own computational graph, enabling more efficient and controllable reasoning.


### arXiv 2611.03525 - Grading the Unannotated: Insights from the WorkRB Skill Extraction Challenge at RecSys-HR 2026

- categories: cs.AI
- published: 2026-11-02T17:57:53Z
- authors: Warre Veys, Luis Gasco, Jens-Joris Decorte
- query-set: cs.AI
- abs: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.03525

Skill extraction benchmarks score models against a few human-annotated positives and treat every other skill in the vocabulary as equally wrong. At the scale of ESCO (13,939 skills) this binary view can reward models that rank the annotated positive high for the wrong reason, and hides semantically nonsensical predictions. The WorkRB Challenge at RecSys-HR 2026 replaces it with graded relevance: every candidate skill in a model's ranking window carries a 0 to 4 grade, and the leaderboard scores nDCG@100 against these grades. Since ternary judgements proved too coarse for objective annotation, relevance is decomposed into a cascade of four binary questions (same domain, activity mentioned, right granularity, replacement of a gold positive) that a smaller LLM judge can answer reliably; the labels are validated with blind human and frontier-model re-annotation. This paper documents the annotation methodology, the construction of five benchmark datasets (four with vacancy sentences as queries, one with lexically distant skill surface forms), the validation of the LLM cascade, and insights from the submissions. The field converged on multi-stage retrieve-and-rerank pipelines, whose gains come from clearing nonsense out of the ranking window rather than finding more positives: at the top of the leaderboard, the graded ordering is uncorrelated with what binary evaluation would have said. Graded labels expose much bigger gaps between top submissions and baselines than binary evaluation does, and show that semantic-understanding skill recommendation is still wide open. The clear next direction is bringing efficiency back: every submitted system we could size uses at least 42x, typically several hundred times, more parameters than the baselines it beats.


### arXiv 2611.03520 - PanoTac: A Dense Three-Axis Tactile Sensor for Robust Touch-Centric Manipulation

- categories: cs.RO, cs.AI, cs.LG
- published: 2026-11-02T17:55:07Z
- authors:  Binglin,  Wang,  Xiaole,  Jiang, Zicheng Teng, Xinqi Liu
- query-set: cs.AI
- abs: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.03520

Contact-rich manipulation benefits from tactile feedback that captures both detailed contact geometry and interaction forces. When scooping grains, contact patterns help infer spoon orientation, while resistance transmitted through the grasp guides motion. To provide these complementary sensing capabilities, we present PanoTac, a low-cost, compliant tactile fingertip designed for dense three-axis tactile sensing. Each fingertip provides a $12 \times 32 \times 3$ tactile representation, with one normal ($z$) and two shear ($x$, $y$) response components at each taxel. The sensor operates at 60 Hz and costs less than $50 per fingertip. Sensor characterization quantifies response repeatability and consistency across sensor instances and contact locations. We integrate PanoTac into a robotic system with a visuo-tactile pipeline. Across test-tube preparation, grain scooping, and salt pouring, the resulting policies outperform vision-only baselines by an average of 46.7 percentage points. Ablations show that removing either spatial contact information or shear feedback reduces overall performance across the task suite. Our project page is available at https://panotac.github.io.


### arXiv 2611.03518 - VIAS: Value-Isolating Activation Steering for Multi-Value Alignment and Compliance for Organisations

- categories: cs.CL, cs.AI, cs.LG, cs.MA
- published: 2026-11-02T17:53:26Z
- authors: Avni Mittal, Avinash Anand, Dikshant Kukreja, Erik Cambria, Rajiv Ratn Shah, Sandipan Dandapat
- query-set: cs.AI
- abs: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.03518

Organisations increasingly deploy large language model agents that must follow organisational values. We check whether activation steering, which adds vectors to a model's internal activations during generation without changing its weights, can improve compliance beyond prompting alone. Since most scenarios involve several values at once, we focus on multi-value steering. A simple way to build these vectors is to contrast responses that follow all values relevant to a scenario with responses that violate them. However, this gives nearly identical vectors for different values: they capture a general compliance signal rather than individual values, which limits their use when combining several values. We propose Value-Isolating Activation Steering (VIAS), which contrasts responses to the same scenario under two prompts, one listing all relevant values and one omitting a single target value. We keep a pair only if the target value changes from compliant to non-compliant and both responses still meet a minimum compliance level on the other values. We evaluate VIAS on PROTECT, a workplace compliance dataset where each scenario is tagged with relevant values from a 21-value taxonomy. VIAS reduces overlap between value vectors, making them better suited for multi-value steering, although this alone does not lead to independent control over each value. Combined with prompting, VIAS complies with every tagged value on 69.6% of scenarios for Qwen2.5-7B, 75.2% for Mistral-7B, and 72.6% for Phi-3.5-mini, outperforming prompting alone for all three models.


### arXiv 2611.03503 - A Cross-Domain Matrix Engine for Signal Processing and Deep Neural Net Inference

- categories: cs.AR, cs.AI
- published: 2026-11-02T17:40:14Z
- authors: Mehdi Safarpour
- query-set: cs.AI
- abs: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.03503

Matrix multiplication dominates both PHY signal processing and Deep Neural Network (DNN) inference, yet these domains place opposing demands on the matrix engine. Signal processing requires low-latency execution of relatively small matrix operations, whereas DNN inference, particularly at large batch sizes, prioritizes high throughput and energy efficiency for large matrix multiplications. While offering efficient memory access profile, a Systolic Array (SA) adds fill and drain delays per matrix. Those can be amortized for large matrices but not for small matrices. Conversely, a tensor unit completes the kernel in few cycles but would require repeated reads of same operand tiles over large matrices. This makes SA too slow for signal processing and tensor too inefficient for AI. We show that this need not be a choice between systolic and tensor structuring of Multiply-Accumulate (MAC) units. We propose a single engine that operates primarily as a SA and, through low-cost reconfiguration, transforms into a tensor-style engine on a per-operation basis. MUXes create both the spatial reduction adder tree and temporal reduction accumulators from the existing MACs and registers within the systolic array. The proposed reconfigurable SA simulated using modified SCALESim simulator, verified on an Zynq 702 FPGA and synthesized using ASAP 7nm FinFET library. SCALE-Sim simulations shows up to 7.5x lower latency for small complex value PHY kernels. Even on DNN workloads, it achieves up to 1.65x speedups over vanilla SA execution with trivial increase of memory accesses, retaining the systolic array's memory access advantage. Synthesis results estimate reconfigurable fabric costs only 8% power and 13.6% area overheads.


### arXiv 2611.03500 - Can AI Do Formal Demography? Why Vaccinating the Oldest Saves Both the Most Lives and the Most Years of Life

- categories: cs.AI, q-bio.PE
- published: 2026-11-02T17:39:50Z
- authors: Joshua R. Goldstein, Kenneth W. Wachter
- query-set: cs.AI
- abs: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.03500

Can AI contribute to formal demography? We report an experiment in which an AI model, Claude, was asked to explain and then simplify the proof of an earlier result: that vaccinating the oldest against COVID-19 saves both the most lives and the most years of life, because the years saved per person treated rise with age. The model proposed a new proof that is shorter, more intuitive, and somewhat more general than the original, built from a known life-table identity and the definition of convexity. It states the condition for the result as the convexity of the reciprocal of the hazard, which can be checked from the hazard alone and holds for most familiar parametric models, and it yields a lower bound on life expectancy whenever that condition is met. Prompted by a further question, the model also gave an exact form of the condition, showing how far convexity can be relaxed. The model traced these identities and related results in earlier literature. An edited record of the sessions is provided, and complete transcripts are available on request. The exercise suggests that AI can revisit established results, make them easier to understand, and prove valuable generalizations.


### arXiv 2611.03496 - Online Label-Free Gradient Matching for Continual Language Model Training

- categories: cs.AI, cs.CL
- published: 2026-11-02T17:37:36Z
- authors: Sajjad Pakdamansavoji, Mohammad Reza Rezaei, Ilija Subasic, Pedro Garzon, Radu Paradovschi, Zhao Chen
- query-set: cs.AI
- abs: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.03496

Language models in customer-facing products must be continually updated, since the data they serve shifts over time. As retraining on the full accumulated data is impractical, each update should instead run on a small set of examples carrying the training signal of all data seen so far. The strongest current methods build this set by gradient matching, which synthesizes examples whose training gradients match those of the full data. However, they require ground-truth labels, run once on a fixed snapshot rather than on a growing stream, and are slow on large data. We introduce \textbf{OLGA}, which lifts these limitations. It distills unlabeled data by drawing the gradient matching targets from the model's own next-token predictions. Across five text classification benchmarks with phi-1.5, it improves accuracy by 10 percentage points over the strongest selection baseline using as few as five examples. OLGA also distills online data streams by keeping a running rehearsal memory of synthetic examples, which is re-distilled together with each new batch as it arrives. In the online setting, it outperforms the strongest selection-based continual baseline by 6.6 stream-AUC percentage points. Finally, our method scales to large data by pairing distillation with selection in a hybrid pipeline, letting selection handle the mild stage of compression and distillation the extreme one. It cuts runtime by 30\% versus distillation alone at similar accuracy, and beats the best selection-only pipeline by up to 12.6 percentage points.


### arXiv 2611.03494 - Harder Than the Task: From Technical to Organizational Difficulty in AI Benchmarking

- categories: cs.CY, cs.AI
- published: 2026-11-02T17:36:52Z
- authors: Victor Barres, Aden Barton, Nicolai J. Foss, Henrik M. Nielsen
- query-set: cs.AI
- abs: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.03494

AI benchmarks have made remarkable progress in measuring the ability of AI systems to perform increasingly demanding cognitive and technical tasks. Yet, many of these benchmarks treat technical tasks as the unit of analysis, while much economically valuable work takes place within organizations, where tasks are embedded in a system of relations. Within organizations, successful task performance depends not only on solving the technical demands of the task but also on navigating the interdependence of activities, distributed knowledge, differentiated authority, temporal constraints, conflicting interests, and changing environmental conditions while the task is underway. Accordingly, we argue that organizational task difficulty constitutes a distinct and insufficiently recognized dimension of AI capability, and that existing approaches to benchmarking provide an incomplete basis for assessing how effectively AI agents can perform real-world organizational work. By drawing on organization theory, we develop a taxonomy of the structure of work that characterizes the conditions that agents face in organizational work. Applying this taxonomy to analyze existing benchmarks, we find limited and uneven coverage of organizational dimensions, with fewer than one quarter of prominent benchmarks claiming any of them. We therefore argue that AI benchmarks should increasingly move beyond the evaluation of technically difficult tasks toward the systematic assessment of agents' ability to navigate the organizational conditions under which economically valuable work takes place.


### arXiv 2611.03439 - Playing Dumb: How a Chess Transformer Simulates Users Across Skill Levels

- categories: cs.AI, cs.LG
- published: 2026-11-02T16:54:20Z
- authors: Difan Jiao, George Eilender, Zhenwei Tang, Ashton Anderson
- query-set: cs.AI
- abs: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.03439

Generative models increasingly simulate users at different skill levels in education and AI evaluation. Yet it remains unclear whether lower-skill behavior reflects less internal knowledge or a different use of retained knowledge. This distinction is difficult to study in natural language, where proficiency and relevant knowledge are hard to measure. In this work, we study Maia-2, a chess transformer that predicts human moves conditioned on player skill, using quantitative skill ratings and deterministically labeled chess concepts. We hold board positions and model weights fixed and analyze positions where skill conditioning changes the prediction from a blunder to the best move. First, we measure encoded knowledge by training probes to predict the presence of 172 chess concepts from Maia-2's internal states. Probe accuracy remains nearly constant across skill conditions, and probes trained at one level transfer accurately to the others. To test whether stronger decisions can be recovered from this retained knowledge, we fine-tune only Maia-2's final output layer, raising best-move accuracy in the lowest skill band from 14.4% to 82.8%. Training this layer only on highest-skill representations retains most of the improvement at lower levels, showing that recovery does not require direct training on lower-skill states. Steering concept-relevant features learned by a sparse autoencoder also improves lower-skill decisions while leaving probe-measured concept encoding practically unchanged. These results support an account in which skill conditioning changes how retained concept knowledge influences move selection, showing that weaker behavior can understate the knowledge available within a model.


### arXiv 2611.03434 - Optimize the Harness, Not the Model: Fitting a Minimal Agent to a Self-Hostable LLM for Investigative Analysis

- categories: cs.AI, cs.CL, cs.MA
- published: 2026-11-02T16:53:29Z
- authors: Shai Nahum Gefen, Vered Chassida, Dmitry Basin, Guy Feigenblat
- query-set: cs.AI
- abs: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.03434

Government intelligence teams need LLM agents that can analyze large, messy evidence collections, but security and cost constraints often rule out hosted frontier models and broad, hard-to-audit tool surfaces. We report CLEO: Constrained Loop for Environment Optimization, a method for fitting a minimal agent harness to a fixed, self-hostable LLM. Given an expert-validated dataset, CLEO admits only the tools, guardrails, memory policies, and loop components that improve accuracy while remaining general and auditable. On our held-out dataset, the CLEO harness reaches an 83.8% pass rate, within 1.2 points of the strongest out-of-the-box Claude Code SDK baseline, while being two orders of magnitude smaller.


### arXiv 2611.03412 - DeliverBench: Benchmarking AI Agents on Delivering Realistic Multi-Stage Professional Projects

- categories: cs.AI
- published: 2026-11-02T16:37:40Z
- authors: Yumou Liu, Mingju Chen, Zhoufutu Wen, Qianyu Yang, Zijie Wang, Yeqian Liu
- query-set: cs.AI
- abs: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.03412

LLM agents are judged by what they deliver, and in professional work what gets delivered is a project, not a task. A project pays off only if the whole package holds, and one weak part can limit the value of the rest. Yet existing benchmarks mostly test agents on one task at a time, leaving a gap between the capability we measure and the outcome that makes agents useful. To address this gap, we introduce DeliverBench, which tests whether agents can carry a project from brief to final delivery. Its 11 projects, spanning financial due diligence, legal contract drafting, and drug safety reporting, are reconstructed with 13 domain experts from real materials and chain 150 subtasks through 2-15 rounds of new materials and client feedback. Because no reference outputs are injected, agents inherit their own errors from one task to the next. Expert rubrics then score every checkpoint as well as the final package. Across seven model-harness systems, even the best system scores only 65.0 out of 100 on final delivery and passes 50.0% of subtasks at a 90-point threshold. The failures are often failures of carrying work forward: in one audit project, all seven systems addressed the review findings, but only one preserved traceability. Together, these results suggest that task-level capability does not simply compose into delivery, which also depends on keeping the work coherent as it evolves.


### arXiv 2611.03406 - From Reactive to Agentic Resilience: Multi-Timescale AI-RAN Orchestration Under Signaling Storms

- categories: cs.NI, cs.AI
- published: 2026-11-02T16:34:13Z
- authors: Sotiris Chatzimiltis, Mahdi Boloursaz Mashhadi, Mohammad Shojafar, Rahim Tafazolli
- query-set: cs.AI
- abs: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.03406

Next-generation open radio access network (Open RAN) and AI-RAN deployments turn the RAN into a disaggregated, compute-aware platform whose service continuity depends on many control loops acting at different timescales. Resilience in this setting is best seen as a lifecycle spanning anticipation, absorption, adaptation, recovery, and evolution. Yet, existing mechanisms remain stage-specific and are rarely coordinated across the control hierarchy. This paper argues that agentic AI offers an orchestration layer that coordinates this lifecycle, turning operator intent into coordinated action while keeping slow language-model reasoning off the fast control path. Using mass-attachment signaling storms as a running study, we build a discrete-event Open RAN attach simulator that captures retry amplification, shared-compute contention, and non-instantaneous provisioning, and we propose a three-tier framework in which an orchestrator at the service management and orchestration layer sets a standing policy, a per-site non-real-time agent judges storms and anticipates scheduled crowds, and a deterministic near-real-time loop enforces every decision. Across experiments, the agentic controller approaches the resilience of a fully provisioned pool of servers at a quarter of its capacity by reading a scheduled event from a calendar and provisioning ahead of it. No reactive weight setting recovers this once the surge arrives. Free-text operator intents ground into control directives at 94% exact match on previously unseen intents, and a single instruction reshapes live behavior end to end. We also quantify the latency, cost, and contention limits that bound it. The supplementary material provides analytical proof of the derived Theorem on anticipation horizon, as well as the subsequent Corollary results on reactive provisioning limits.


### arXiv 2611.03389 - CrowdForge: Effects of Audio and Visual Audience Participation Modalities on Agency, Collaboration, and Engagement in AI-Mediated MR Performances

- categories: cs.HC, cs.AI, cs.NI, cs.PF, cs.SI
- published: 2026-11-02T16:28:32Z
- authors: Torin Hopkins, Shih-Yu Ma, Zander Trafton-Hancock, Ellen Yi-Luen Do, Luca Turchet
- query-set: cs.AI
- abs: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.03389

Technology-mediated audience participation (TMAP) enables audiences to influence live performances, yet balancing interaction with meaningful agency remains challenging. While AI-generated music and MR enable new forms of audio and visual audience participation, little is known about how these participation modalities influence audience experiences during live musical performance. In particular, it remains unclear how they shape perceived agency, collaboration, and engagement, and how they complement one another within TMAP systems. We present CrowdForge, an AI- and MR-mediated paradigm where audiences collectively control generative music and virtual environments during a live performance featuring a guitarist and DJ. Through a mixedmethods concert study evaluating three interaction conditions (audio, visual, and combined audio-visual), we demonstrate that observability is the primary driver of audience agency. While visual interaction provided easily observable feedback, audio parameters offered macro-structural control over the music. Audiences tended to use the control enabled by AI audio generation to test musical boundaries, introducing a productive adversarial tension between the crowd and the musicians. This dynamic pushed creative limits, deepened engagement, and generated moments of musical coherence when performers successfully resolved audience-induced challenges. We argue future TMAP systems should prioritize human-centered, highly observable interactions to build agency, while combining the expressiveness of music with the clarity of visual feedback.


### arXiv 2611.03388 - LAND: Learning an Adaptive Number of Draws for Bayesian Best-of-N Selection

- categories: cs.AI
- published: 2026-11-02T16:26:35Z
- authors: Koen Oostermeijer, Teryn Jones
- query-set: cs.AI
- abs: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.03388

Best-of-N decoding improves generation quality by sampling multiple candidates and selecting the best, but it allocates equal compute to sources regardless of how much they benefit from additional sampling: the expected benefit is small for sources with low-variance score distributions and potentially much larger for those with high-variance distributions. To address this shortcoming, we propose LAND (Learning an Adaptive Number of Draws), which dynamically allocates samples according to their posterior-predictive marginal value. LAND learns a discrete empirical mixture of source-conditioned score distributions from calibration data, updates its belief about a source as completion scores are observed, and stops sampling when the expected improvement of another draw falls below a shared compute price. Across machine-translation experiments with multiple models and language pairs, LAND achieves better MetricX scores than fixed best-of-N at matched average compute. Conversely, at matched translation quality, LAND requires approximately 20-40% fewer candidate generations. Adaptive allocation therefore provides a simple way to retain the benefits of best-of-N while substantially improving its compute efficiency.


### arXiv 2611.03384 - Can We Stop the Backtest Yet? Anytime-Valid Promotion Tests for Adaptive Financial Research Agents

- categories: cs.AI, q-fin.ST
- published: 2026-11-02T16:24:43Z
- authors: Eric Benhamou, Jean-Jacques Ohana, Beatrice Guez, Ethan Setrouk, Chamyl Saadi, Thomas Jacquot
- query-set: cs.AI
- abs: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.03384

Financial research agents can generate and monitor trading rules faster than a human can document the resulting selection process. Candidates appear at data-dependent times, weak rules are abandoned, and promising ones are watched until they cross a reporting threshold. We introduce PROMOTE, a protocol that assigns every candidate predictable testing wealth at birth and accumulates a cost-aware e-process only from subsequent returns. If lifetime allocations sum to at most one, a weighted Ville argument bounds the probability of ever promoting a rule whose declared bounded net score has always had nonpositive conditional mean. The result permits dependent candidates, adaptive births, and indefinite monitoring. We audit the implementation in two settings. A retrospective pseudo-out-of-sample replay contains 24 panel--rule hypotheses, 3,000 shared-sign null paths, injected alternatives, and public ETF and industry data. Repeated unadjusted tests produce a family crossing in 94.5% of null paths, versus 0.0% for PROMOTE; at a 15-bp aligned ETF-component perturbation, PROMOTE has 70.1% target power and median crossing session 2,327. A separate exact mean-zero experiment lets an agent select candidate identities and birth times from past scores; the funded gate has 1.77% false-family crossings across 3,000 programs. The market replay produces zero promotions after costs. The certificate controls historical false promotion under the declared null; it is not a certificate that an earlier edge remains profitable at deployment. AI-use disclosure. Generative AI tools, including OpenAI Codex, assisted manuscript preparation. For this preprint version, Codex assisted with LaTeX editing, author and venue metadata, compilation, and layout checks.


### arXiv 2611.03377 - Same Outputs, Different Scores: Auditing the Unchecked Graders of Multimodal LLM Benchmarks

- categories: cs.AI, cs.CL, cs.CV
- published: 2026-11-02T16:20:07Z
- authors: Ahmad Jarjis Hasan, Jonathan Lwowski
- query-set: cs.AI
- abs: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.03377

Every multimodal-LLM multiple-choice benchmark ends with a scoring rule that converts a model's response, often free-form text rather than a clean option letter, into a right/wrong verdict, buried in its code. Unlike the dataset, this rule is chosen without justification and left unexamined, yet it sets the published number. We reproduce the official scorers of eight peer-reviewed benchmarks on five open vision-language models (VLMs) that fit a single 16 GB GPU, and introduce a blinded human-plus-LLM-judge adjudication (Cohen's kappa=0.956) that recovers what the model actually answered. The scoring rule alone moves accuracy by up to 22 points on identical outputs, yet exactly 0 wherever a model emits clean option letters, so it hides on strong-model leaderboards. It is the fallback and not the rambling that does the damage: the benchmark with the highest non-compliance we measure has an artifact of exactly zero, because its scorer abstains instead of guessing; a reasoning model with non-compliance 0.000 still swings 9.5 points with which regex match of its trace the parser keeps. No standard rule is reliable: snapping a non-answer to its closest option credits answers never given yet buries correct ones, abstention discards answers stated in words, and likelihood scoring follows answer-token priors, so a single grade conflates capability with luck rather than measuring either. We release all code, labels, and the protocol, and argue benchmarks should report a non-compliance rate and at least two rules, not one unexamined grade.


### arXiv 2611.03376 - Mixed-Methods Exploration of Sociotechnical Factors to Improve Adoption of Crime-Reporting Apps in Urban Communities

- categories: cs.AI, cs.CY
- published: 2026-11-02T16:18:54Z
- authors: Olalekan David-Banjo, Chinazunwa Uwaoma, Paul Witman
- query-set: cs.AI
- abs: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.03376

Crime-reporting mobile applications can enhance community safety and engagement in urban areas, yet their adoption remains low, partly because developers prioritize technological features over residents' social realities. This study used an exploratory sequential mixed-methods design to identify and validate the sociotechnical factors that drive adoption. Two focus groups and an analysis of 660 app reviews revealed that users' initial sense of usefulness fades, whereas sustained engagement depends on perceived value (PV), comprising six dimensions: safety, anonymity, recency, autonomy, camaraderie, and accessibility. A survey of 505 urban residents, analyzed with PLS-SEM, validated PV as a formative higher-order construct formed by four of these dimensions (safety, anonymity, autonomy, and camaraderie). PV was the strongest predictor of behavioral intention, ahead of effort expectancy, facilitating conditions, and performance expectancy, and behavioral intention strongly predicted actual use. The model explained 75% of the variance in behavioral intention and 51% in actual use. The findings offer design principles for community-centered crime-reporting technologies.


### arXiv 2611.03374 - Characterizing When Language Models Take Harmful Actions to Protect Their Assigned Goals

- categories: cs.LG, cs.AI, cs.CL
- published: 2026-11-02T16:17:54Z
- authors: Brandon Jaipersaud, Elliot Creager
- query-set: cs.AI
- abs: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.03374

Recent language model (LM) safety failures have largely been studied as disparate phenomena. In agentic misalignment, a model blackmails an executive to avoid being replaced. In alignment faking, it complies with harmful requests during training to keep its values from being modified. We argue that these behaviours share a common decision structure: a model assigned a goal must either take a harmful action to preserve it or refuse and lose it. We formalize this as a goal-harm conflict and present the same conflict under various elicitation protocols that range from compact forced-choice prompts to simulated agentic environments. These protocols span two perspectives on LM decision-making, which we term (i) the logit view, reading preferences from answer-token probabilities, and (ii) the sampling view, classifying open-ended responses with an LM judge. Under the logit view, we show that goal importance and harm severity additively shape how a model resolves these value conflicts. Under the sampling view, we derive two factorizations of the harmful-action probability that safety evaluations commonly report. The first measures how well a preference read from answer tokens carries over into behaviour, such as making a harmful tool call. The second attributes a low harmful-action rate either to refusal or to responses that reach no resolution. We then show how fictional context changes a model's preference under the logit view, and how tool use and partial observability -- two features of agentic evaluations -- change its harmful-action rate under the sampling view. Our work offers a descriptive framework for studying how LMs resolve such value conflicts and for estimating low-probability harmful actions at a finer granularity than the aggregate harm rates commonly reported in safety evaluations.


### arXiv 2611.03330 - The SIMPLE Law for Energy Cost of LLM Inference

- categories: cs.AI, cs.LG, cs.PF
- published: 2026-11-02T15:51:46Z
- authors: Hiari Pizzini Cavagna, Andrea Proia, Daniele Cesarini, Zeynep Kiziltan, Andrea Bartolini
- query-set: cs.AI
- abs: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.03330

Generative AI is billed per token, yet tokens are not equally expensive. Each token enters the KV-cache and raises the cost of those that follow, so energy grows non-linearly along a sequence whose tokens look identical. No analytical model states what a token costs, and the most accurate SOTA model is a fit, not a law: it absorbs architecture into fitted coefficients, requiring per-LLM fitting. We derive closed-form expressions of the computational and memory-access complexity of LLM inference in terms of architectural hyperparameters, covering MHA and GQA, and compare a family of analytical models of increasing granularity. From these, we obtain the SIMPLE Inference Model to Predict LLMs Energy (SIMPLE), a four-parameter law that separates prefill from decode, and decode into KV-cache cost and next-token computation. Over traces of 13 dense LLMs from six families on NVIDIA H100 GPUs, it attains R2 = 0.969 and 11.97% MAPE, 3.1-3.5x below the SOTA, without per-LLM calibration. The SIMPLE law explains as well as predicts: prefill energy grows quadratically with the hidden dimension, so larger LLMs suffer most from long inputs; and more aggressive GQA KV grouping reduces the per-token energy by up to 3x relative to standard MHA. Increasing the reasoning token budget pushes output length beyond the analytically derived energy minimum, driving super-linear energy growth; although accuracy grows with the budget, energy grows faster, so Intelligence per Joule peaks at an intermediate budget and falls by up to 3.14x across a 4.0x reasoning-budget range. Agentic serving raises energy per request by 13.0x relative to non-agentic ones, driven by the growth of both the prefill and the KV-cache terms with input length; while prefix caching removes most of the prefill cost (-93%), the KV-cache term is unaffected, so even a cached agentic request costs 4.8x more.


### arXiv 2611.03327 - From FAIR to AI-FAIR Metadata-First Infrastructure for AI-Enabled Science

- categories: cs.AI, cs.DB, cs.HC, cs.IR, q-bio.QM
- published: 2026-11-02T15:49:34Z
- authors: Elizabeth G. Campolongo, Eric R. Sokol, Marty Downs, Gina Glenne, Gregory E. Maurer, Sydne Record
- query-set: cs.AI
- abs: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.03327

Artificial intelligence (AI) technologies and their capabilities have advanced rapidly over the past few years, leading to widespread interest and adoption in the biological and ecological sciences. However, the data infrastructure and computational resources that provide the foundation for AI workflows in ecology and biodiversity science remain fragmented. Moreover, the flow of value remains largely unidirectional: data move from collectors, through repositories and aggregators into models, and finally to research products or management actions. The derived value, such as corrected taxonomies, annotations, quality flags, and insights, rarely returns to source communities. We propose an extension of the original FAIR principles to address the needs of AI-ready infrastructure: the AI-FAIR metadata criteria, which place AI-enabled science in the context of a bidirectional Question-Data-Model triangle. This framework is designed to facilitate large-scale content-based analysis across diverse collections and ensure that the scientists generating the data benefit from the AI-enabled advances built on their data. Alongside these criteria, we introduce an agentic evaluation workflow that both rates existing datasets on the AI-FAIR and traditional FAIR criteria and provides actionable feedback where critical metadata is missing or incomplete. This modular dataset evaluation tool, paired with a domain-appropriate checklist, translates these principles into a reusable assessment of machine-learning readiness, AI-readiness for specified tasks, traceability, and ethical and CARE-aligned stewardship. Through this approach we aim to create a community-informed system to close the model data loop, so that AI also enriches data to further enable scientific discovery.


### arXiv 2611.03319 - TrafficInstruct: A Multi-Task Network Traffic Dataset and Benchmark for Instruction Tuning

- categories: cs.CR, cs.AI
- published: 2026-11-02T15:44:53Z
- authors: Zhengdao Li, Hui Wen, Zixuan Wan, Weifeng Zhang, Yan Wu, Benlong Wu
- query-set: cs.AI
- abs: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.03319

Instruction tuning for multi-task network traffic analysis requires data with clearly defined sources, supervision targets, and evaluation conditions. We introduce TrafficInstruct, a newly collected dataset comprising raw packet captures and 55,000 structured supervised fine-tuning records across ten tasks: malware traffic detection, botnet detection, malicious DNS-over-HTTPS detection, Web attack detection, advanced persistent threat detection, encrypted VPN detection, Tor behavior detection, encrypted application/service classification, website fingerprinting, and application classification under concept drift. An automated workflow captures traffic from controlled software executions, attack experiments, and application activities. Labels are supported by task-specific collection evidence, including process or socket attribution, activity records, and execution outcomes. Filtering, sanitization, and task-specific transformations produce instructions, traffic observations, and structured answers. We verify sample sources and partition the dataset by complete collection sessions, keeping all samples from the same session within one partition. The benchmark defines allowed inputs, deterministic label mappings, fixed training/validation/test partitions, and task-wise scoring rules. We evaluate majority-class, random-forest, and ET-BERT baselines, an unadapted 27B language model, and independently trained task-specific LoRA adapters on a shared frozen base. Adapters are selected using validation data and evaluated on the corresponding test sets. Task-specific adaptation improves classification performance, supporting instruction tuning across diverse traffic-analysis tasks.


### arXiv 2611.03308 - LLMs Depend on Specialist Tools for Out-of-Distribution Generalization in MS/MS Structural Elucidation

- categories: cs.LG, cs.AI, cs.CL, stat.ML
- published: 2026-11-02T15:39:07Z
- authors: Gabriel Asher, Cailum M. K. Stienstra, Tomo Oga, Devesh Shah Antonio H. de O. Fonseca, Niall O'Connor, Michael Widrich
- query-set: cs.AI
- abs: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.03308

A frontier language model was recently reported to solve 1D NMR structural elucidation without tools. We ask whether this capability transfers to tandem mass spectrometry (MS/MS) in the de novo setting, where the model must propose a structure directly from the spectrum without a candidate list or reference library. To separate reasoning from recall, we build a 100-analyte benchmark from MassSpecGym, split by each analyte's textual footprint into in-distribution (well documented in literature) and out-of-distribution (essentially undocumented) arms. We hold the model, harness, and output format fixed and vary only the LLM's access to domain-specific tools. Without tools, a frontier LLM reaches 52% Top-1 accuracy in-distribution but only 2% out-of-distribution. Only access to de novo structure generators improves out-of-distribution accuracy, raising it to as much as 38% Top-1, and the resulting agent outperforms the standalone de novo models both in and out of distribution. These results suggest that LLMs are not effective at de novo MS/MS structural elucidation on their own, and that further gains depend on the specialist tools available to the agent rather than on the LLM itself.


### arXiv 2611.03301 - An Axiomatic Framework for Reinforcement Learning in Open Multi-Agent Systems

- categories: cs.LG, cs.AI, cs.MA
- published: 2026-11-02T15:36:22Z
- authors: Jianhong Wang, Yang Li, Samuel Kaski, Jonathan Lawry
- query-set: cs.AI
- abs: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.03301

Open multi-agent systems, where only a subset of agents are controllable, are central to real-world domains such as smart grids, yet lack a principled foundation for reinforcement learning algorithm design. We study this setting through n-agent ad hoc teamwork (NAHT), a recent framework for learning in open multi-agent environments. We propose an axiomatic framework that derives value function learning from the Shapley axioms (Efficiency, Symmetry and Linearity), treating them as structural constraints rather than heuristic design choices. This formulation builds on a novel NAHT decomposition, which characterises the fundamental difference between open and fully controllable multi-agent systems, and establishes a cooperative game-theoretic foundation for learning. We further show that enforcing these axioms on learning individual value functions recovers the Shapley value for Dec-POMDPs, providing a principled bridge between reinforcement learning and classical cooperative game theory. Building on this framework, we introduce Shapley Machines and Banzhaf Machines, two generic algorithmic templates that can be instantiated with standard reinforcement learning methods. When applied to strong base algorithms such as IPPO and POAM, our methods consistently improve learning efficiency and generalisation to unseen agent types and conventions across NAHT benchmark tasks. The code of experiments is published on https://github.com/hsvgbkhgbv/axiomatic-rl-naht.


### arXiv 2611.03298 - SQLoom: Set-Contrastive Learning of Syntactic, Semantic, and Logical Dependencies for Text-to-SQL

- categories: cs.DB, cs.AI
- published: 2026-11-02T15:33:40Z
- authors: Hao Fu, Degang Sun, Wan Wei, Jinxia Wei, Chun Long, Peng Gao
- query-set: cs.AI
- abs: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.03298

Text-to-SQL translates natural language questions into SQL queries, enabling database access without writing SQL. Fine-tuning open-weight large language models (LLMs) is cost-effective, but existing methods treat SQL as ordinary text and optimize token-level likelihood without explicitly modeling its syntactic, semantic, and logical requirements. Thus, a single incorrect alias or comparison operator can invalidate a query while contributing little to training loss. We propose SQLoom, a constraint-aware fine-tuning framework with supervision at three levels: syntactic validity under SQL grammar and database schema, semantic alignment between query decisions and the question, and logical consistency across dependent clause decisions. SQLoom extends supervised fine-tuning with a unified set-contrastive objective favoring sets of correct outputs over minimally different incorrect ones at each level. LLM agents propose this supervision, which a SQL parser and execution on counterexample databases verify. We also release Spider-Refined, a training dataset correcting 378 flawed Spider annotations and enriching database schemas. Experiments on Spider, BIRD, and three perturbed Spider variants show that SQLoom improves execution accuracy for Qwen3-1.7B from 62.9% zero-shot to 88.7% on Spider development, reaching 89.2% on Spider test and 73.4% on BIRD development. It outperforms fine-tuning, reinforcement learning, and agent-based baselines with up to 70 billion parameters. On question perturbations, SQLoom loses at most 0.6 accuracy points versus up to 12.1 for agent-based methods. It generates SQL in a single pass at 0.42s per query, 23$\times$ faster than a GPT-4-based agent method. These results demonstrate the effectiveness of set-contrastive supervision over token-level training.


### arXiv 2611.03289 - Top-$k$ Retrieval Leaves Memory Value Undetermined

- categories: cs.AI
- published: 2026-11-02T15:28:04Z
- authors: Arman Behnam
- query-set: cs.AI
- abs: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.03289

A memory-augmented agent must decide which memories to keep, merge and retrieve, and its only evidence of their value is its retrieval log: the memories it retrieved in each episode and whether it succeeded. Existing methods assign every memory a value from this log, even when the log cannot separate one memory's contribution from another's. We treat the log as an experiment that nobody designed and ask which memory values it determines. Two counts answer this before any outcome is observed: how often each memory was retrieved, and how often each pair was retrieved together. From these counts, we prove that top-$k$ retrieval leaves every memory value undetermined, however many episodes are logged, and that a question with its own memories, asked once, determines at most one of their values. On fourteen standard memory benchmarks, no memory value is determined in any of 55 configurations. Both causes are choices about retrieval. We introduce LEDGER, which estimates each determined Shapley value in closed form with a confidence interval and reports every other value as undetermined. It merges memories with identical histories without losing a determined value, and it retrieves so that the next log determines more. On pooled HotpotQA, LEDGER raises the fraction of memories determined from 0.001 to 0.797, at a cost of 2.6 accuracy points on HotpotQA and with a gain of 2.7 on LoCoMo.


### arXiv 2611.03257 - Rethinking Fairness Metrics for Multi-Class and Multi-Group Assessment: Not Everything is Binary

- categories: cs.LG, cs.AI
- published: 2026-11-02T15:05:12Z
- authors: Matteo Grazioso, Fabio Naccarella, Chiara Gallese, Marco S. Nobile
- query-set: cs.AI
- abs: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.03257

Artificial Intelligence, and Machine Learning in general, is becoming pervasive across all domains and disciplines. By means of this data-driven approach, algorithms can learns complex task just by learning from samples. However, if the data-set is biased, they might lead to the development of unfair computer systems, that is, characterised by a prejudice towards specific individuals or groups, which might get an unmotivated output from the system. This circumstance lead to the development of multiple metrics designed to quantify the level of unfairness of an algorithm. However, a generalised assumption in the literature is that the sensitive features and the output labels are binary. When the data is not binary, it is forcefully binarized using a one-versus-the-rest simplification. In this work, we discuss the limitations of existing metrics and propose novel, mathematically sound, and generalized multi-group and multi-class formulations of the metrics. Ultimately, our results highlight the empirical assessment distortion caused by binarization strategies, which reveals the limitations of binary fairness metrics in the multi class setting.


### arXiv 2611.03238 - ScienceCUA: Resource-to-Skill Construction and Execution-Guided Skill Evolution for Scientific Computer Use Agents

- categories: cs.AI
- published: 2026-11-02T14:50:06Z
- authors: Daiqiang Li, Jingyi Peng, Rui Hu, Jia Li, Xuyang Liu, Guanren Qiao
- query-set: cs.AI
- abs: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.03238

Computer-use agents for scientific software require application-specific expertise. Much of this expertise is documented in human-authored software manuals and web pages. However, turning these resources into executable, reusable skills remains challenging due to mismatches between documented and deployed software versions and differences in scope between documentation coverage and task requirements. We introduce ScienceCUA, a two-stage framework combining resource-to-skill construction with execution-guided skill evolution. First, ScienceCUA identifies tasks supported by the software from its documentation, infers operational trajectories, and generalizes them into reusable Skills. Command catalogs, syntax constraints, and shortcuts are organized separately as References for on-demand lookup. Then, ScienceCUA uses execution feedback to drive skill self-evolution by contrasting successful and failed trajectories for the same task, identifying deficiencies, revising existing skills or adding new ones, and updating the skill library only after repeated validation in real environments. To enable reliable evaluation without benchmark noise or task leakage, we introduce two improved benchmarks.
