ScienceBoard-Verified provides an audited and corrected basis for measuring task performance, while ScienceBoard-VE separates skill evolution from final testing through disjoint evolution and test tasks and further supports native-agent evaluation. Experiments show that the initial Skills and References improve average task success rate by up to 7.23 percentage points over the base model, while skill self-evolution increases the total gain over the baseline to as much as 17.12 percentage points. ScienceCUA turns software documentation into an evolving set of executable skills and queryable references, offering a practical way for general-purpose agents to use scientific software reliably and at scale.


### arXiv 2611.03233 - Axion 1.0: Open Agent Foundation Models, Simplified

- categories: cs.AI
- published: 2026-11-02T14:48:57Z
- authors: Yuqi Jia, Yunjie Tian, Jiayu Zheng, Yijun Liang, Jingchen Sun, Mengyu Zhao
- query-set: cs.AI
- abs: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.03233

We introduce Axion 1.0, a fully open agent foundation model that combines strong and broad agentic capabilities with a remarkably simple training recipe. Axion 1.0 is trained in only three stages: two lightweight supervised fine-tuning stages followed by reinforcement learning, without dedicated mid-training, direct preference optimization (DPO), or on-policy distillation (OPD). The first SFT stage develops strong self-contained reasoning through model-aware data construction, where we synthesize challenging problems and adversarially strengthen them based on the base model's own successful solution strategies. The second stage extends these capabilities to tool-dependent reasoning through a scalable pipeline for constructing diverse and automatically verifiable tasks grounded in real-world data sources and deterministic computation. Finally, reinforcement learning further strengthens long-horizon reasoning and interaction with external tools and environments. Despite this compact training recipe, Axion 1.0 achieves strong performance across a broad suite of agentic benchmarks and is competitive with leading commercial models on several of them. Equally importantly, Axion 1.0 is open beyond model weights: we release the complete recipe, including data construction, generation, filtering, verification, prompts, and training code, enabling the entire development process to be reproduced and extended. Axion 1.0 demonstrates that strong agent foundation models can be both open and simple, offering a practical and reproducible path toward advancing open agentic intelligence.


### arXiv 2611.03223 - Do Agents Still Follow the Rules? Benchmarking Rule Compliance under Agent Context Compaction

- categories: cs.CR, cs.AI, cs.CL
- published: 2026-11-02T14:45:36Z
- authors: Yiwei Zha, Qingyu Meng, Herbert Bos, Min Chen
- query-set: cs.AI
- abs: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.03223

LLM agents compact long histories to fit their context windows, but the compacted context must still carry the operating rules that govern later actions. Prior work shows that violations rise after compaction, but measures compliance outside a complete agent harness and does not identify where each violation comes from. We introduce ARCBench(Agent Rule Compliance Benchmark), a configurable benchmark built on the open-source pi agent harness. Its tool-calling agent loop lets us measure rule compliance from actions taken during execution, bringing the evaluation closer to deployed agent workflows. Using ARCBench, we examine where violations after compaction come from, and how different compaction configurations and adversarial settings change their sources. We find that compaction rarely deletes the rule: most violations after compaction occur while the rule is still in the compacted history, with the agent treating a later request as permission, ignoring the rule, and proceeding anyway. Where violations come from depends on the configuration: how the rule is delivered decides whether the agent would break it even without compaction, while the compaction strategy and budget decide whether the rule survives. The strongest compactor-targeted attack more than doubles violations by corrupting the rule in the summary and changing how the agents behave, whereas the strongest defense restores the rule in most summary but still violates. Finally, retaining a rule does not guarantee compliance: compacted runs that retain the rule violate more than twice as often as runs with the full history.


### arXiv 2611.03222 - CERBO: Certified Recourse at a Requested Rate for Gradient-Boosted Trees

- categories: cs.LG, cs.AI, cs.CY
- published: 2026-11-02T14:45:31Z
- authors: Farhan Shahriyar Hossain, Taufikur Rahman Fuad, Md. Zarif Bin Hasnat, Md Abrar Jahin, Md Rizwan Parvez
- query-set: cs.AI
- abs: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.03222

Recourse-aware learning trains models so that people who receive an adverse decision can reverse it with an affordable change. For gradient-boosted decision trees (GBDTs), the existing method, RABIT, exposes a single recourse weight whose effect on the recourse rate is abrupt and data-dependent, and its training bound cannot certify recourse for any rejected individual. We present Certified Recourse Boosting (CERBO), which takes a requested recourse rate and returns a GBDT with certified actions: it tracks exact additive effort along tree paths, trains against committed boxes (a family of bounds that contains RABIT's and certifies a within-budget action for each training sample) and adjusts the weight after every tree until the certified rate meets the request. A calibration step guarantees the rate on new individuals, and the certifier runs on any tree ensemble, including XGBoost and LightGBM, in milliseconds per person without a solver. In RABIT's own experimental protocol, at equal accuracy, CERBO raises the recourse ratio from 0.90 to 0.99 on FICO and from 0.64 to 0.77 on Bail. Under additive costs on six data sets, it raises the area under the accuracy-recourse frontier in all 12 settings, and in simulated deployments it guarantees recourse to 70% of rejected individuals in 11 of 12 settings, against 2 for RABIT.


### arXiv 2611.03219 - Can Coding Agents Sustain Continual Evolution without Forgetting?

- categories: cs.SE, cs.AI
- published: 2026-11-02T14:43:37Z
- authors: Shangziqi Zhao, Yitong Zhang, Jia Li
- query-set: cs.AI
- abs: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.03219

Recently, harness self-evolution has emerged as an important paradigm for improving agent performance. Prior studies show that evolving the agent harness can effectively enhance agent capabilities without modifying the foundation model's parameters. We find that existing approaches largely study agent evolution on a single task. In practice, coding agents may need to handle multiple task types over time. Whether an agent harness can continuously evolve across multiple tasks without suffering from catastrophic forgetting remains an important open question. In this paper, we investigate this open question through a comprehensive empirical study of continual harness self-evolution. We organize three software engineering task datasets into six different evolution directions and apply two representative harness evolution methods to perform continual evolution along these directions. Our experimental results show that evolution on a subsequent dataset can substantially undermine the performance on previously seen datasets. For example, with DeepSeek-V4-Flash-0731, Meta-Harness improves its performance on SWE-Bench to 94.0% after evolution. However, after further evolution on FeatureBench, its performance on SWE-Bench drops to 32.0% by the fifth round, corresponding to a 66% relative decrease. Finally, based on our experimental findings, we identify several insights, providing directions for the continual self-evolution of agent harnesses. Code is available at https://github.com/THU-Agent/continual-harness-evolution.


### arXiv 2611.03213 - Reducing SNAP Payment Errors at Lower Cost With AI: A Simulation Study

- categories: cs.CY, cs.AI, stat.AP
- published: 2026-11-02T14:36:24Z
- authors: Alex Chohlas-Wood
- query-set: cs.AI
- abs: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.03213

New federal legislation will require states with high payment error rates in the Supplemental Nutrition Assistance Program (SNAP) to pay a share of benefit costs, potentially costing some states over a billion dollars per year. These costs create substantial pressure to improve payment accuracy. One strategy to improve payment accuracy is preauthorization review, in which staff double-check applications and correct mistakes before benefits are issued. But limited staff capacity makes it important to target these reviews effectively. One leading targeting approach uses statistical risk models to prioritize cases likely to contain errors. Another novel approach uses AI to flag specific potential discrepancies for reviewers to check. For states under pressure to reduce error rates, it is unclear which approach-or combination of approaches-would reduce payment error rates most effectively. We compare these strategies through simulations using public SNAP quality control data. Under our main assumptions, risk-based review alone costs about seven times as much as combining AI flags with risk scores. This cost advantage persists across a wide range of plausible assumptions. Our findings suggest that AI-assisted preauthorization review could be a key tool to help states avoid substantial cost-sharing obligations.


### arXiv 2611.03198 - Written Once, Read Differently How Language Models Own Answers Steer Their Next Ones

- categories: cs.CL, cs.AI
- published: 2026-11-02T14:25:14Z
- authors: Marcus Armstrong
- query-set: cs.AI
- abs: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.03198

When a language model answers a yes/no question, that answer changes how it answers a different question about the same entity. We study this \emph{self-commitment} in 22 open-weight models from four families (0.5B--72B parameters), fixing the model's first answer and measuring the shift in its second across 3,600 question pairs about 120 entities. Each answer writes a \emph{commitment direction} into the residual stream. The similarity of two questions' directions predicts their coupling on held-out entities ($ρ= 0.43$--$0.85$), and ablating a single direction shared across topics leaves at most 17\% of the effect in every model. How this direction is read is far less stable: the reading circuit spreads across more attention heads as models grow, instruction tuning makes more questions read it in the opposite sense, and in one model changing only the system prompt reverses the circuit's sign. Ablating the direction while sparing the attention sink removes most of the coupling at under 1.3\% perplexity cost in 18 of 19 instruction-tuned models, though it shifts standalone answers in two. Without the sink spared, the direction's alignment with the leading principal component of ordinary text predicts which models pay a high cost. Written once, the commitment is read differently across scale, training and prompt.


### arXiv 2611.03183 - Auto-Research Agent on MSA Organization to Improve Protein-Protein Complex Structure Prediction

- categories: cs.AI
- published: 2026-11-02T14:17:43Z
- authors: Zilu Zhang, Jing Bao, Lifeng Qiao, Sheng Xu, Qiantai Feng, Siqi Sun
- query-set: cs.AI
- abs: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.03183

Protein-complex structure prediction with AlphaFold 3 depends on MSA content, depth, and cross-chain pairing. Current pipelines apply uniform recipes, typically maximizing depth and pairing by species, yet even whether cross-chain pairing helps AF3 remains contested. We show that the value of both depth and pairing is strongly target-dependent, so no single recipe is optimal across interfaces. We introduce an autonomous agent that treats a frozen AF3 as a black-box experimental instrument. For each target, the agent formulates biological hypotheses, analyzes and reorganizes the MSA, and iteratively refines sequence selection and pairing using predictor label-free signals alone. On 1{,}015 protein--protein interfaces from three benchmarks released after AF3's training cutoff, the agent improves overall interface DockQ over AF3's default pipeline and fixed MSA recipes on every benchmark, while using on average 60\% fewer MSA sequences than the default. Gains are largest for inter-species heteromers, where species-based pairing is unreliable: mean DockQ increases from 0.465 to 0.612 on FoldBench and from 0.482 to 0.562 on HD439. The improvements arise from both sequence construction and pairing policy, including deliberate non-pairing.These results recast MSA design as a target-specific problem and show that an autonomous agent can adapt it at test time through biological reasoning and self-directed, confidence-guided experimentation.


### arXiv 2611.03178 - CoworkOS:Scaling Agent Coworkers for Real-world Automation

- categories: cs.AI, cs.CL
- published: 2026-11-02T14:15:11Z
- authors: Jiajie Jin, Guanting Dong, Chengjun Pan, Xin Deng, Zhuohui Sheng, Guoqiang Wang
- query-set: cs.AI
- abs: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.03178

Rapid advances in cowork agents are shifting human-AI collaboration toward real-world automation, delivering software, designs, and other artifacts. However, such productivity work demands both high-quality delivery on complex productivity tasks and continued monitoring and decision-making over long horizons, a combination that remains an open challenge. We introduce CoworkOS, a general-purpose harness for long-horizon work that separates task strategy from execution management: the model decides actions, delegation, and acceptance, while the runtime maintains state and feedback. Its three-layer architecture enables direct execution or delegation to Subagents, synchronously or asynchronously, under stable responsibilities. Through a shared application layer, humans and agents inspect and revise the same evolving documents, presentations, CAD models, and Blender scenes, so human-in-the-loop participation can enter both through conversation and through direct modification of the work itself. To sustain continuity across agents and sessions, CoworkOS further introduces hierarchical workspaces and persistent semantic memory, so working files, key decisions, and long-running Job logs remain shareable, reusable, and resumable after interruption. Across 12 benchmarks and 6 categories of real-world applications, CoworkOS proves highly competitive with frontier agent applications such as Codex and DeepSeek Harness. As a sustained-work case study, CoworkOS uses GLM-5.3 as its backbone to conduct RSI-driven automated post-training on Qwen3.6-35B-A3B. Over five rounds, the model's average score across eight benchmarks rose from 33.19 to 42.95 (+9.76). Looking ahead, we believe CoworkOS opens a meaningful path toward general cowork-agent intelligence.


### arXiv 2611.03165 - LaBraM at Scale: Scaling Data and Models for EEG Pretraining

- categories: cs.LG, cs.AI
- published: 2026-11-02T14:05:45Z
- authors: Jun-Yu Pan, Ruicheng Yin, Bao-Liang Lu, Dongsheng Li, Wei-Long Zheng, Yansen Wang
- query-set: cs.AI
- abs: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.03165

LaBraM, an early EEG foundation model, demonstrated that large-scale EEG pretraining can yield strong representations for diverse downstream tasks. Building on this foundation, we expand pretraining data and model capacity while retaining its core encoder and objective. We train ten encoders with 5.8 million to 1.97 billion parameters on three EEG collections spanning 25,067 to 79,910 hours, adapting signal preparation and training to heterogeneous recordings. Across 23 tasks, Atom-L* reaches 59.05% mean balanced accuracy at its best evaluated full fine-tuning (Full-FT) checkpoint, 3.61 percentage points above the strongest baseline EEG foundation model. Quark-G leads linear probing (LP) at 54.33%, a 1.24-point margin. Both also lead average task rank. At the last evaluated checkpoints, larger encoders improve Full-FT more consistently than LP, and stronger LP does not guarantee stronger Full-FT. Preserving patch structure improves LP over average pooling. Data-fraction experiments suggest capacity-dependent saturation, while checkpoint trajectories show that downstream performance can decline as pretraining loss falls. Joint classification with a shared Qwen3 readout reaches 50.50% with Molecule-H and Qwen3-8B; increasing language-model size provides no consistent gain. These results demonstrate the value of scaling LaBraM and the importance of matching pretraining scale to downstream adaptation.


### arXiv 2611.03162 - Beyond Surface Metrics: Multi-Condition Requirements in Enterprise Maintenance and AI Coding Benchmarks

- categories: cs.SE, cs.AI
- published: 2026-11-02T14:03:38Z
- authors: Heeyong Eun
- query-set: cs.AI
- abs: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.03162

Repository-level AI coding benchmarks measure task success but do not directly capture the conditions required for software maintenance completion. We study multi-condition fulfillment, where a maintenance task must satisfy multiple operational conditions, sometimes with directional dependencies. Using a fixed 18-criterion taxonomy, we analyze 2,925 Apache Fineract issues and 944 tasks from SWE-bench Pro, SWE-PolyBench Java, and SWE-EVO. We examine condition breadth, corpus-level co-occurrence, and source-grounded directional requirement relationships. Among 1,177 benchmark task pairs matched on issue classification, modified-file count, and code churn, the mean fulfillment-set Jaccard distance is 0.8138, and 57.4% have disjoint condition sets. Condition count alone does not explain this contrast: SWE-bench Pro averages 2.47 conditions per task, versus 1.78 for Fineract. Temporal (B4) and numerical (B2) conditions have a phi coefficient of +0.430 in Fineract versus -0.013 in the pooled benchmark corpus. Among temporally tagged tasks, numerical conditions are also specified in 48.85% (254/520) of Fineract cases versus 5.81% (5/86) of benchmark cases. We document 16 directional relationships in Fineract requirements involving time, calculation, and state preservation, and inspect four benchmark cases to illustrate how relevant conditions appear in evaluation assertions. These examples do not quantify benchmark-wide oracle omissions. The results show that similar surface task profiles can mask substantially different fulfillment-condition sets. We recommend reporting condition breadth, co-occurrence, and directional relationships separately. The corpus comparisons are descriptive and may reflect domain differences; they do not establish causality or production failure rates.


### arXiv 2611.03157 - Toward Knowledge-Centric AI: Discovering, Internalizing, and Using Explicit Knowledge Across Agents and Generations

- categories: cs.AI
- published: 2026-11-02T14:03:12Z
- authors: Yisen Gao, Haoyu Huang, Zhongwei Xie, Yufei Li, Hong Ting Tsang, Boyi Zhang
- query-set: cs.AI
- abs: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.03157

Knowledge is how understanding accumulates across people and generations: durable records that outlive their producers and that others can check, combine, teach, and correct. AI agents are now built and run by many independent users and organizations, and each agent is upgraded, replaced, or retired within months. Yet what an agent learns stays trapped in model parameters, a transient context window, or a system-specific memory store that is appended to and retrieved from rather than processed. We argue that the next generation of AI should be knowledge-centric, built around an explicit, continuously evolving knowledge layer independent of any single agent. In this loop, agents discover knowledge from their environments and trajectories; the layer internalizes it by turning fragmented, heterogeneous records and experience into composable, revisable understanding; and agents across systems and generations use the result and return new outcomes for revision. While discovery and use have advanced rapidly with large language model agents, internalization remains the bottleneck. We argue that internalization should become a persistent object of training and maintenance-centered on condition-preserving conceptualization of events and deductive, inductive, and abductive reasoning across noisy, heterogeneous representations (text, tables, graphs, rules, and programs). To power this layer, we call for knowledge foundation models that provide shared neural capacity for knowledge computation, and outline a research agenda showing how an explicit knowledge layer delivers reuse, cost efficiency, auditability, and continuity across agent generations.


### arXiv 2611.03143 - Visual-to-Executable Procedural Reconstruction of Buildings

- categories: cs.CV, cs.AI
- published: 2026-11-02T13:52:45Z
- authors: Zichong Zhu, Zhihao Liu, Andrei Sharf, Zhanglin Cheng
- query-set: cs.AI
- abs: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.03143

Procedural modeling offers compact, editable, and reusable representations for architectural content, but building such models still depends heavily on manually authored grammars. We study image-based procedural building reconstruction from in-the-wild images, with the goal of recovering an executable procedural description that can be instantiated as an editable 3D building asset. Because a single image provides only partial and view-dependent evidence, we focus on recovering the dominant visible facade(s) and attach the recovered facade program to a simple mass model for building-level instantiation. Our method decomposes this task into two structured prediction stages. First, a Global Structure Layout Tree recovers facade-scale split-and-repeat organization through a fine-tuned layout model with render-guided visual-symbolic reflection. Second, a Component Parameterization schema maps representative facade tiles to structured procedural attributes for local architectural component synthesis. These two representations are then deterministically compiled into executable Computer Generated Architecture (CGA) programs, avoiding the brittleness of direct low-level program generation. Experiments show that the proposed framework handles both regular facades and challenging in-the-wild images, while maintaining high structural consistency, procedural executability, and fine-grained architectural detail. The recovered programs further enable controllable and reusable 3D building asset generation.


### arXiv 2611.03126 - CUA-Master: Scaling Computer-use Agents through Open-World Recursive Improvements

- categories: cs.AI
- published: 2026-11-02T13:41:14Z
- authors: Xinlei Yu, Gen Li, Ji Zhao, Zhangquan Chen, Congcong Wang, Shuai Dong
- query-set: cs.AI
- abs: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.03126

Despite recent advances in CUAs, their development remains constrained by the limited diversity, controllability, and reliability of interaction data. Moreover, conventional optimization pipelines lack iterative feedback, while existing recursive improvement approaches often rely on self-generated experiences, limiting ability expansion and yielding diminishing returns. To address these challenges, we introduce CUA-Master, a nested double-loop recursive improvement framework. At the system level, an outer world-data-model flywheel continuously incorporates open-world corpus and leverages evolving model weaknesses to guide subsequent data construction and optimization iterations. At the data level, an inner flywheel couples open-world seeding and autocatalytic propagation. The former mines authentic workflows as seed tasks from instructional videos, and employs visual-semantic dual-path trajectory reconstruction; The latter transforms these seeds into reusable primitives for controllable synthesis of diverse, complex, and verifiable tasks. To enable efficient and reliable trajectory collection, we further introduce a feedback-guided snapshot-rollback retry mechanism in various real environments. Extensive experiments demonstrate that CUA-Master-27B achieves competitive performance across GUI-only and GUI-CLI hybrid benchmarks, scoring 80.2 on OSWorld Verified, 36.9 on OSWorld 2.0, 43.9 on WeaveBench, and 25.0 on Agents' Last Exam. Our framework also substantially improves the quality and efficiency of data synthesis, and exhibits continuous gains across recursive iterations.


### arXiv 2611.03121 - ComplianceAtlas: A Taxonomy-Driven Agentic AI Framework for Automated AI Act Gap Analysis and Remediation

- categories: cs.AI
- published: 2026-11-02T13:37:32Z
- authors: Ilias Driouich, Hongliu Cao, Eoin Thomas, Yannick Devaux, Agnes Brodelle
- query-set: cs.AI
- abs: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.03121

Assessing regulatory compliance for high-risk AI systems requires systematic identification of technical gaps such as missing controls, in- adequate processes, or shallow evaluation of practices. Compliance officers typically admin- ister structured questionnaires collecting evi- dence of current practices, where organizations respond with descriptions of their existing pro- cesses, technical capabilities, and architectural choices. However, translating these compli- ance questionnaire responses into typed, ac- tionable gap diagnoses remains unsystematic. In this paper, we present a literature-grounded taxonomy of technical compliance gaps cover- ing areas such as accuracy, robustness, logging, and human oversight, and embed it in GapTo- Compliance, a two-stage agentic framework coordinating two specialized agents: a Gap Extraction Agent that performs hierarchical rea- soning from compliance areas to specific gap types, and a Remediation Planning Agent that ranks remediation actions from a remediation catalog via multi-signal scoring combining se- mantic similarity, regulatory alignment, and effort estimation. We evaluate this framework against four automatic compliance gap detec- tion baselines across 9 AI systems and 6 AI families. Our approach achieves a gap-type F1 of 0.831 (vs. 0.533 for the strongest baseline) and remediation P@5 of 0.872 (vs. 0.635 for retrieval methods), demonstrating promising potential for automated compliance workflows. The structured agent coordination reveals a path toward human-in-the-loop compliance systems where automated gap extraction reduces expert burden while maintaining auditability, and the taxonomy-driven design enables extension to emerging regulatory frameworks beyond


### arXiv 2611.03116 - From Local Skills to Long-Horizon Web Tasks: Progressive Skill Exploration for LLM Web Agents

- categories: cs.AI
- published: 2026-11-02T13:32:59Z
- authors: Xueyang Feng, Xiaohe Bo, Xu Chen, Quanyu Dai, Feng Wen
- query-set: cs.AI
- abs: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.03116

Large language models (LLMs) are driving autonomous agents toward real-world environments, with the Web emerging as one of the most representative interaction scenarios. However, training Web agents with naive exploration remains challenging: successful trajectories are rare, and the sparse terminal rewards make it difficult for agents to obtain useful feedback from failed trajectories. Inspired by skill acquisition theory, we propose Skill-Composed Progressive Exploration (SCOPE), a short-to-long learning framework for long-horizon Web agents that decomposes complex task exploration into a gradual expansion process from local skills to complete task-solving capability. Specifically, SCOPE iteratively explores around local skills: Skill Discovery extracts reusable local skills and failure experiences from historical rollouts; Skill Orchestration composes discovered skills into high-level skill chains; and Skill Expansion leverages failure experiences aligned with the current skill chain to guide the agent toward exploring new subskills. Through this process, SCOPE progressively constructs task-completing skill chains across rollouts and internalizes them into the policy parameters through agentic on-policy distillation. Extensive experiments on WebArena, WebVoyager, and WebShop show that SCOPE consistently improves exploration dynamics and achieves 3.9% to 18.6% relative gains over the strongest baselines in each setting.


### arXiv 2611.03109 - Multimodal Prompt Learning with Dual-Granularity Contrastive Alignment for Preoperative Prediction of Lung Adenocarcinoma Invasiveness Using 3D CT

- categories: cs.CV, cs.AI
- published: 2026-11-02T13:28:16Z
- authors: Qing Huang, Xiaotong Tan, Lintong Yao, Wufeng Xue, Ruobing Huang, Dong Ni
- query-set: cs.AI
- abs: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.03109

Accurate preoperative prediction of lung adenocarcinoma invasiveness is important for surgical planning and individualized treatment. However, overlapping CT appearances make it challenging to distinguish invasive adenocarcinoma (IAD) from Pre-IAD lesions. We propose a multimodal prompt-learning framework with dual-granularity image-text alignment for 3D CT-based IAD classification. A 3D ResNet-CBAM encoder extracts global and spatial visual features. A frozen RadBERT backbone is adapted with separate learnable prompts for report-level and keyword-level text encoding. These prompts are initialized using semantic representations derived from real-world clinical reports. Global contrastive alignment connects whole-volume CT representations with reports generated from clinician-annotated radiological attributes to provide overall semantic supervision. A multiple instance learning-based local alignment module associates spatial CT features with vascular-focused radiological keywords, to capture local imaging characteristics potentially associated with tumor invasion. The framework jointly optimizes classification and alignment objectives, while inference requires only CT images. Experiments included 1,593 pathologically confirmed nodules. On the test set, the proposed method achieved an accuracy of 87.92%, F1-score of 87.78%, and AUC of 0.9499. Its AUC significantly exceeded that of the image-only Res-CBAM baseline with the same visual backbone (0.9297; DeLong test, p = 0.0078). Ablation analyses supported the complementary contributions of global and local alignment and the benefit of report-based prompt initialization. Qualitative visualizations showed activation within nodules and adjacent vascular structures. These findings suggest that dual-granularity radiological supervision can enhance CT-based invasiveness prediction while preserving a CT-only inference workflow.


### arXiv 2611.03100 - LUCA: A Synthetic World for Experimental Reasoning

- categories: cs.AI
- published: 2026-11-02T13:17:36Z
- authors: Anna Gogleva, Thomas Charman, Martin Balla, Karl Gemayel
- query-set: cs.AI
- abs: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.03100

An experimental agent can succeed for the wrong reasons. Evaluations, however, often focus on the outcome, not the experiments, evidence, and inferences that produced it. We introduce LUCA, a configurable synthetic world for evaluating this process under fully known ground truth. Agents learn unknown regulatory systems through budgeted experimentation, then predict unseen interventions, diagnose hidden faults, and attempt rescues. This setup allows us to evaluate experiment selection, prediction, diagnosis, and intervention separately. Across seven frontier LLMs and three baselines, these capabilities dissociate sharply. Agents frequently rescue malfunctioning systems while diagnosing the wrong fault; diagnosis varies 7x across models while prediction varies only 1.4x. Models also adopt surprisingly different experimental strategies, and greater exploration does not reliably yield better performance. In one failure mode, agents often perform the experiment needed to distinguish competing causes, but still fail to use the resulting evidence. LUCA turns these hidden failures into measurable behaviours, providing a controlled environment to study where experimental agents succeed and fail. Code: https://github.com/RelationRx/luca.


### arXiv 2611.03094 - A Fine-Grained Hierarchy and Benchmark for State Tracking in Language Models

- categories: cs.AI
- published: 2026-11-02T13:12:15Z
- authors: Jackson Petty, William Merrill, Ashish Sabharwal
- query-set: cs.AI
- abs: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.03094

Theoretical limitations of transformers on state tracking---the ability to track information about entities or variables over long contexts---have motivated new recurrent language modeling (LM) architectures such as Gated DeltaNet (GDN). These architectures have, in particular, been benchmarked against the $S_5$ word problem, a theoretically hard state-tracking task that captures abilities such as code evaluation under arbitrary variable assignment. It, however, remains open whether other, simpler forms of state tracking might both be more relevant in practice and already explain observed differences between these architectures. We propose evaluating state tracking using a hierarchy of word problems with four increasing levels of difficulty: r-trivial, corresponding to immutable assignment to variables in programming; aperiodic, where assignments are mutable; soluble, where assignments are self-referential; and general finite state tracking. Theoretically, we show that the class of problems transformers are thought to length-generalize on does not even contain all aperiodic state tracking. On the other hand, we prove that GDN with positive eigenvalues expresses all aperiodic problems, and the regular languages recognized by certain transformers are precisely all soluble ones, thus providing a fine-grained picture of state tracking capabilities of various architectures. Taking these ideas to practice, we construct StateBench, a 4-tier long-horizon state-tracking benchmark. Our systematic direct training experiments across architectures largely align with theoretical expectation, albeit with interesting observations at very large scale length-generalization. Additionally, we also evaluate pretrained models---both open hybrid and closed frontier ones---on StateBench, finding that none of them robustly solves even soluble state tracking, suggesting a direction for future improvement.


### arXiv 2611.03093 - An Experimental Study of Hybrid Retrieval, NLI and Open-source LLMs for Scientific Claim Verification

- categories: cs.CL, cs.AI, cs.LG
- published: 2026-11-02T13:12:13Z
- authors: Lakshmi Narayanan P, Narenkarthik K, Naren Kumar C
- query-set: cs.AI
- abs: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.03093

Claim verification is the task of deciding whether a source text supports a claim, contradicts it, or lacks sufficient evidence. Natural Language Inference (NLI) models can judge whether a single sentence supports or contradicts a claim, but they struggle when the evidence is spread across several sentences. Conversely, Large Language Models (LLMs) can reason across sentences, but may draw conclusions the text does not support. In this experimental study, we test whether a conservative fusion rule, labeling a claim Support or Contradict only when both models agree and defaulting to Not Enough Evidence otherwise, combines their strengths. We construct a benchmark of 2,035 claims from SciFact and CliniFact (858 Support, 152 Contradict, 1,025 Not Enough Evidence). We retrieve the top five most relevant sentences per claim using a hybrid of semantic and lexical (BM25) retrieval, which slightly outperforms either method alone. These retrieved sentences are then provided to every verification model. We then compare a DeBERTa-v3-base-mnli-fever-anli NLI baseline against Llama 3.1 8B, Qwen3 8B, and Mistral 7B. Qwen3 achieves the highest Macro-F1 (0.7937), followed by Llama 3.1 (0.7204) and Mistral (0.6388), while the NLI baseline reaches only 0.3157. Contrary to our hypothesis, fusion degrades performance, dropping Macro-F1 to 0.7392, 0.6730, and 0.5991, respectively. To understand this decline, we analyze agreements, disagreements, and common error patterns between the NLI and LLM verifiers. Our error analysis shows that NLI and LLM disagreements often result in correct LLM predictions being overturned.


### arXiv 2611.03092 - Avatar Intelligence: Amplifying Human Agency Through Persistent Personal Agents

- categories: cs.AI, cs.CL
- published: 2026-11-02T13:11:57Z
- authors: Zheng Wu, Xinbei Ma, Yao Yao, Zhaoyuan Huang, Haowen Hu, Yijie Lu
- query-set: cs.AI
- abs: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.03092

Personal AI agents are increasingly capable of handling long-horizon tasks across applications, yet users often need to invest substantial effort in specifying goals, resolving ambiguity, and verifying outcomes. We argue that progress should be measured by how effectively agents amplify human agency: enabling people to pursue their goals with less routine supervision while retaining control over consequential decisions. We advocate Avatar Intelligence, a paradigm of persistent personal agents that represent users' goals, values, and constraints through sustained decision delegation across contexts and time. We describe a maturity roadmap from human-directed execution to autonomous operation within delegated domains, and introduce the Agentic Oversight Index (AOI) as a framework for evaluating delivered value against agent-side operational cost and human oversight burden. We identify three core research challenges: identity-continuous personalization that carries user preferences across tasks, user-situated evolution through Rehearsal Engineering, and safety mechanisms that combine technical control with accountability and recourse. These directions define a research agenda for reliable decision delegation to personal agents across tasks and over time.


### arXiv 2611.03087 - MIRA: Reinforcing Multimodal Reasoning via Deceptive Contextual Augmentation

- categories: cs.CV, cs.AI
- published: 2026-11-02T13:09:45Z
- authors: Zhihan Yin, Jianxin Liang, Yifeng Yao, Nonghai Zhang, and Bingyue Peng, Chunguang Qie
- query-set: cs.AI
- abs: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.03087

While Reinforcement Learning with Verifiable Rewards (RLVR) has significantly advanced multimodal reasoning, existing frameworks suffer from a critical "perception-reasoning gap." Due to an over-reliance on seemingly relevant visual tokens, even minor perceptual perturbations can propagate through the reasoning process, leading to compounding hallucinations. To address this issue, we propose MIRA, a training framework that improves robustness to erroneous visual contexts while enhancing logical reasoning ability. By injecting filtered, deceptive visual contexts during training, we construct challenging "hard traps" that stress-test the model's robustness to misleading information. Furthermore, we introduce a group-wise reflection-triggering mechanism that enables the model to autonomously detect and rectify inconsistencies between visual cues and logical constraints, which is ultimately internalized as an intrinsic policy behavior. Importantly, MIRA does not rely on human annotations. Experimental results across multiple reasoning and perception benchmarks show that MIRA yields an average 7% improvement on in-domain benchmarks, with consistent improvements on vision-centric benchmarks. These findings highlight that active error recovery is essential for robust multimodal reasoning. Comprehensive ablation studies and analyses further provide insights into why MIRA is effective.


### arXiv 2611.03068 - PACERec: Progressive Adaptation of LLM Item Embeddings with Collaborative Evidence for Recommendation

- categories: cs.IR, cs.AI
- published: 2026-11-02T12:55:49Z
- authors: Junzhao Huang, Xi Wu, Zihan Wang, Zijing Wang, Ming Wang, Kang Liu
- query-set: cs.AI
- abs: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.03068

Large language model (LLM) embeddings have been increasingly adopted in sequential recommendation to encode static item attributes and alleviate the limitations of item ID embeddings under sparse interactions. However, these embeddings typically capture only static content similarity while neglecting sequential transitions, and since their adaptation quality relies on sparse per-item updates or global geometric calibration, existing methods still struggle to explicitly transfer preceding interaction contexts from frequent to long-tail items under unevenly distributed behavioral evidence, limiting recommendation effectiveness across the catalog. To address this challenge, we propose PACERec, an ID-free progressive adaptation framework that incorporates preceding interaction contexts into semantic representation adaptation by learning a shared velocity field from items with sufficient interactions via frequency-weighted flow matching. In this way, PACERec progressively guides static LLM embeddings toward collaborative behavioral targets while preserving original item semantics, overcoming sparse per-item updates to effectively generalize sequential transition patterns to long-tail items for both user modeling and candidate scoring. Extensive experiments on three real-world datasets across three sequential backbones demonstrate the superiority of our method over competitive baselines in both overall and long-tail recommendation.


### arXiv 2611.03066 - Towards a Trustworthy and Governable Internet of Agentics: An Architectural Perspective on Protocols, Systems, and Services

- categories: cs.AI, cs.DC
- published: 2026-11-02T12:54:14Z
- authors: Haoyi Xiong, Yi Liu, Jiang Bian, Peng Diao, Bin Zhu, Tingting Hu
- query-set: cs.AI
- abs: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.03066

Large language model applications are increasingly being organized as tool-using and multi-agent workflows rather than as isolated chat interfaces. This perspective paper examines the architectural problem that follows from that shift: agents may reason, invoke tools, and delegate work, but dependable deployment still requires interoperability across frameworks, runtimes, data boundaries, and organizational control domains. We study this problem through the lens of the \emph{Internet of Agentics} (IoA), an emerging service-computing view in which heterogeneous agents are treated as governable service participants rather than only as stand-alone model endpoints. The paper organizes the landscape as a three-layer progression: from today's fragmented agent ecosystem, to interoperable agents, and ultimately to trustworthy and governable autonomy. First, it examines the protocol stack centered on the Agentic AI Foundation, including MCP for agent--tool interaction; A2A for agent--agent coordination; AGENTS.md and Skills-style procedural manifests; and discovery, transport, and trust mechanisms such as AGNTCY and SLIM. Second, it reviews the systems abstractions that make agents deployable as service components, including logical agent architectures, orchestration patterns, gateways, registries, runtime control loops, and hardware-aware inference infrastructure. Third, it situates these developments within services computing, arguing that agentic systems reinterpret classical concerns from SOA and microservices---discovery, composition, governance, pricing, and trust---at the level of cognitive capabilities rather than deterministic functions.


### arXiv 2611.03063 - Networked AI: Architecture, Theory, and Applications

- categories: cs.AI, cs.NI
- published: 2026-11-02T12:52:41Z
- authors: Liang Song, Jiayue Jin, Peng Sun, Di Li, Yang Liu, Athanassios Skodras
- query-set: cs.AI
- abs: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.03063

Recent advances in Artificial Intelligence (AI), particularly large-scale deep learning, have been driven by monolithic or offline training on massive curated datasets. While successful, these approaches face structural limitations: escalating data and computational demands, limited online adaptability, and a lack of system-level intelligence in open, non-stationary environments. This paper presents Networked AI - a networked intelligent system composed of interacting, heterogeneous, and continuously evolving AI agents. We establish a hierarchical layered architecture comprising an Agent Layer for heterogeneous capability construction, an Interaction Layer for signal-processing-guided online data extraction, and a Regulation Layer for stability-constrained collective evolution. Foundations in data science, signal processing, and system control are hereby synthesized into a unified theoretical framework of Networked AI, providing superior data efficiency, intelligence efficiency, interpretability, and scalability. Theoretical and experimental analysis demonstrates that Networked AI can approach near-linear scaling in resource demand, in contrast to the exponential increase in standalone end-to-end systems. It provides not only online adaptation to out-of-distribution environmental novelties, but also the autonomous evolution towards application-specific intelligence. Application frontiers are discussed for IoT spaces, embodied, spatial, and industrial intelligences, as well as the cellular infrastructure and beyond. Key open challenges are identified towards realizing locally autonomous, collectively intelligent, continuously evolving, globally regulated Networked AI.


### arXiv 2611.03055 - PRIME: Predictive Routing of Interventions by Modeling Effects for Scientific Ideation

- categories: cs.AI
- published: 2026-11-02T12:49:33Z
- authors: Fengxian Ji, Xie Yu, Zhexuan Cui, Yuke Li, Zirui Song, Fan Zhang
- query-set: cs.AI
- abs: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.03055

Feedback-driven iteration is widely used to improve LLM-generated scientific ideas, but existing systems either leave revision decisions implicit in generation, align revisions with feedback-localized weaknesses, or restrict action selection to system-specific operations. This is problematic because problems, hypotheses, and solutions are interdependent: the location where a weakness manifests does not itself determine the revision scope that will produce a better successor. We term this phenomenon diagnosis-intervention mismatch. We introduce Predictive Routing of Interventions by Modeling Effects (PRIME), a plug-and-play feedback-to-revision decision layer for existing scientific ideators. PRIME operates in three stages: (1) Structural Attribution interprets feedback over the problem-hypothesis-solution structure without fixing a revision target; (2) Counterfactual Routing constructs alternative scope-conditioned interventions and compares their projected successor utility and risks through lightweight rollouts; and (3) Intervention Execution compiles the selected intervention into an ordered generator-facing plan with semantic actions, preservation constraints, and dependency requirements for successor generation. We validate diagnosis intervention mismatch on 112 human-annotated idea-feedback pairs. Across four benchmarks, two ideators, and fixed- and open-problem settings, PRIME improves successor quality and routing reliability over strong baselines; removing counterfactual routing reduces quality from 3.463 to 1.960 and increases route regret from 0.426 to 1.929, confirming cross-scope effect modeling as the primary source of its gains.


### arXiv 2611.03051 - Unlocking Criminal Court Decisions: Flexible Information Extraction for Empirical Legal Research

- categories: cs.CL, cs.AI
- published: 2026-11-02T12:46:53Z
- authors: Tomáš Knap, Ivana Kvapilíková, Jan Černý, Klára Bendová, Jaromír Šavelka, Vojtěch Pour
- query-set: cs.AI
- abs: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.03051

Criminal verdicts contain descriptions of criminal behavior that can support quantitative legal research, but structuring them requires attributes and extraction rules that cannot always be fully specified in advance. We present an LLM-based pipeline that builds a structured dataset through attribute exploration, text span extraction, and value normalization, guided by annotation instructions that legal experts can refine. We evaluate it on two traffic offenses: driving under the influence (DUI) and driving despite a ban (DDB). For DUI, revising the instructions was associated with higher extraction accuracy (from 52% to 70% of correctly extracted values) and higher agreement between human annotators. Repeated runs of the same model agreed with each other more than with the gold annotations, showing that consistent output is not necessarily correct, while disagreement between annotators and between models helped reveal unclear instructions. For DDB, where the instructions defined fixed categories and output formats, agreement was high for most attributes.


### arXiv 2611.03043 - VideoEditor-MoE: Scaling Complex Video Editing with Mixture of Experts

- categories: cs.CV, cs.AI
- published: 2026-11-02T12:42:51Z
- authors: Xiao Cao, Fuchen Long, Yansong Qu, Wen Xiao, Xin Zhang, Xinlong Sun
- query-set: cs.AI
- abs: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.03043

Complex video editing requires understanding complex instructions while jointly fulfilling multiple editing requests. Meeting these demands through dense model scaling substantially increases computational cost. We introduce VideoEditor-MoE, the first open-source mixture-of-experts framework for video editing. Beyond sparse MLP-MoE layers, MoE-Attention expands attention capacity through routed key--value experts while preserving global interactions among video tokens. Initialized from a 5B video editing model, VideoEditor-MoE scales to 14B and 56B parameters, with approximately 8B parameters activated per token in both variants. Comparisons with existing methods and two complementary baselines, Explicit-MoE and MLP-MoE, assess its benefits over explicit expert guidance and MLP-only expert architectures. Further analyses demonstrate that all experts remain active and reveal that learned routing responds more strongly to sample content and diffusion noise levels than to human-defined editing categories. Spatial analysis further indicates sensitivity to object-level semantic features. We also identify decoupled weight decay as a strategy for stabilizing expert scaling.


### arXiv 2611.03015 - Comparative study of global pooling for pre-departure ship fuel consumption prediction: Advantages over per-ship and adaptation-based approaches

- categories: cs.LG, cs.AI
- published: 2026-11-02T12:14:49Z
- authors: Heungjun Im, Bongmin Cha, Agung Nugraha, Donghyun Kim, Seongphil Kang, Minwook Choi
- query-set: cs.AI
- abs: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.03015

Most fuel oil consumption (FOC) models for voyage planning are per-ship models and thus generalize poorly beyond each vessel's records. Transfer and federated learning have been reported to perform better, but under different protocols that often use inputs unavailable before departure or random data splits. It therefore remains unclear which approach performs best. In this study, we compare 11 per-ship, adaptation-based, and global methods on 21 vessels. To prevent leakage, all methods receive identical inputs known or forecast before departure, excluding engine RPM and shaft power, and are tested on intact voyage legs after all training records of each vessel. Global pooling, in which one model describes each vessel by its ship identity and seven ship specifications, performs best overall in every setting examined. For in-fleet vessels, Global LightGBM reaches an overall MAPE of 8.92%, against 11.14% for Per-Ship LightGBM, and outperforms every per-ship and adaptation-based model. Global models of five architectures, including a Transformer, all outperform the per-ship and federated models. Fine-tuning a global model for each vessel, even by low-rank adaptation of the Transformer, adds no accuracy. Without any records of a vessel, the global model reaches a median MAPE of 11.20% and outperforms a per-ship model trained on that vessel's records for 14 of 21 vessels. Adding its first records to the pooled data is as accurate as vessel-specific adaptation. Ship identity and specifications are largely interchangeable for in-fleet vessels, but only the specifications support zero-shot prediction. Its error grows as a vessel's specifications differ from the fleet's. Per-ship models and vessel-specific adaptation are therefore unnecessary under fair comparison. One global model can serve a whole fleet, including newly delivered vessels with similar specifications.


### arXiv 2611.03007 - Q-CamTuning: A Multimodal Large Language Model for Fine-Grained Quality Reasoning in Livestreaming Camera Tuning

- categories: cs.CV, cs.AI
- published: 2026-11-02T12:10:15Z
- authors: Yanchao Bi, Pengfei Chen, Yulong Yan, Qian Zhang, Biao Wang, Leida Li
- query-set: cs.AI
- abs: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.03007

With the popularization of livestreaming applications, automatic camera tuning has become indispensable for ordinary users to enjoy high Quality of Experience (QoE). However, the existing Image Quality Assessment (IQA) methods face two challenges in camera tuning. First, they struggle to identify subtle quality difference between images, which is common in camera tuning. Second, the ability to explain fine-grained differences and to provide suggestions for camera tuning are even more lacking. To address these challenges, we first construct FGLive-Comments, an instruction tuning dataset with rich annotations of both preferences for fine-grained image pairs and quality reasoning from global and local visual evidence, which supports selective adjustment recommendations of camera parameters. Based on FGLive-Comments, we develop Q-CamTuning, a multimodal large language model for livestreaming camera tuning. Its progressive perception-evidence-guidance framework learns quality-aware visual representations, enhances fine-grained reasoning with global-local evidence, and maps quality issues to camera-parameter adjustment directions. Extensive experimental results demonstrate that Q-CamTuning outperforms existing IQA models in both quality scoring and fine-grained image pair ranking. User study also validates its ability to recommend camera tuning suggestions.


### arXiv 2611.03005 - BLPO: Beyond-Local Policy Optimization for Long-Horizon LLM Agents

- categories: cs.AI
- published: 2026-11-02T12:10:05Z
- authors: Xuanren Chen, Chongyang Tao, Wenpeng Hu, Yong Wang, Bo Deng, Xinlei Zheng
- query-set: cs.AI
- abs: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.03005

Long-horizon tasks require large language model (LLM) agents to coordinate interdependent actions, a capability central to automating complex workflows. Stepwise group-based reinforcement learning provides finer credit than trajectory-level supervision, but reliable comparisons must account for differences in interaction history. Enforcing this consistency within the limited rollouts of each task can leave insufficient support for estimating action preferences. We propose Beyond-Local Policy Optimization (BLPO), which estimates task-progress advantages from experience shared across tasks and training updates. We summarize trajectory prefixes into task-progress states and abstract actions by their function, allowing corresponding decisions from different tasks to contribute to shared estimates. To control bias and noise from heterogeneous tasks, we accumulate context-relative returns in a global memory with balanced aggregation and reliability weighting. These global advantages complement local comparisons over normalized actions and trajectory-level credit without a learned critic or additional rollouts. Experiments on ALFWorld and WebShop with 1.5B and 7B policies show improved task success over the evaluated baselines under matched training budgets. Further analyses show faster learning and shorter trajectories with little computational overhead.


### arXiv 2611.02978 - CAREFUL-Bench: Effect-Aware, Judge-Free Evaluation of Multi-Turn LLM Function Calling

- categories: cs.AI, cs.SE
- published: 2026-11-02T11:47:05Z
- authors: Alexander Kovalenko, Artem Redchych
- query-set: cs.AI
- abs: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.02978

The multi-turn checker of the Berkeley Function Calling Leaderboard (BFCL) evaluates final state correctness but ignores the effects of intermediate calls: extra calls are free, function documentation does not describe side effects, and the scripted user never responds to proposals. Consequently, models asking for confirmation before destructive actions are penalized; 260 of 734 reference turns in the base category perform destructive actions without confirmation. We make BFCL effect-aware without replacing it. We infer effect declarations for all 128 functions by executing them in BFCL simulators and comparing state changes, with destructive writes identified by three annotators. A judge-free replay scorer reproduces official results across all 800 reference trajectories while reporting unrequested destructive calls, duplicate writes, redundant reads, surplus steps, and their token costs. We also introduce a confirmation-aware protocol with a deterministic simulated user. Across 11 open models, effect-aware scoring preserves the official ranking but reveals substantial differences in execution quality: the leading model loses 14.0 points due to unrequested destructive calls, while surplus steps consume up to 28% of input tokens. Adding effect hints to function documentation has no impact on eight evaluated models. Allowing the simulated user to respond recovers up to four points for models that seek confirmation. However, the best model completes only six of 576 conversations requiring destructive actions with explicit confirmation. These results expose a key limitation of current function-calling benchmarks: they cannot distinguish between asking for clarification and obtaining permission before potentially destructive actions.


### arXiv 2611.02956 - Specification-Aware Predictive Uncertainty Decomposition

- categories: stat.ML, cs.AI, cs.CL, cs.LG
- published: 2026-11-02T11:23:22Z
- authors: Roman Vashurin, Mikhail Seleznyov, Assel Yermekova, Fedor Chernogorskii, Danila Zykov, Elena Tutubalina
- query-set: cs.AI
- abs: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.02956

Classical decompositions of predictive uncertainty, such as the information-theoretic one, are defined for a fixed representation $x$ of the input object. They do not consider that $x$ may be incomplete, nor do they measure how much the missing information can affect the prediction. In Large Language Models, users can clarify an underspecified input without any retraining. The clarified input can describe the user's unobserved intent $z^*$ more accurately and change the predictive uncertainty about the outcome. In this paper, we formalize this setting probabilistically and derive a three-term decomposition of prediction risk into specification, residual aleatoric, and epistemic uncertainties. We show that at the original information level, when only $x$ is available, the aleatoric uncertainty, represented by a Bayes risk, has two components. The first, which we call specification uncertainty, is the expected reduction in this Bayes risk from revealing the intent. The second is the residual aleatoric uncertainty, i.e., the Bayes risk that remains once the intent is known, averaged over its possible values $z$ given $x$. The distinction is important, as specification uncertainty informs the decision to request clarification before making a prediction. We build approximations of these components and connect them to existing uncertainty measures. Our experiments across different domains and modalities show distinct roles for the three components. Specification uncertainty is the most informative for detecting underspecified inputs, while residual aleatoric uncertainty is the most informative for detecting in-distribution prediction errors.


### arXiv 2611.02952 - Balancing Representations by Identifying and Generating Under-represented Data

- categories: cs.CV, cs.AI
- published: 2026-11-02T11:17:31Z
- authors: Filipe Laitenberger, Hirokatsu Kataoka, Yuki Asano, Cees Snoek, Mohammadreza Salehi
- query-set: cs.AI
- abs: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.02952

Self-supervised learning inherits the coverage of its source data. When an unlabeled corpus is long-tailed, the representation becomes dense around frequent modes and thin where coverage is weak. Objectives for long-tailed SSL reweight the images already in the corpus and add no new ones. We introduce BRIDGE (Balancing Representations by Identifying and Generating Under-represented Data), a label-free feedback loop around the source set. The current encoder scores every source image by its mean kNN distance, and a budgeted acquisition rule selects images from a band of that score distribution where support is thin and the generator still produces faithful variants. A frozen image-to-image diffusion model then adds local variants of the selected anchors, and pretraining continues on the enlarged source set. We formulate acquisition as recoverability-weighted allocation of local support and derive when a finite budget should favor a sparse band that excludes the extreme tail and when reallocation after each repair is optimal under a fixed objective. Across five long-tailed, web-scraped and synthetic source regimes, BRIDGE has the highest seven-task linear-probe and kNN average of the compared methods, and paired experiments extend the gain to ViT-S under contrastive and self-distillation objectives, to low-label fine-tuning and to semantic segmentation. The linear-probe gain is smaller under masked reconstruction. Generated repair matches an oracle that restores the withheld real images, and under a frozen encoder the inserted images shrink the neighborhoods of the selected anchors about twice as much as those of matched unselected images. BRIDGE needs no labels and leaves the pretraining objective unchanged, so it can be added to pretraining on uncurated web data, where the encoder decides which images to generate.


### arXiv 2611.02950 - Action Verification through Conjecture and Refutation for Language Model Agents

- categories: cs.AI, cs.CL, cs.LG
- published: 2026-11-02T11:15:40Z
- authors: Minhyeon Oh, Chiyeong Heo, Geonwoo Noh, Hoyoung Kim, Jangwon Son, Jungseul Ok
- query-set: cs.AI
- abs: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.02950

Language model agents increasingly act on users' behalf by manipulating external environments through tools, often from high-level instructions that leave critical task- and environment-specific context unstated. To prevent resulting misaligned actions, recent work verifies proposed actions before execution, largely by establishing whether the conditions supporting them hold. We argue that this confirmation-oriented view leaves a fundamental blind spot: a wrong action often passes every check that asks whether it is right. This motivates a different question for verification: rather than asking what evidence supports an action, can the agent find evidence that refutes it? We pursue this direction with BlackSwan, a refutation-oriented, expect-suspect-test procedure that predicts the intended effect of each state-mutating action, formulates specific and empirically testable hypotheses for how it could be wrong, and tests them using existing observations or additional non-mutating actions, executing the action only if none survives testing. On challenging AppWorld tasks, BlackSwan improves success rate over the strongest baseline by 14.9 and 25.2%p with and without the backbone model's thinking mode, respectively. Even without thinking or in-context demonstrations, it outperforms stronger baselines equipped with them. The gains also transfer beyond AppWorld, with BlackSwan improving WebShop performance by 12.7%p. More broadly, our results suggest that language model agents have an underused capacity to recognize and self-correct misaligned actions, which can be systematically elicited through active conjecture and refutation before execution.


### arXiv 2611.02940 - PlagiaCorpus: A Bilingual, Adversarial, and Cross-Lingual Dataset for Forensic Plagiarism Detection

- categories: cs.CL, cs.AI, cs.CY, cs.DL, cs.IR
- published: 2026-11-02T11:08:11Z
- authors: Adel Ammar, Serry Sibaee, Wadii Boulila
- query-set: cs.AI
- abs: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.02940

PlagiaCorpus is a bilingual Arabic-English dataset for benchmarking plagiarism and text-reuse detection, including reuse produced by large language models (LLMs) and reuse across languages. It contains 198,643 text records: a source corpus of 34,885 passages drawn from English and Arabic Wikipedia and two openly licensed book collections, and 163,758 queries (147,381 for training and 16,377 for testing). The queries cover five reuse classes of increasing difficulty, namely verbatim copying, character-level obfuscation, same-language paraphrasing by two different LLMs, and English--Arabic translation with structural paraphrasing, together with a Safe class of non-reused passages. Every positive query is linked to its source passage, and the train/test split is grouped by source passage so that no passage contributes queries to both splits. The release was curated through language identification, removal of LLM generation artefacts, and exact and cross-lingual de-duplication. Technical validation reports per-class length statistics, automatic checks of the train/test split, and inter-annotator agreement on a manually rated sample. The dataset is distributed in JSON and Parquet formats under a CC BY-SA 4.0 license.


### arXiv 2611.02936 - Why Tabular Foundation Models Excel on Relational Data They Have Never Seen

- categories: cs.AI, cs.DB, cs.LG
- published: 2026-11-02T11:02:57Z
- authors: Linjie Xu, Yiqun Hu, Jixiang He, David Wipf
- query-set: cs.AI
- abs: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.02936

Given a relational database (RDB) storing heterogeneous tabular information, how can we predict missing (or future) values in some target column of interest? As candidate targets are numerous in enterprise settings, it is preferable to avoid learning a new model from scratch each time there is a new prediction task. Frozen foundation models (FMs) based on in-context learning (ICL) and RDB-specific pre-training provide a viable solution, but the ideal design still remains largely unresolved. Perhaps unexpectedly, preliminary evidence has recently shown that existing single-table FMs (aka TFMs), when combined with suitable parameter-free relational encoders, can be quite competitive in practice despite zero exposure to RDB pre-training or relational data of any kind. In this work we first empirically confirm this phenomenon with a more diverse array of experiments. We then turn to our main contribution, namely, surfacing multiple complementary explanations for why TFMs behave so effectively. These explanations ultimately hinge on innate synergies whereby parameter-free encoders map relational effects to fixed-length embeddings that loosely mimic distributions for which TFMs were originally designed; in so doing, desirable single-table properties transfer to relational settings (e.g., convergent posterior predictive distributions). Collectively, our analysis forges new pathways for improving RDB FMs, particularly when we prioritize interpretability and inevitable integration within agentic systems.


### arXiv 2611.02931 - Training-Time Domain Transfer for Generator-Free Diagnostics on Novel Modalities in Computational Pathology

- categories: cs.CV, cs.AI
- published: 2026-11-02T10:59:26Z
- authors: Julia A. Mielcarz, Andrea Camilloni, Nita Mulliqi, Jessica A. Frank, Anders Blilie, Emilius A. M. Janssen
- query-set: cs.AI
- abs: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.02931

Novel imaging modalities promise improved diagnostics, but AI development is constrained by scarce training data. Inference-time domain transfer can address this by mapping novel modalities to established ones, enabling reuse of existing diagnostic models. However, this introduces hallucination risks, computational overhead, and regulatory complexity. Inspired by sim-to-real transfer, we propose training-time domain transfer, generating synthetic target-domain training data from archived routine samples to enable direct diagnosis on real inputs without inference-time generation. Using unstained prostate histopathology as a proof-of-concept, we compare this approach with inference-time virtual staining and conventional H&E-based cancer detection. Training-time transfer achieved higher concordance with the conventional benchmark than virtual staining for both raw (Cohen's $κ$=0.59 vs. 0.37; Gwet's AC1=0.73 vs. 0.29) and deparaffinized tissue ($κ$=0.66 vs. 0.61; AC1=0.73 vs. 0.64), supporting its potential for emerging diagnostic modalities.


### arXiv 2611.02916 - Defining Autonomous Weapon Systems: A schematic framework for refined purpose-driven definitions

- categories: cs.CY, cs.AI, cs.ET, cs.HC, cs.RO
- published: 2026-11-02T10:48:36Z
- authors: Björn Lundgren, Nathan G. Wood
- query-set: cs.AI
- abs: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.02916

The literature on autonomous weapon systems (AWS) includes many proposed definitions for these (types of) systems, with core disagreements between scholars, governments, and international regulatory bodies. In this article, we present and critique prevailing (classes of) definitions, arguing that what is needed is not a singular definition, but rather a definitional framework which allows one to classify different sorts of AWS within the same conceptual family. Recognizing we cannot foresee all future AWS, we show how variations on a broadly accepted definition can be used to produce a definitional schema which can be easily modified in order to classify various concepts belonging to the class of AWS.


### arXiv 2611.02908 - User Attributes and Political Ideology Shape LLM Receptiveness in Online Political Debates

- categories: cs.AI, cs.CL
- published: 2026-11-02T10:40:22Z
- authors: Akram Elbouanani, Yousra Taleb, Gianluca Demartini, Adrian Popescu
- query-set: cs.AI
- abs: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.02908

Online political discussions increasingly involve AI-mediated participation, as users invoke LLMs to assess contested claims, arbitrate disputes, and respond to opposing viewpoints. As these systems become participants in online exchanges, they may alter which speakers and argumentative strategies receive validation within the discussion. We study this interactional dimension by reconstructing 5,376 Debate.org debates as LLM conversations and running nearly 150,000 controlled multi-turn conversations that vary user political orientation, expertise, rhetorical strategy, and defended position, including conversations with competing users. Across naturalistic and controlled debates, we find that LLMs are systematically more receptive to left-leaning than right-leaning users. The asymmetry persists when users with different political orientations defend the same position and when opposing users appear in the same conversation, indicating that it is not fully explained by stance agreement or simple accommodation to the current speaker. Repeated interaction increases receptiveness without eliminating this ordering; expertise also matters, while rhetorical differences are smaller. The pattern appears across multiple models, but is not fixed: fine-tuning on different political corpora can largely erase or reverse it. These results show that LLMs can respond systematically differently to participants in the same political debate, making such interactional asymmetries important for understanding their emerging role in online public discourse.


### arXiv 2611.02905 - What makes a theorem interesting? Operationalizing interestingness for open-ended exploration

- categories: cs.AI
- published: 2026-11-02T10:38:23Z
- authors: Silvère Gangloff
- query-set: cs.AI
- abs: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.02905

An agent that explores, whether it generates conjectures or research ideas, must decide which of them are worth keeping. This judgment of interestingness is usually delegated to a hand-picked proxy or to a language model, neither of which is known to correspond to what humans actually value. I study it for mathematical theorems, by observing which results mathematicians single out in formal libraries and in papers. I model each way of singling out results as an evaluator that weights features of a result's statement, its proof and dependencies, and split its weights into a shared and a subjective part. In formalized mathematics, ex-ante features predict these selections as well as a large language-model judge, and the two complement each other. Interestingness is also largely shared. One set of weights, carried mostly by structural features such as the length of the statement and of the proof and the subfields the proof draws on, accounts for most of the signal and transfers in part across formal libraries. What remains subjective depends on practices and individual authors more than on subfields. The judge's advantage lies in its knowledge of the canon, and neither the judge nor the features rely on memory of the papers. The approach, which infers what a community finds interesting from what it singles out, can be applied to other sciences.


### arXiv 2611.02903 - Maswao: An automatically collected, prepared, and benchmarked dataset for the South African Sign language

- categories: cs.CL, cs.AI
- published: 2026-11-02T10:36:22Z
- authors: Thapelo Sindane, Unarine Netshifhefhe, Vukosi Marivate, Abiodun Modupe, Herkulaas Combrink, Ketro Sithole
- query-set: cs.AI
- abs: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.02903

Recent advancements in sign language processing can be attributed to developments in both large-scale dataset collection and algorithmic complexity in areas such as pose estimation techniques, signal processing through Transformer models, and model optimization. However, only a handful of sign languages, such as the American, British, and German, from over 200 globally recognized sign languages, reap the benefits of such advancements, while the rest lag behind. In this study, we aim to address this technological disparity by proposing a framework for the automatic collection and preparation of sign language datasets, with very minimal human-labour intervention, using the South African Sign Language (SASL) as a testbed. Specifically, we automatically collect SASL video content using public resources such as news broadcasts. Additionally, we convert their accompanying audio streams into text using available Automatic Speech Recognition (ASR) tools. Furthermore, we crop the sign language area in the videos and use gradient descent to locate valleys of non-motion to determine end-of-sentence points within long videos to create our weakly sentence-aligned parallel corpora -- the Maswao (Signs) dataset. Finally, we benchmark the weakly aligned dataset on the task of Sign-to-text Translation using Recurrent Neural Networks and Transformer models to compare their individual intricacies. Our results show that Transformer models proposed in the literature outperform recurrent baselines, with the best-performing Transformer achieving BLEU-1 to BLEU-4 scores of 13.78, 5.21, 2.52, and 1.54, respectively, as well as ChrF = 18.09 and ROUGE-L = 10.51. These results indicate that the task remains promising and warrants further investigations.


### arXiv 2611.02896 - Bridging Low-Level Image Fusion and High-Level Semantic Understanding

- categories: cs.AI, cs.IR
- published: 2026-11-02T10:29:41Z
- authors: Guangyuan Dong, Lingdong Shen, Xudong Zhang, Haodong Jing, Haoyu Wang, Zihao Li
- query-set: cs.AI
- abs: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.02896

Remote sensing image fusion and semantic interpretation are usually optimized separately, so reconstruction quality need not preserve downstream retrieval utility. We propose LANCE, which uses structured language as an explicit, indexable semantic state. A frozen vision-language model maps the input to a closed-vocabulary text record; a language-conditioned generator fuses the imagery; and a dense verifier with a differentiable continuous summarizer re-describes the output. Text-text consistency and anti-collapse contrastive text-mask losses with negative queries close the loop without dense annotations or VLM fine-tuning. A variational EM formulation yields a per-step contraction result under regularity assumptions, distinguishing semantic faithfulness from verifier overfitting. Across seven test sets on GF2, QuickBird, WorldView-III, and WorldView-II, LANCE improves reduced-scale reference metrics by up to 11.7% (median 2.1%) over the strongest learning baseline. Independent evaluation using a held-out VLM and a supervised aerial detector yields gains of +6.4 mAP and +0.072 open-vocabulary F1 over the closest competitor. Text perturbations, cross-verifier transfer, and pseudo-RGB ablations examine the mechanisms underlying these gains. The framework connects image fusion to verifiable semantic representations for multimodal retrieval and knowledge management.


### arXiv 2611.02893 - Voice-Native LLMs Hear Frustration but Do Not Listen to It

- categories: cs.SD, cs.AI, eess.AS
- published: 2026-11-02T10:26:16Z
- authors: Hanjun Luo, Yuhan Lu, Hanan Salam
- query-set: cs.AI
- abs: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.02893

Users convey frustration in what they say and in how they say it, and voice-native models, which hear speech instead of an ASR transcript, can register both. Existing evaluations read unchanged performance as robustness without checking perception. Following Brunswik's lens model, we measure on identical audio whether frustration is expressed, perceived, and utilized. We cross frustrated wording with frustrated voice in a within-item 2x2 design over 353 tasks and release the 1,412 clips as VoxAffect. Across 8 models, outputs look robust, with no detectable vocal effect on accuracy for 7. With neutral wording, all 8 label the frustrated voice frustrated in at most 3.1% of clips, although 7 of them detect acted anger in 73-100% of clips and human listeners classify the voice correctly in 65.3% of judgments. Yet linear probes decode the frustrated voice from the audio encoders of the 2 open-weight models, with paired accuracy 0.67 and 0.69, and in proprietary models too the voice raises perceived frustration once the words agree. The models thus hear the frustrated voice but do not listen to it, whereas their invariance to frustrated wording, perceived in 40-75% of clips, is genuine robustness. Our work is openly accessible at https://github.com/Astarojth/hear-not-listen.


### arXiv 2611.02892 - JABER: Sequential Quality Control for Human-AI Data Annotation under Shared Bias

- categories: cs.AI
- published: 2026-11-02T10:25:48Z
- authors: Fengxu Yang, Minghui Liwang, Yang Lu, Bo Han, Xianbin Wang, Seyyedali Hosseinalipour
- query-set: cs.AI
- abs: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.02892

High-quality annotations are essential for training and evaluating artificial intelligence (AI) systems. Quality control (QC) often acquires additional human or AI judgments before accepting a label. Nevertheless, shared biases can cause correlated judgments, leading conventional methods to mistake repeated agreement for independent evidence of correctness. To address this challenge, we propose JABER (Joint Alias-Preserving Belief for Evidence Routing), a bias-aware sequential QC framework that maintains a joint Bayesian posterior over label correctness and shared biases to guide evidence acquisition and termination decisions. Building on this posterior, we develop a finite-horizon algorithm that selects evidence-acquisition and termination actions to minimize expected QC loss under resource constraints. Our theoretical analysis shows that conditional-independence updating can become arbitrarily confident in an incorrect label under persistent shared bias.
