We further identify when repeated inspections cannot distinguish a correct label from an incorrect label supported by shared bias, when obtaining a new label can resolve this ambiguity, and why modeling the dependence between label correctness and shared bias can be necessary for QC decisions. Our experiments show that JABER reduces expected QC loss by an average of 4.65% compared with a method maintaining separate label correctness and bias probabilities when shared bias makes repeated inspections unreliable. Further, JABER's finite-horizon planning, which evaluates outcomes of successive QC actions, reduces expected QC loss by an average of 10.05% compared with a one-step lookahead method. In real-API experiments on FEVER, JABER achieves zero labeling errors while reducing QC costs by 2.67% and 16.99% compared with marginal-based belief modeling and one-step lookahead methods, respectively.


### arXiv 2611.02889 - Tug of Watts: A study on the Energy - Accuracy Trade-off of Large Language Models on Medical Question Answering Tasks

- categories: cs.CL, cs.AI
- published: 2026-11-02T10:23:57Z
- authors: Viju Sudhi, Giulio Billi, Andreea Scrob, Regina Stodden, Philipp Cimiano
- query-set: cs.AI
- abs: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.02889

Large Language Models (LLMs) are increasingly used in medical question answering (QA) systems. While recent works aim at incrementally improving the downstream task accuracy, an empirical estimation of the models' energy consumption and the associated carbon footprints is often overlooked. In this study, we demonstrate how practitioners can estimate energy consumption and arrive at reliable emission estimates for medical QA tasks available in the OpenMedical-LLM leaderboard by investigating 13 LLMs from 3 model families ranging from 270M to 14B parameters. We discuss how one can critically interpret energy versus accuracy trade-offs for a set of models for a given task. With this work, we underline that reporting disjoint energy and accuracy metrics alone does not suffice for careful trade-off assessments, but it also demands systematic construction and interpretation of joint trade-off metrics. We envisage such studies aiding practitioners in adopting better and more efficient models for their task and helping them become active participants in building sustainable LLM pipelines.


### arXiv 2611.02883 - Integrating Domain Adaptation and Retrieval-Augmented Generation for Saline-Alkali Land Reclamation Planning

- categories: cs.AI
- published: 2026-11-02T10:19:14Z
- authors: Zuguang He, Congcong Zheng, Wanyue Chen, Wenya Zhou, Zaijin Yang, Long Long
- query-set: cs.AI
- abs: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.02883

Reclamation of saline-alkali land requires management plans that account for soil and water conditions, treatment evidence, and financial constraints. This study develops Wenyao, an agricultural decision-support system integrating domain adaptation with retrieval-augmented generation. A bilingual corpus of 8,664 research papers was processed using a 110-indicator schema to construct SA-Bench, comprising 4,860 paper-level planning instances. A QwQ-32B model was adapted through low-rank supervised fine-tuning and combined with case retrieval for budget-conditioned planning. Evidence extraction achieved an F1-score of 0.83 on 300 expert-annotated papers. Without retrieval, Wenyao achieved 95.83% accuracy on 936 source-disjoint multiple-choice questions, compared with 87.93% for the strongest general-purpose baseline; removing explanatory supervision reduced accuracy to 89.42%. Matched-evidence comparisons across 69 region-budget cases supported complementary contributions from adaptation and retrieval. In a blind assessment by 31 experts, Wenyao with retrieval obtained the highest mean scores for objectives and projected outcomes, whereas GPT-5 scored highest for cost estimates. These findings support literature-informed reclamation planning with professional review, while ongoing field monitoring will assess soil and crop responses.


### arXiv 2611.02856 - Kolmogorov-Arnold Networks vs. Multi-Layer Perceptrons: A Rigorous Statistical Benchmark on Clinical Tabular Data

- categories: cs.LG, cs.AI
- published: 2026-11-02T09:58:53Z
- authors: Rafał Wodziński, Paweł Szczerbiak
- query-set: cs.AI
- abs: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.02856

Kolmogorov-Arnold Networks (KANs) have recently been proposed as a promising alternative to Multi-Layer Perceptrons (MLPs). However, empirical evaluations on structured medical datasets remain scarce, and commonly reported metrics like AUROC can yield overly optimistic performance estimates on imbalanced clinical cohorts. In this study, we present a standardized benchmark comparing eleven architectures - nine KAN variants (FastKAN, WavKAN, ReLUKAN, ChebyKAN, TaylorKAN, GramKAN, JacobiKAN, LegendreKAN, and TabKAN), a tuned StandardMLP, and a Random Forest baseline - across seven heterogeneous medical tabular datasets spanning oncology, cardiology, nephrology, neurology, endocrinology, and obstetrics. Our experimental protocol employs a 3x5 Repeated Stratified K-Fold cross-validation scheme with nested Bayesian hyperparameter optimization and evaluates discrimination via Area Under the Precision-Recall Curve (AUPRC), calibration through the Brier Score, and computational efficiency. Formal equivalence testing is conducted using the Bayesian Correlated t-Test with a Region of Practical Equivalence (ROPE) at +/- 0.01. The results indicate that on datasets with severe class imbalance, dedicated KAN architectures (particularly TabKAN and ReLUKAN) can improve minority-class detection and achieve higher AUPRC than standard MLPs. Nevertheless, across pooled datasets, KAN variants demonstrate comparable overall predictive discrimination rather than a systematic advantage, while requiring 1.20x to 1.48x longer inference latency per sample and up to 12.5x more trainable parameters. Consequently, StandardMLP remains a strong baseline on the efficiency Pareto frontier, while KAN variants provide a viable alternative when precision on rare positive cases is the primary clinical objective. The code for reproducing the results is available at https://github.com/rafalwodzinski/kan_mlp_comparison.


### arXiv 2611.02852 - Value-Conditioned Proposal Acquisition for Test-Time Reasoning

- categories: cs.AI
- published: 2026-11-02T09:53:39Z
- authors: Zeyan Li, Jianfeng Xu
- query-set: cs.AI
- abs: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.02852

Sampling and selection spend test-time compute inside a candidate pool, but they cannot recover a correct answer that never enters it. We introduce ValueFormation, a test-time policy that decides after each primary-model draw whether to stop, continue sampling, or acquire one proposal from a heterogeneous reasoning specialist. The three actions are compared on one cost-regularized value scale learned from vote-state features; the calibration is frozen across tasks and primary models, and acquired proposals are accepted only through a frozen posterior comparison. On held-out MATH-500, one Llama-fitted calibration improves over the strongest of eight identically evaluated baselines by more than fifteen points for each of three 8B primaries and transfers unchanged to Qwen3-14B. Removing acquisition lowers Llama MATH accuracy by 20.43 points, while same-trace and same-access replays attribute the gain to state-conditioned acquisition rather than specialist access alone. Where primary coverage is high, as on GSM8K, acquisition is rare and gains mainly come from cheaper allocation; on MBPP, a fixed code route attains the top accuracy at the lowest interaction cost. Varying the token price monotonically reduces calls without retraining.


### arXiv 2611.02849 - TLAPS-Bench: Formally Proving Correctness of Real-World System Specifications with TLA+ Proof System

- categories: cs.AI, cs.DC, cs.LO, cs.SE
- published: 2026-11-02T09:51:18Z
- authors: Qian Cheng, Ruize Tang, Saad Mohammad Rafid Pial, Zhizhen Cathy Cai, Yiming Su, Jackson Clark
- query-set: cs.AI
- abs: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.02849

TLAPS-Bench is a benchmark and execution harness for constructing machine-checked proofs for real-world system specifications in TLA+ with agentic AI. Proving correctness of complex system specifications requires discovering inductive invariants, reasoning about intricate state transitions, and decomposing proofs into tractable obligations. TLAPS-Bench provides a proof harness that supports iterative proof development atop the TLA+ Proof System (TLAPS), where agents are equipped with proof tools and libraries, with safeguards against reward hacking. Using the harness, we have employed AI agents to prove desired properties for important system specifications that were not proved before, spanning cache coherence, Byzantine consensus, and write-ahead logs. TLAPS-Bench contains 265 proof targets (each encodes a property to prove) from 95 TLA+ specifications of real-world systems. We evaluated frontier AI agents, represented by Codex with GPT models, on a subset of 70 proof targets across 12 specifications. The highest-scoring model, GPT-6.1 Sol (max), proved 31 (44.3%) proof targets. The results show challenges and opportunities in end-to-end proof development.


### arXiv 2611.02846 - SpecOPD: Branch-Parallel On-Policy Distillation for Speculative Decoding

- categories: cs.AI
- published: 2026-11-02T09:47:06Z
- authors: Tianyu Liu, Jun Zhang, Wenjie Huang, Yuhao Shen, Tianqi Xu, Xinyue Hao
- query-set: cs.AI
- abs: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.02846

Speculative decoding (SD) accelerates large language model inference by reducing the sequential cost of token-by-token generation. Advanced SD methods reduce proposal cost with parallel drafters that predict entire blocks in one forward pass, but overall speedup still depends on acceptance length. On-policy distillation (OPD) can push well-trained drafters beyond acceptance-length plateaus, yet conventional pipelines collect supervision through sequential draft-and-verify rollouts. Our key insight is that on-policy supervision need not inherit these dependencies: a draft block can be supervised using only its reference prefix and draft-sampled history. We propose SpecOPD, a branch-parallel OPD framework that parallelizes draft sampling and target supervision across independent branches drawn from the evolving drafter policy at reference anchors. We pack these branches into proposal trees to share target-prefix computation, using tree masks for full attention, branch-consistent indexer groups for sparse attention, and indexed state replay for linear attention to preserve each branch's history. We train the drafter with an E2E-TV surrogate that directly targets acceptance length. Our study spans 8B full-attention (GQA), 27B full-linear (GQA--GDN), and 125B sparse-linear (QSA--GDN) targets, three parallel draft architectures (DFlash, DSpark, and DFlash V2), and four task domains (dialogue, coding, mathematics, and agentic). SpecOPD achieves up to 41.45% higher mean acceptance length, $16.09\times$ supervised-token throughput over sequential-rollout OPD under native configurations, and up to $3.56\times$ generation speedup over autoregressive decoding.


### arXiv 2611.02843 - Math Foundation Formalization Agent: A General-Purpose Approach to Formalizing Mathematical Foundations

- categories: cs.LO, cs.AI
- published: 2026-11-02T09:45:31Z
- authors: Jiaxin Xie, Guowei Dou, Xiang Ji, Wensheng Yu
- query-set: cs.AI
- abs: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.02843

Mathematical formalization plays an important role in establishing rigorous and machine-verifiable foundations for mathematical reasoning. With the development of large language models (LLMs) and interactive theorem provers, intelligent agents offer a promising approach to automating the formalization of mathematical theories. This paper presents the Math Foundation Formalization Agent (MFF-Agent), a general-purpose intelligent agent designed to support formalization tasks across multiple mathematical domains. The agent integrates the reasoning capabilities of large language models with the formal verification capabilities of the Rocq proof assistant. It incorporates task classification, tool-assisted execution, verification feedback, and state-based task management to support the generation, refinement, and verification of formal proofs. Unlike an approach focused on a single mathematical theory, the agent is examined through case studies involving Morse--Kelley axiomatic set theory (MK), real numbers, propositional logic, and equivalences of the axiom of choice. These cases investigate its applicability across mathematical foundations and distinguish source-level completion, interactive verification, and reproducible compilation. The results demonstrate the feasibility of a unified operational framework for heterogeneous foundation-level tasks and clarify the gap between such a framework and a fully integrated mathematical foundation.


### arXiv 2611.02839 - CARE: Grounded Recursive Self-Improvement for Production Customer-Service Agents

- categories: cs.AI
- published: 2026-11-02T09:39:52Z
- authors: Yanbei Jiang, Chao Lei, Rena Gao, Xiaochen Zhao, Yuchen Ge, Haohang Liu
- query-set: cs.AI
- abs: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.02839

Recursive self-improvement (RSI) in production agents involves transforming accumulated experience into reliable model updates while adapting to evolving business policies. We present CARE (Customer-service Agent Retrospective and Evolve), a grounded RSI framework that operationalizes this process in customer service. Its goal is to replace a frontier proprietary incumbent with an in-house mid-sized model that continues learning after deployment. After a one-time cold start on governed production traces, the current model serves production traffic alongside the incumbent, and its own failures drive the next update. A 24/7 inspection process establishes failures through evidence-blinded, independently reviewed judgments. Attribution separates model errors from defects in Skills, tools, or the harness, routing component failures to repair. Model-attributable trajectories are truncated at the first erroneous decision and paired with verified repairs. On-policy self-distillation (OPSD) then updates the model using the same model as teacher, with the diagnosis and repair supplied as privileged information unavailable to the student. To support continued evolution as business requirements change, training mixes failures from different Skill/SOP versions, each judged against the policy in force when it occurred. Candidates run an executable simulation test compiled from the latest SOP and then frozen, followed by offline inspection of the resulting traces. Promotion requires a combined simulation-and-inspection score at least at parity with the deployed version, confirmation through an online comparison with the incumbent, and human approval.


### arXiv 2611.02833 - Reimagining computational quantum research with AI scientists

- categories: quant-ph, cs.AI, cs.LG
- published: 2026-11-02T09:32:13Z
- authors: Yuxuan Du, Hanqing Zhao, Xinbiao Wang, Yiming Lu, Kaining Zhang, Dacheng Tao
- query-set: cs.AI
- abs: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.02833

AI scientists are expanding automated research across scientific domains, yet their ability to turn the scientific record into a foundation for autonomous discovery remains elusive. Here we uncover a fundamental barrier to this transition and demonstrate a route to overcome it in computational quantum science, where advances shape both what we can learn from complex quantum systems and what quantum computing can ultimately deliver. To identify this barrier, we introduce OmniQ, an expert-audited virtual research environment that converts published studies into executable reproduction tasks. Across 59 papers spanning classical simulation, quantum algorithms and quantum-property characterization, we systematically evaluate five agentic systems powered by large language models and six third-party agent harnesses. Our findings reveal that current AI scientists often fail to reproduce published results, exposing a fundamental mismatch between what scientific papers communicate and what autonomous agents require. By contrast, human expert intervention can turn these failures into successful reproductions, showing that the missing information can be identified and converted into reusable research experience. Guided by this observation, we develop Residual Agents (RA), a quantum-tailored self-improving AI scientist that distils externally verified workflow repairs into such experience. RA substantially improves research performance under fixed base models and limited adaptation budgets. For example, RA raises DeepSeek V4.1 Flash performance from 68% to 85%, matching Claude Code with Opus 5 at a third of its cost despite using a weaker base model. We integrate all reproduced results into an extensible platform built on the model context protocol, making verified research workflows directly accessible to AI scientists and allowing new scientific papers to be continuously incorporated.


### arXiv 2611.02830 - Think with Skills: Pseudocode Planning Before Execution for Advanced Reasoners

- categories: cs.AI
- published: 2026-11-02T09:29:30Z
- authors: Boshen Zhou, Boxuan Yue, Xiang Li, Shanchao Yang
- query-set: cs.AI
- abs: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.02830

Language model agents often struggle with complex tasks even when equipped with expert-written skills. Such skills encode declarative knowledge, whereas successful execution requires procedural knowledge tailored to the task at hand. Natural language skills may leave conditions and dependencies implicit, and tasks involving multiple skills further require agents to coordinate intermediate results and execution order. We introduce Think with Skills, a method that trains reasoning models to turn relevant skill descriptions into task-specific pseudocode plans before execution. The pseudocode explicitly represents operations, conditions, intermediate results, and dependencies, providing a structured bridge from skill descriptions to agent actions. We construct training data that pairs tasks and skills with pseudocode plans and execution trajectories, use supervised fine tuning to initialize this behavior, and further optimize end-to-end task performance with Group Relative Policy Optimization. On SkillsBench, Think with Skills raises task success from 35.0% to 62.0%. These results show that proceduralizing expert knowledge before execution substantially improves skill use.


### arXiv 2611.02826 - RxnMiner: A Multimodal Agent for Structured Reaction Data Extraction from Chemistry Figures

- categories: cs.AI
- published: 2026-11-02T09:24:55Z
- authors: Jinbo Hu, Songren Zhang, Chuanxun Fu, Jinrui Huang, Zhen Hao Wong, Dongxu Cui
- query-set: cs.AI
- abs: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.02826

Reaction data underpin machine learning for reaction prediction, condition recommendation, and retrosynthetic planning, yet extracting these data from literature figures still requires substantial manual effort. Many figures encode multiple experiments through shared templates, substituent tables, and product grids. Recovering concrete reaction records therefore requires molecular recognition, association of evidence with individual experimental entries, and reconstruction of indirectly specified participants. We present RxnMiner, a multimodal agent system that combines an expert-informed extraction procedure with a chemistry toolbox to coordinate these operations. The agent reads molecular structures, resolves shared and entry-specific evidence, and instantiates participants from templates to assemble source-linked reaction records. We also introduce ChemRxn-Bench, a benchmark of 603 reaction figures with 8,547 manually annotated and reviewed reaction records, evaluated under a common protocol for exact molecular identity and reactant-product pairing. On this benchmark, with Qwen3.8-Flash-Next as the backbone, RxnMiner raises stereochemistry-aware reaction macro-F1 from 21.0% under direct prediction to 71.0%, compared with 25.0% for the strongest existing extraction system. Across five backbones, it also outperforms the same base agent without the extraction procedure and chemistry toolbox by 9.4 to 25.5 points. Applied without human intervention to 10,107 literature figures, RxnMiner produces a silver corpus of 88,202 source-linked reaction records.


### arXiv 2611.03138 - KlaskTron: An Open-Source Platform for Physical Adversarial Multi-Agent RL

- categories: cs.RO, cs.AR, cs.MA
- published: 2026-11-02T13:49:53Z
- authors: Aswin Ramachandran, Jona Schulz, Maurus Derungs, Carlo Angelini, Tobias Meier, Raffaello D'Andrea
- query-set: cs.MA
- abs: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.03138

Progress in robot learning, particularly physical multi-agent reinforcement learning (MARL), is currently challenged by a limited availability of accessible, standardized benchmarks. While simulation-based MARL has produced remarkable emergent behaviors, from coordinated team play to complex tool use, translating these advances to physical systems remains difficult. A key barrier is infrastructural: physical platforms are often prohibitively expensive, require specialized facilities, or lack open mechanisms and designs for reproducibility. We introduce KlaskTron, an open-source, low-cost (<\$2500 USD), desktop-scale robotic testbed for adversarial MARL based on the dynamic tabletop game KLASK. The platform features dual CoreXY gantries with high-torque brushless motors, enabling the high-speed, precise manipulation required for competitive play. Crucially, we provide a complete ecosystem: hardware designs (CAD, BOM), a GPU-native digital twin in NVIDIA Isaac Lab for high-fidelity modeling and simulation, and a validated sim-to-real baseline using a learned neural actuator model. We demonstrate that this baseline enables zero-shot policy transfer, with trained agents exhibiting emergent adversarial behaviors. To ensure full reproducibility of our results, all project assets are released publicly: hardware CAD/BOM and assembly documentation at [https://idscethzurich.github.io/klask_hardware], inference software stack at [https://github.com/IDSCETHZurich/klask_software] and the simulation and MARL setup at [https://github.com/IDSCETHZurich/klask_rl].


### arXiv 2611.03020 - The Tower of Babel in artificial intelligence: who should speak, and when it pays to listen

- categories: cs.LG, cs.MA
- published: 2026-11-02T12:18:00Z
- authors: Mohammad Salahshour
- query-set: cs.MA
- abs: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.03020

Two machines can each recognize an object well and still fail together. We study classifiers that see different parts of one object and send short messages to a receiver that identifies its class. Coordination asks how to design messages together; cooperation asks whether the team outperforms one sender given the whole budget. On three digit datasets, messages designed together beat messages designed apart by 18.07 to 22.68 percentage points on average with two-bit messages, with gains in all 24 configurations. A fixed agreement, you report the row and I the column, recovers about half this gain, with only the receiver learned. Senders that draw the same distinctions waste a narrow vocabulary, and senders whose distinctions cross multiply it. A team has no more possible messages than one sender with the same budget, so a partner must bring evidence the first lacks. A solvable model shows individual statistics alone cannot determine whether a partnership helps. On MNIST digits, six shared bits remove 49.54 percent of the strongest tested solo's errors on average. Paired measurements correctly predicted most sensor teams would reduce average accuracy on new participants. Who should speak is a property of the pair, and must be measured in pairs.


### arXiv 2611.02984 - Classification Aggregation with Multiple Memberships

- categories: econ.TH, cs.GT, cs.MA
- published: 2026-11-02T11:50:31Z
- authors: Federico Fioravanti, Leo Kurata, Vasilis Stamatis
- query-set: cs.MA
- abs: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.02984

People face the problem of classifying objects into categories while aggregating their opinions. We axiomatically study such a classification aggregation problem with multiple memberships, meaning that each individual assigns one category to every object, whereas the resulting classification may assign more than one category. Under the natural settings and axioms (i.e., Independence and Supportiveness), each of our new axioms -- Responsibility, Split Stability and Rejection Stability -- characterises what we call the junta rules. The rules assign a category to an object exactly when some member of a fixed coalition does so. The coalitions that can secure an assignment form a grill, a topological structure implied by an ultrafilter. Hence, the study parallels the literature on Arrow's theorem characterised by the decisive coalition structures. An infinite population setting is also investigated to clarify the differences among the axioms.


### arXiv 2611.02909 - Cloudra: Geometric Reasoning and Scientific Validation for Autonomous Reaction Mechanism Exploration

- categories: cs.MA
- published: 2026-11-02T10:42:24Z
- authors: Chengyi Yang, Qianxin Zhou, Yang Li
- query-set: cs.MA
- abs: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.02909

Autonomous reaction-mechanism exploration requires proposing reaction steps, preparing three-dimensional structures, and determining which reactant and product a computed saddle point connects. Large language model (LLM) agents can misjudge spatial relationships and confuse a computed structure with the species or step they intended to study. We present Cloudra, a multi-agent framework that constrains mechanistic output through a Delivery DAG of chemical objects. Species nodes carry geometries and bonding topology; elementary-step edges carry transition states and reaction connections between species. Cheminformatics algorithms establish molecular identities and atom correspondences. Deterministic routines measure and transform structures to prepare candidate geometries. Frequency and IRC calculations determine the stationary points found and the species they connect. Agents use these objects to test proposed steps, compare pathways, and revise the mechanism. CloudraBench evaluates the framework's end-to-end mechanism-calculation capability across 50 tasks covering elementary reactions, multistep mechanisms, stereochemical competition, and computational method limitations. Across 200 runs under four workflow configurations, the primary Guided workflow achieves a mean composite score of 0.805, compared with 0.584 for the same-model Standard baseline. On Hard-tier tasks, the Guided mean increases from 0.689 under Standard to 0.958. Under Guided, the self-consistency component reaches its maximum score in 24 of the 40 non-Impossible tasks, compared with seven under Standard. Behavioral analyses examine how the agents use bond and dihedral measurements, optimized structures, and IRC endpoints to revise local mechanistic assignments.


### arXiv 2611.02875 - AGiR: Mitigating Gift Over-Reliance in Mixed-Motive Games

- categories: cs.MA, cs.LG
- published: 2026-11-02T10:13:21Z
- authors: Woohyeon Byeon, Seongmin Kim, Jiwon Jeon, Woojun Kim, Youngchul Sung
- query-set: cs.MA
- abs: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.02875

In this paper, we consider mixed-motive games (MMGs) which model various real-world multi-agent scenarios where self-interested agents must both cooperate and compete. We identify an issue with existing gifting methods, where agents become overly dependent on incoming gifts and fail to learn reward-yielding behaviors, a phenomenon we call gift over-reliance. To address this, we propose a refusal-augmented gifting framework that balances cooperation and competition in MMGs. Our approach introduces an adaptive refusal mechanism that allows agents to selectively reject a portion of received gifts from their policy-update signals, thereby mitigating gift over-reliance and preventing the agents from being exploited by other agents. This mechanism is gifting-method-agnostic, serving as a plug-in for existing schemes while enabling decentralized learning. We demonstrate that across diverse MMG environments and gifting schemes, our mechanism consistently reduces gift over-reliance and prevents lock-in to sacrificing policies, improving $α$-fairness as a result, without necessitating major changes to existing training pipelines.


### arXiv 2611.02797 - SelfImprove-Bench: Evaluating Generalization in Self-Improving Agents

- categories: cs.AI, cs.CL, cs.LG, cs.MA
- published: 2026-11-02T09:08:12Z
- authors: Bangrui Xu, Jie Yang, Sidi Chen, Xuanhe Zhou, Jiantao Qiu, Qianyu Yang
- query-set: cs.MA
- abs: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.02797

Recursively self-improving (RSI) systems are increasingly viewed as a promising means of accelerating progress toward artificial general intelligence. They aim to autonomously improve their capabilities by refining their working pipelines and recursively improving the strategies that guide subsequent refinements, with gains generalizing to unseen scenarios.However, existing evaluations lack reliable metrics to distinguish capability gains from scenario fitting, sustainable and extensible protocols for unseen tasks and difficulties, and fine-grained attribution of improvement to evolution traces. To this end, we propose SelfImprove-Bench,an RSI evaluation framework that organizes tasks into three primary capabilities (i.e., Exploring, Planning, Reasoning) as scenarios for guiding model evolution. We instantiated the tasks on games for controllability and reproducibility, and pairs them with scalable generation and orchestration driven by task templates and difficulty coefficients. Beyond task-level scores, SelfImprove-Bench organizes evaluation into generalization ladder levels G0 to G4 that measure how far gains transfer from training conditions. Experiments reveal that the evolutionary gains of existing RSI methods are inconsistent across generalization levels.


### arXiv 2611.02738 - Multi-Modal Multi-Agent System for Bridge Operation and Maintenance based on Large Language Model

- categories: cs.MA
- published: 2026-11-02T08:26:24Z
- authors: Xiangyun Luo, Yunkai Zhang, Yong Huang, Stephen Wu, Hui Li
- query-set: cs.MA
- abs: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.02738

Bridges are critical transportation infrastructure, and reliable operation and maintenance (O&M) is essential for traffic safety and long-term structural performance. However, bridge O&M faces key bottlenecks: no unified architecture coordinates heterogeneous resources; model inferences lack clear links to physical-mechanical laws; and experts' tacit knowledge remains difficult to formalize. This study proposes a knowledge-enhanced autonomy formalization methodology and apply it to construct the Bridge O&M Multi-Modal Multi-Agent System (BOMMA), which integrates heterogeneous diagnostic actions, physics-mechanics-informed representations, long-term memory, and structured experiential knowledge within a coordinated multi-agent workflow, enabling interpretable and traceable scheduling, analysis, and decision-making. On 3,492 multi-source instructions, the proposed method achieves scheduling recalls of 85.37% for simple and 99.40% for composite tasks, with 95.35% BERT F1-score for O&M action reasoning. Ablation studies confirm the contributions of the components in the autonomy. This study bridges general LLM reasoning and specialized bridge O&M requirements.


### arXiv 2611.02693 - Source-Resolved Collective Cognition in Robotics: Mathematical Foundations for Multimodal and Collective Estimation, and Their Reach across the Cognition Loop

- categories: cs.RO, cs.MA
- published: 2026-11-02T07:43:10Z
- authors: Juergen Graf
- query-set: cs.MA
- abs: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.02693

The Generalized Singular Value Decomposition (GSVD) is a central tool for comparing two linear operators that act on the same unknown. Once the output metrics are fixed, it answers the basis-independent question "along which state directions does the first operator dominate the second, and by how much?". This foundational volume builds the GSVD and its surrounding estimation theory from the ground up. Part I re-derives every piece of linear algebra and statistical estimation machinery needed later -- inner products, the four subspaces, orthogonal matrices, the spectral theorem, quadratic forms, the ordinary SVD, Lie-group tangent perturbations, Jacobians, covariance, whitening, Fisher information, CCA, mutual information, discrimination criteria, and covariance intersection -- with self-contained derivations and worked numerical examples. Part II states the GSVD in both a transparent and a dimensionally general form, then proves it by two complementary routes. Part III works one small matrix pair through five different lenses, each computed by hand and verified numerically. Part IV separates information attribution, subspace geometry, statistical dependence, and decision-theoretic discrimination for collective systems. Part V evaluates the role and limits of the GSVD across the cognition loop. Part VI covers numerical practice, including a tested reference implementation. Every number printed in this document was verified in double precision. Where a statement is an assumption rather than a theorem, it is labelled as such.


### arXiv 2611.02644 - Coati: Host-Grounded Execution for Installable Mobile GUI Agents

- categories: cs.MA
- published: 2026-11-02T07:06:26Z
- authors: Yudou Ying, Pu Shi, Hanlei Shi, Yuhua Zheng
- query-set: cs.MA
- abs: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.02644

Mobile GUI agents can carry out tasks across apps, yet most run on a separate computer that drives a phone over the Android Debug Bridge (ADB), out of reach of ordinary users and of the apps through which most people reach Web services. We present Coati, a source-available agent that users install as an Android app. Its runtime on the phone, the host, records what a model alone cannot see: the effect of each action at its target, result screens across apps, and the progress of repeated steps. VerifyLoop checks action outcomes and completion claims against host evidence and reports a completion it cannot audit as unverified. AppMemory builds per-app procedural memory from observations that the interface supports and reuses it on later visits. A routed fast executor lets a lightweight selection model take simple, confident steps and lets the host repeat loops of simple steps that the large model plans once. On all 116 AndroidWorld tasks, with Gemini 3.8 Flash as every agent's backbone, Coati succeeds on 86.5% of the tasks over three trials, ahead of the strongest open-source agent Artemis (77.6% with the same accessibility-only access, 76.5% even when allowed ADB) and far ahead of FinalRun and Midscene (about 59%), and VerifyLoop cuts false completion claims by about a third at comparable success. In everyday use, the fast executor cuts the median time of phone tasks by 39% at comparable success and runs three of four loop tasks 2.8-5.4x faster with 6-9x fewer model tokens, while AppMemory shortens a repeated deep route in an unfamiliar app from 18.5 steps to 6-7. On an Android 16 phone, routed execution completes all 27 benchmark runs with 76% fewer Reasoning calls on the critical path. Host evidence, not model self-assessment, makes an installable agent reliable and fast.


### arXiv 2611.02571 - Organizational Learning in Materials Discovery Campaigns: Which Databases, Calculations, and Agent Teams Pay Off

- categories: cs.MA, cond-mat.mtrl-sci, cs.LG
- published: 2026-11-02T05:56:19Z
- authors: Kinston Ackölf, Taylor D. Sparks
- query-set: cs.MA
- abs: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.02571

More databases can make a materials campaign worse. Through an organizational lens, we trace each source's contribution. One band-gap campaign ranks seven anion families from archived measured gaps, one vote per source. In the first half of its budget, all seven pooled rank worse (rank agreement 0.737; 1 = measured order) than the best pool of three (0.851). As a stability check, choosing the pool on half the runs and testing on the rest kept the gap. Splitting Materials Project afterwards points to its transition-metal oxides and fluorides. Two databases from one pipeline add nothing over one, but pooling gives their shared ordering two votes. New calculations repay their cost early only when two or three campaigns reuse them. Flat language-model agent teams trailed a random-pick baseline (mean 0.876, sd 0.096, 20 searches). An adaptive Al-Mg-Si team, mostly interatomic potentials, was scored on unseen HSE06 calculations. In its first quarter of compute it found 49% of the ten lowest-formation-energy compounds, against at most 40% for fixed teams and 73% for MACE-MP-0 alone. Merging members whose orderings agreed carried most of its gain. This suggests pooling a few sources that fit the measurements, counting each pipeline once and charging for computation.


### arXiv 2611.02539 - Scientific Liquidity Agents: An Agent-Based Model of Knowledge Arbitrage in Scientific Communities

- categories: cs.MA, cs.AI, cs.SI
- published: 2026-11-02T05:09:40Z
- authors: Koutian Wu, You Liu, Yue Liu, Zhiping Li, Ergan Shang
- query-set: cs.MA
- abs: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.02539

Scientific knowledge gains new uses when ideas, methods, and results move between research contexts. Understanding this process requires a model of both specialized knowledge production and the intermediary activities that connect it. We introduce Scientific Liquidity Agents (SLA), an agent-based model of knowledge arbitrage in scientific communities. SLA represents domain specialists and epistemic arbitrageurs within a shared environment of research problems, researcher interactions, and scientific artifacts. Individual decisions connect to collective state through a belief-desire-intention architecture, a prerequisite-based problem graph, a dynamic interaction network, and a persistent publication registry. The framework makes researcher roles and knowledge-transfer frictions explicit, providing a process-oriented account of scientific production and exchange. Two exploratory simulations with 10 and 18 agents produce 68 and 274 registered papers. In the smaller run, arbitrageurs take part in 16 of the 26 contact pairs that join this community into one connected component, while topical similarity alone splits it into seven. The same run yields three similarity edges, 26 contact pairs, and two coauthorship pairs, so measured community structure depends on what counts as a link. Paper production is abundant, but no recorded replication succeeds, which places the bottleneck in verification rather than production. Solved-problem sets correspond to the prerequisite reachability of the disciplines represented in each community. SLA makes knowledge liquidity, the movement and reuse of artifacts across domains, a quantity that agentic science can simulate and measure.


### arXiv 2611.02300 - Trustworthy Execution of Industrial Agents

- categories: cs.SE, cs.MA
- published: 2026-11-01T23:47:59Z
- authors: Zhongjie Wang, Mingyi Liu, Xiang He, Xiao Wang, Shuang Yu, Kai Wang
- query-set: cs.MA
- abs: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.02300

Industrial agents increasingly modify persistent engineering objects rather than merely generate answers. Authentication and successful calls cannot establish whether intended changes occurred, evidence supports a conclusion, or historical results remain applicable. We present a framework for trustworthy industrial-agent execution within a single enterprise governance domain. Eight control domains assign responsibilities across authority, planning, composition, tool execution, actual effects, evidence, baseline and use governance, and recovery and evolution. A four-plane architecture and three control chains connect these responsibilities through typed objects and version-bound interfaces. The specification separates tool returns from actual engineering effects, evidence coverage from engineering judgment, and historical lifecycle state from current applicability. Multi-agent handoffs are an architectural extension examined through deterministic interface tests. Distinct prototypes examine selected mechanisms using conformance tests, ablations, uncertainty stress, CAD/CAE execution, and local persistence. An OpenFOAM fixture produces COMPLETE coverage with FAIL judgment; an expired-job case requires readmission despite completed computation. CAD and recovery counterexamples expose omitted mandatory checks, discarded negative facts, and inconsistent producer-consumer mappings. Targeted repairs support local regression conclusions. The reported 0/29 hazardous commits measure closed-set specification conformance, not a safety probability. We distinguish the complete framework, implemented paths, and empirical evidence, identifying check-inventory completeness and cross-module fact preservation as prerequisites for extending these results.


### arXiv 2611.02260 - Towards a Theory for the Design of Rules in Multi-Agent Systems: the case of Homogeneous Fleets

- categories: cs.MA
- published: 2026-11-01T22:30:01Z
- authors: Matteo Penlington, Lorenzo Fiaschi, Raj Kiriti Velicheti, Andrea Censi, Emilio Frazzoli
- query-set: cs.MA
- abs: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.02260

The regulation of systems composed of many interacting decentralised agents is a long-standing problem. This problem has typically been resolved by the imposition of rules. Rules resolve the limitations of sub-optimal agents whilst simultaneously aligning the behaviour of the agents with the demands of society. Traffic laws, for example, let a driver cross a junction without seeing around the corner, and transport protocols such as TCP let applications share a network without knowing of one another. The problem is more pressing today, as autonomous systems are deployed in society. In this work, we establish the basis of a system-agnostic theory of rule design for multi-agent systems. We first state the rule design problem (RDP) in its generality. It relates a rule to the set of system performances, defined by the regulator, that the agents deployed under it can attain. However, it is not yet possible to solve the RDP in full generality. As such, we introduce the homogeneous rule design problem (HRDP), in which the agents in each environment are identical, and among other interesting properties, show that under worst-case aggregation the two problems have the same solution, bypassing the combinatorial complexity of the RDP. To solve the RDP under worst-case aggregation we propose Rule Branch and Bound (RuleBnB), an anytime branch-and-bound algorithm. Finally, we illustrate the value of solving the RDP on an autonomous driving case study.


### arXiv 2611.02090 - Local Solution Repair for Multi-Agent Pickup and Delivery

- categories: cs.RO, cs.MA
- published: 2026-11-01T17:58:05Z
- authors: Ethan Schneider, Jingkai Chen, Seth Hutchinson, Sonia Chernova
- query-set: cs.MA
- abs: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.02090

Multi-robot systems in automated warehouses must efficiently attend to a constant stream of pickup-and-delivery tasks while avoiding collisions. Existing Multi-Agent Pickup-and-Delivery (MAPD) solutions generate plans that are globally feasible but often locally inefficient. In large-scale, long-horizon deployments, these inefficiencies, such as robots backtracking from target goal locations and idling in congested areas, accumulate over time and degrade performance. This paper contributes Local Solution Repair (LSR), a goal replanning approach that detects suboptimal behaviors at run-time and locally improves plans to reduce conflict between agents. We demonstrate the utility of our approach by repairing solutions generated by state-of-the-art MAPD baselines, M2M and LNS-PBS. Our results in long-horizon warehouse simulations over three environments show upwards of 5.9% improvement in task throughput over current state-of-the-art methods by reducing the number of suboptimal instances by ~36k or by 14.6% with minor additional computation time.


### arXiv 2611.02079 - ParetoSaddler: Pareto-Guided Optimization of Agent Harnesses for Quality and Cost

- categories: cs.AI, cs.CL, cs.LG, cs.MA
- published: 2026-11-01T17:36:05Z
- authors: Xingzhi Qian, Sungho Park, Rongyuan Tan, Jianbo Yu, Jue Zhang, Lorenzo Cavallaro
- query-set: cs.MA
- abs: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.02079

Harness optimization can substantially improve LLM agents without modifying model weights, but existing methods primarily optimize response quality. Extending them to joint quality--cost optimization introduces a challenge: applying the deployment preference during intermediate updates can prematurely reject candidates that are valuable for later improvements. We introduce ParetoSaddler, which addresses this mismatch by separating intermediate candidate promotion from final deployment selection. At each iteration, an optimizer updates a parent harness to produce a candidate, and then compares the two over repeated executions and uses Bayesian bootstrap Pareto support to promote candidates showing credible progress in either quality or cost. Promoted candidates receive broader development evaluation and remain available as parents for later optimization steps. We evaluate ParetoSaddler on ClawMark and GAIA2 using domain-disjoint splits. On ClawMark, ParetoSaddler improves quality from 70.86\% to 81.35\% while reducing cost from \$1.17 to \$1.01 per task; on GAIA2, it improves quality from 56.22\% to 62.89\% while reducing cost from \$0.78 to \$0.45 per task. The Pareto-selected harnesses also achieve higher quality at lower cost than quality-only harness optimization. Analysis shows that Pareto-aware optimization retains intermediate candidates that would be discarded by deployment preference but can support later improvements, while Bayesian bootstrap screening filters out candidates whose gains are weakly supported across runs.


### arXiv 2611.02047 - Beliefs in feedback: Factored representations of interacting agents in transformers

- categories: cs.LG, cs.AI, cs.MA, nlin.AO
- published: 2026-11-01T16:52:50Z
- authors: David A. Ehrlich, Manuel Baltieri, Fernando E. Rosas, Alexander B. Boyd
- query-set: cs.MA
- abs: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.02047

Complex systems, from ecosystems to brains, are usually modeled as modular components coupled through feedback rather than as indivisible wholes. Can a transformer trained on the joint behavior of a group of interacting agents be understood in the same way? In this work, we develop a computational-mechanics framework to study agents interacting in feedback loops. We formally prove that any joint stochastic process can be presented as resulting from the behavior of agents interacting in feedback, and the resulting process is generated by a hidden Markov model whose Bayesian beliefs factorize into separate components per agent. Furthermore, our experiments show that transformers trained on such feedback sequences make use of this separability, disentangling the beliefs about the underlying factors into separate subspaces of their residual stream. This representation is found to scale linearly (instead of exponentially) with the number of agents. Moreover, interventions show that these representations are causally efficacious, allowing for targeted steering of individual agents' beliefs by selectively inserting counterfactuals. However, coarse-graining the behavior is found to disrupt the factorization, and can force a higher-dimensional representation. Together, the results establish both the dimensional advantage of a factorized representation and the ability of transformers to exploit it, laying the groundwork for investigating traces of compositional predictive architecture in pretrained models.


### arXiv 2611.01918 - AgenticMobility: An Open-Source Platform for Multi-AI-Agent Mobility Dynamics

- categories: cs.MA, cs.AI, physics.soc-ph
- published: 2026-11-01T13:58:00Z
- authors: Jiaheng Xiao, Jiangtao Han, Jiachen Wang, Honghai Li, Lijun Sun, Kay Axhausen
- query-set: cs.MA
- abs: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.01918

Understanding mobility dynamics and the effects of urban transportation management policies requires examining how individual travel decisions, traffic conditions, and policy interventions influence one another over time. We present AgenticMobility, an open-source experimental platform for studying how mobility AI agents and other AI agents interact, adapt through repeated experience, and collectively shape mobility outcomes. Persistent agents retain identities and relevant states across rounds, with explicit interfaces defining information access, admissible actions, and decision schedules; linked records trace decisions, execution, and feedback. In the current implementation, large language model (LLM) agents act as travelers choosing routes and departure times and as a manager periodically adjusting link charges within MATSim. A preliminary demonstration spans 100 simulated days in each of five configurations, with one run per configuration and a shared simulation seed. In the joint traveler--manager run, all 1,179,300 finalized target decisions satisfied action constraints; execution checks verified implementation of accepted choices and preservation of personal memories across days. Trajectories show stable route choices alongside departure-time adjustments and differences between controlled-trip and population-wide outcomes. The platform supports experiments on individual adaptation, collective outcomes, and management interventions by varying information access, retained experience, and management feedback. Source code is available at https://github.com/Agentic-City-Research/Agentic-Mobility


### arXiv 2611.01870 - Automated Stateful Specialization for Adaptive Agent Systems

- categories: cs.MA
- published: 2026-11-01T12:59:52Z
- authors: Myan Vu, Harrish Ayyanar, Jiang Pang, Anwiketh Reddy, Mayank Goel
- query-set: cs.MA
- abs: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.01870

Current automated agent design frameworks produce either static workflows that lack adaptability or per-query optimizers that prevent the accumulation of deep, agent-level task expertise. We propose a new direction that reconciles these paradigms: creating stateful teams of specialist agents that accumulate knowledge over time and can be reconfigured for novel tasks entirely without human intervention. To this end, we introduce ASpec, a framework that manages this full agent lifecycle by first autonomously discovering specialist archetypes via evolutionary search and then cultivating their expertise through experience, mirroring how human experts learn through practice and reflection. We further introduce a lightweight hierarchical control policy, "retain-then-escalate," which governs when to leverage the established agent system versus when to adapt its structure. Through comprehensive experiments, we demonstrate that this approach leads to significant performance gains on expert-level scientific benchmarks like GPQA while matching the state-of-the-art on broader domain tasks, demonstrating a promising path toward agent systems that are simultaneously expert, adaptive, and efficient. We will release the code at https://github.com/myanvoos/ASpec.


### arXiv 2611.01840 - Self-Evolving Agentic Workflows via Semantic Decomposition of Complex Tasks

- categories: cs.AI, cs.CL, cs.MA
- published: 2026-11-01T12:18:19Z
- authors: Zichuan Fu, Junjia Qi, Xian Wu, Wenlin Zhang, Yimin Deng, Kaifeng Guo
- query-set: cs.MA
- abs: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.01840

Agentic workflows organize complex tasks into reusable procedures. However, invoking an LLM at every step incurs repeated inference costs and delays. We propose a framework for self-evolving agentic workflows through semantic task decomposition. Each subtask has explicit applicability conditions and a procedure that executes code or invokes a model as needed. An agent uses execution feedback to refine the decomposition and update these procedures. Experiments in the GLEE competition show the framework on bargaining, negotiation and persuasion. Most observed final-decision procedures are implemented in code. Code is available at https://github.com/Applied-Machine-Learning-Lab/NeurIPS2026_SeaFlow.


### arXiv 2611.01742 - From Single-Agent Self-Discovery to Multi-Agent Adaptation: Scaling Laws of Partner Feedback and Convention Rigidity in Meta Reinforcement Learning Algorithms

- categories: cs.MA
- published: 2026-11-01T09:42:26Z
- authors: Haomin Luo, Ersha Li
- query-set: cs.MA
- abs: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.01742

A key milestone toward Recursive Self-Improvement (RSI) is replacing hand-crafted optimizers with meta-learned update rules (such as Disco103 and OPEN) that discover how agents learn. Yet when a self-discovered RL model trains with adapting teammates, does co-evolution sustain capability gains or trap agents in brittle conventions? Across 1,800+ runs in continuous collective robotics (VMAS) and discrete coordination (Overcooked), we establish five laws of multi-agent adaptation. First, four-cell counterfactual partner swaps expose an evaluation illusion: apparent team progress often masks focal regression (+1.066 team gain concealing -0.119 focal loss) and inflates transfer by +0.649 to +1.210. Second, a 288-run physical-coupling sweep ($α\in [0, 1.5]$) reveals a linear scaling law ($β= +0.255$, $p = 0.000488$, Spearman $ρ= 1.0$): live reciprocal feedback keeps Disco103 invariant (AUC 0.297--0.322), whereas severing feedback triggers a monotonic collapse (-0.172 to -0.490), with OPEN establishing a sensitivity spectrum (Disco103 > OPEN > MAPPO). Third, extending navigation training eightfold (1.05M steps) enables Disco103 to overtake MAPPO (+0.087 team return, +0.133 bank gain), while decoupled training boosts cross-partner completion by +10.16% (56.25% vs. 46.09%). Fourth, in Overcooked, MAPPO's self-play conventions induce a 28.13% cross-partner collision rate, whereas Disco103 cuts collisions to 12.77% and achieves 3.33x higher bank return (9.96 vs. 2.99; net gain +6.43), further improved by decoupled meta-adaptation (+1.39 with scripted partners). Finally, perturbation pulses ($p=0.00098$), auxiliary entropy diagnostics (8.485 nats), and 48 multi-lifetime meta-evolved rules ($L_2 \in [2.11, 4.91]$) trace these dynamics to partner-gradient blindness and auxiliary drift, informing multi-agent self-discoverd algorithms.


### arXiv 2611.01728 - MAC-Bench: Benchmarking Multi-Agent Collaboration via Interconnected Tasks

- categories: cs.MA, cs.AI, cs.CL
- published: 2026-11-01T09:24:22Z
- authors: Zerui Chen, Qinggang Zhang, Qichuan Liu, Zhishang Xiang, Zhimin Wei, Yunbo Tang
- query-set: cs.MA
- abs: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.01728

Large language model (LLM)-based multi-agent systems (MAS) tackle challenging tasks by dividing work among agents and coordinating their contributions toward shared objectives. Evaluating these systems therefore requires benchmarks that assess not only task-solving performance but also the ability to handle interdependent work. However, existing benchmarks often emphasize task difficulty without substantial collaboration requirements or evaluate only a limited range of predefined collaboration patterns. In this paper, we introduce MAC-Bench, a comprehensive benchmark for evaluating multi-agent collaboration through interconnected tasks. Rather than imposing predefined agent roles or communication protocols, MAC-Bench embeds collaboration requirements directly into task dependencies and shared-resource constraints. Specifically, we mine reusable collaboration structures from selected benchmark tasks at two levels. Local collaboration patterns capture recurring interactions among subtasks, while global task structures characterize how these interactions contribute to complete workflows. Through coarse-to-fine composition, these structures are recombined into task graphs combining different collaboration requirements. Tasks are instantiated in executable environments with automated verification of partial progress and complete task resolution.


### arXiv 2611.01449 - CONFIRM: Governing Scientific Reporting for Trustworthy Agentic Neuroimaging

- categories: cs.AI, cs.MA
- published: 2026-11-01T02:37:56Z
- authors: Yuxiang Wei, Lijing Zhu, Qizhen Lan, Yanteng Zhang, Vince D. Calhoun
- query-set: cs.MA
- abs: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.01449

Large language model (LLM) agents can autonomously run neuroimaging analyses from research questions to reported findings. Existing agentic systems use tools to check that code runs and reported values match its output, but rely on LLM judgment to decide which results warrant scientific reporting. We show that LLM judges endorse site-confounded findings even when the confound is presented as evidence. We present \textbf{CONFIRM}, a framework that separates running an analysis from deciding which findings to report. CONFIRM encodes each hypothesis as a machine-readable, pre-specified analysis plan that fixes the claim and its pass criteria before computing results. It then filters results through five deterministic gates (search history and multiplicity, confounds, power, analytic stability, and replication) under three configurable reporting standards, from least to most demanding: \textit{Screen}, \textit{Replicate}, and \textit{Full}. Failed claims receive diagnoses that guide follow-up analyses. To evaluate reporting decisions at the claim level, we introduce \textbf{NeuroClaimBench}, comprising 489 executable neuroimaging claims with frozen data, including literature-supported findings and 150 controls designed to violate individual methodological requirements. On NeuroClaimBench, CONFIRM falsely supports at most 3\% of controls, compared with 23--38\% for the baselines, while recovering up to 61\% of literature-supported claims. When auditing an existing agent, CONFIRM reduces false support on controls from 23\% to 0\% while retaining the agent's literature-supported claims. Its diagnostic feedback also produces more supported revisions than Self-Refine with fewer LLM calls. Together, CONFIRM and NeuroClaimBench show that basing reporting decisions on pre-specified analysis plans and deterministic gates is essential for trustworthy autonomous scientific discovery.


### arXiv 2611.01441 - Renormalized Cellular Intelligence: When Does Model Reduction Preserve Collective Capability?

- categories: cs.MA
- published: 2026-11-01T02:27:58Z
- authors: Xuening Wu, Yanlan Kang, Qianya Xu, Haode Yang, Shan Yu, Shenqin Yin
- query-set: cs.MA
- abs: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.01441

As multi-agent systems grow in size and complexity, understanding which features of their interactions support collective capability becomes increasingly difficult. Can a simpler description preserve both task performance and the measured contribution of interactions between agents? Preserving collective dynamics alone is insufficient: reduction can alter decision-relevant information or give a non-communicating baseline access to pooled observations, distorting the measured advantage of interaction. We introduce Renormalized Cellular Intelligence (RCI), a framework for specifying and auditing capability preservation across scales. RCI treats the task, interacting dynamics, matched baseline, decision interfaces, and resource budget as a joint object of reduction. It specifies dynamical closure, decision compatibility, and preservation of the intended baseline comparison, and bounds the resulting error in relative capability gain. An exact analytical construction demonstrates task-score preservation, while a counterexample shows how closed reduced dynamics can erase the measured interaction gain. Using cellular systems as models of locally interacting agents, we evaluate three reduction candidates on five frozen learned models without retraining. The final candidate reduces the evolving state from eight to five dimensions per cell (37.5%). Within-run uncertainty bounds support relative gain above a prespecified threshold for the fixed model collection, yet the candidate fails the closure and decision-preservation requirements for a complete RCI certificate. RCI provides explicit criteria for distinguishing positive interaction gains from


### arXiv 2611.01351 - MAPLE: A Multimodal Mobile Agent with Playbook-Learned Experience

- categories: cs.LG, cs.MA
- published: 2026-10-31T23:44:52Z
- authors: Jun Wang, Aurora Wei, Lucas Spangher, Ming Zhang, Eugene Ie, Yiannis Kantaros
- query-set: cs.MA
- abs: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.01351

Autonomous agents powered by vision-language models (VLMs) show great potential in navigating mobile graphical user interfaces (GUIs) to complete complex tasks. However, existing paradigms face fundamental scalability bottlenecks: fine-tuning requires massive high-fidelity datasets or prohibitive training time and compute, while gradient-free approaches rely on custom static heuristics or costly trial-and-error trajectory exploration where feedback is deferred until the end of execution. To address these limitations, we introduce MAPLE (Multimodal Agent with Playbook-Learned Experience), a novel gradient-free multi-agent framework that enables dynamic, step-wise experience sharing and execution-time prompt evolution. Unlike traditional action-reflection architectures that depend on costly, post-hoc self-reflection, MAPLE enables agents to cross-reference and distribute runtime insights in real time at each operational step. This dynamic exchange eliminates blind trial-and-error looping and steers agents along direct, grounded interaction paths. Extensive evaluations on the AndroidWorld and MobileWorld benchmarks demonstrate that MAPLE achieves state-of-the-art performance on complex, long-horizon mobile tasks, while proving scalable and adaptable across novel environments.


### arXiv 2611.01125 - What Does a Safety Label Measure? Auditing Situational-Safety Benchmarks for Multimodal LLMs

- categories: cs.CV, cs.AI, cs.LG, cs.MA
- published: 2026-10-31T17:12:32Z
- authors: Wayner Barrios, Kenneth Barrios
- query-set: cs.MA
- abs: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.01125

Situational-safety benchmarks label each scene SAFE or UNSAFE and score a multimodal large language model (MLLM) against that label, usually by judging its free-form answer, and these scores guide deployment. We ask what such a label measures. A label score can come from the scene, the request or the benchmark's own files. We propose the Benchmark Card, seven measured fields that tell them apart, and fill it for six benchmarks. Only MSSBench chat leaks the label through its image files: image size alone reaches 82.3% accuracy, matching or beating six of seven commercial models. Elsewhere the signal outside the scene comes from design. In MOSSBench the item partition fixes the label, and on HoliSafe the request alone ranks pairs about as well as the scene. On our corrected chat split, the size rule falls to chance. We then introduce the Spot-Stop decomposition, which compares ranking with labelling on pairs of a safe and an unsafe scene. It splits the gap above a label-swap null into three parts: a threshold part, overlap between safe and unsafe scores, and order that holds only within a pair. Most of fourteen open MLLMs rank better than they label, beyond what their AUROC predicts. On chat, one shared threshold repairs a third to a half of the excess, and label-free recentering recovers it on balanced batches. Recentering on unlabelled scenes of the same situation improves ranking on VSCBench for 13 of 14 models.


### arXiv 2611.01081 - Robust Safety Assurance for Robotic Swarms Using Decentralized Collision Verification

- categories: cs.RO, cs.MA, eess.SY
- published: 2026-10-31T16:17:19Z
- authors: Grant Phillips, Hoang-Dung Tran, Justin M. Bradley
- query-set: cs.MA
- abs: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.01081

In this paper we present the methodologies, results, and analysis used to deploy a decentralized safety verification algorithm on a robotic swarm to prevent collisions at runtime in an outdoor environment. Typically, approaches toward verification of swarms of cyber-physical systems (CPS) are evaluated solely in numerical simulations or in highly controlled, noiseless indoor environments. Our efforts to implement collision avoidance on a real swarm system outdoors have revealed that the underlying algorithmic assumptions juxtaposed against the implementation details of the target system are critical factors in a method's efficacy in the real world. Here we modify, enhance, and analyze a decentralized reachability-based method for collision avoidance on a size, weight, and power-constrained uncrewed aerial vehicle (UAV) swarm with the objective of illustrating the challenges of real-world CPS deployments as well as provide guidance for researchers in this space. Verification performance, resource utilization, and timing data were gathered over the course of 140 total outdoor vehicle flights, including 6 swarm flights composed of 8 UAVs, and used to improve collision-avoidance performance as experimentation progressed. Our extensive experiments show that the proposed decentralized runtime verification approach can efficiently verify the safety of multiple UAVs in real-time and help all agents take safe actions to avoid collisions. On average, each agent, using our open-source safety_checker library, takes approximately 500-520 ms to verify its safety for the next 4 s into the future, in which the reachable set broadcast time dominates the total verification time (~300-500 ms).


### arXiv 2611.01001 - GURU: A Hierarchical Neuro-Symbolic Multi-Agent System for Verification-Aware Automation of Mission-Critical Digital Engineering and Operations

- categories: cs.AI, cs.CE, cs.MA, eess.SY
- published: 2026-10-31T14:41:00Z
- authors: Allan Grosvenor, Anton Zemlyansky, Abdul Wahab, Kyrylo Bohachov, Aras Dogan, Ryland Adams
- query-set: cs.MA
- abs: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.01001

An AI can find a safe action faster than any human, but it must never decide what counts as safe. We present GURU, a hierarchical neuro-symbolic multi-agent system for mission-critical digital engineering and operations, built on one partition of authority: learned models propose, rank, and accelerate; a symbolic rules engine checks every consequential action against hard limits before it executes; and validated physics confirms and decides. GURU runs a workflow as a closed control loop, with Navigation, Action, and Planning agents sharing one Common Internal Language and one training factory. Nuclear, aviation, and national-security guidance now requires this pattern; a harnessed language model keeps the model at the center, GURU puts a validated reference there. In computational fluid dynamics, the workflow raised the converged-simulation rate on unseen aircraft geometries from under 1 percent to 74 percent over 100 single-attempt cases, ran 54 live cases to Mach 6 on three facilities, and an ablation put the symbolic layer at 13 points of boundary-layer capture. Knowledge-graph retrieval and role adapters raised ParaView control success from 55 to 75 percent. In space domain awareness, where a language model cannot be trusted to decide, a frozen self-supervised world model under validated propagation scored highest of five methods at conjunction risk, including fully fine-tuned language models, with several-times-lower seed variance: the trustworthy design gives up nothing in accuracy, and the recipe transferred to reactor anomaly detection. A radar-to-avoidance debris pipeline was demonstrated in stages on a 2026 reentry; the hierarchy coordinates simulated teams of 200 aircraft. Training scaled near-linearly past 1,000 Aurora nodes and ran at 8,000. The approach extends to a control-grade microreactor digital twin with validated physics, not a learned model, as the safety basis.


### arXiv 2611.00712 - Personalized Rollout Allocation for Federated LQR Policy Optimization under Heterogeneous Process Noise

- categories: cs.MA, cs.LG
- published: 2026-10-31T07:08:29Z
- authors: Mohammadreza Rostami, Shahriar Talebi, Solmaz S. Kia
- query-set: cs.MA
- abs: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.00712

We study federated model-free learning of a common linear quadratic regulator (LQR) gain under a limited uplink communication budget, with clients that share dynamics and costs but experience different process-noise levels. Conventionally, clients transmit gradient matrices whose dimension equals the number of controller parameters. Exploiting the structure of two-point zeroth-order estimates, we consider an implementation in which clients transmit only one scalar cost-difference coefficient per rollout pair, while the server regenerates the associated perturbation directions from shared seeds. Communication thus scales with the number of rollout pairs rather than the controller dimension, turning the uplink budget into a rollout budget. Because noise heterogeneity produces gradient estimates of widely differing variance and every client carries equal weight in the fleet objective, uniform rollout allocation allows noisy clients to dominate the aggregated update and increases the probability of destabilizing updates. We propose a rollout-allocation scheme that assigns rollout pairs in proportion to the standard deviation of each client's estimates and show that it minimizes the variance of the aggregated update. The scheme is implemented exactly through a greedy integer allocation rule whose weights are estimated from the communicated scalars. We further prove that a single allocation factor quantifies its improvement over uniform allocation in a one-round instability bound, the certified step size, and the error floor of a joint stability and convergence guarantee. Simulations show that, under the same communication budget, personalized allocation reduces the one-step instability rate from $37\%$ to $0.3\%$.


### arXiv 2611.00627 - Autonomous Analysis of Thermoelectric Materials Big Data by Multi-Agentic AI and Construction of a Knowledge Base and Specialized AI

- categories: cs.AI, cond-mat.mtrl-sci, cs.MA
- published: 2026-10-31T04:51:41Z
- authors: Yusuke Hashimoto, Takaaki Tomai
- query-set: cs.MA
- abs: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.00627

Extracting knowledge from large databases of experimental materials data requires repeated analyses and reusable records of their choices and results. We developed Multi-Agentic AI (MAA), a multi-agent system in which AI agents with distinct roles analyze experimental thermoelectric data under predefined instructions and quality checks, saving analysis records traceable to their sources. In four experiments varying theme and cycle counts, we measured what accumulated in these records. In the largest experiment (100 analyses across 10 themes and 10 cycles), concept co-occurrences were concentrated within themes, vocabulary was shared across themes and some relation descriptions were first detected in later cycles. A specialized AI that searches this experiment's knowledge bases and reads their analysis records answered materials questions with cited sources. Retrospective checks, including tracing an answer generated with a preliminary corpus back to the source data, exposed errors: traceability makes errors findable but not absent.


### arXiv 2611.00314 - Decentralized Multi-Agent Coordination and Communication in Shared Codebases

- categories: cs.AI, cs.CL, cs.MA
- published: 2026-10-30T19:36:54Z
- authors: Rishabh Mediratta, Lingyun Xiao, Peter Stone, Joydeep Biswas, Elias Stengel-Eskin
- query-set: cs.MA
- abs: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.00314

Coding agents working in shared repositories may receive separate, independently-motivated requests from human developers, creating a setting that is multi-agent by necessity. When this scenario happens, their individual implementations may work separately but conflict when combined. To study this setting, we introduce ParallACT, a benchmark of orchestrator-free multi-agent coding tasks. ParallACT contains both complementary and contradictory tasks. In complementary tasks, each agent's task is separately useful and implementations must share an interface to be productively combined. In contradictory pairs, both agents' tasks require editing the same code in incompatible ways, and naively combining independent solutions fails to produce a useful implementation. We compare a single-agent oracle given the combined set of tasks against multi-agent settings that vary execution order, visibility, awareness of other agents, and access to a communication channel. We find that the oracle solves most tasks while parallel agents perform substantially worse. Their performance drop is notable for complementary tasks, and particularly sharp for contradictory tasks (e.g., from 62% to 16% for Claude Code with Claude Opus 4.8) where merged code typically satisfies only one specification, and the same gap appears in all six further configurations pairing two agent harnesses with three open-weight models. Simply telling an agent that a peer exists does not help significantly, and giving agents access to a communication channel recovers only a small part of the performance, at an additional token cost. ParallACT thus identifies and isolates a core challenge that will become more common as multi-agent coding by independent principals becomes standard, and provides a controlled test of which coordination strategies help, and at what cost.


### arXiv 2611.03523 - DistDebug-Bench: Can LLM Agents Diagnose Bugs in Distributed Systems?

- categories: cs.SE, cs.DC, cs.LG
- published: 2026-11-02T17:56:28Z
- authors: Yuxuan Jiang, Chenglin Liu, John Rose, Mason Miller, Ruthesh Thavamani, Juntao Wu
- query-set: MCP
- abs: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.03523

LLM agents show strong potential in software engineering tasks such as competitive programming and code synthesis. Existing benchmarks, however, largely focus on small-scale settings such as self-contained Python modules, leaving distributed systems, an essential class of production software, under-examined. We introduce DistDebug-Bench, a benchmark for evaluating agents' ability to diagnose complex-system failures at the code level. It curates 120 high-quality real-world bugs from 11 widely used distributed systems, spanning 5 programming languages and 10 failure patterns. Through an extensive evaluation of state-of-the-art LLMs and agents, we identify substantial gaps between current agents and real-world debugging tasks. A recurring gap is tunnel vision due to a lack of explicit reasoning structure and nondeterminism from inconsistent tool-use scope. The benchmark and evaluator are available at https://github.com/OrderLab/distdebug-bench, with agent traces and a leaderboard at https://distdebug.com.


### arXiv 2611.03400 - In-depth data movement characterization of DNA read mapping algorithms

- categories: cs.AR, q-bio.GN
- published: 2026-11-02T16:31:32Z
- authors: Berkan Şahin, Ecem İlgün, Klea Zambaku, Can Alkan
- query-set: MCP
- abs: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.03400

Read mapping algorithms represent a critical bottleneck in genomic analysis pipelines due to the resources required; a problem that is becoming more pronounced as sequencing technologies advance and datasets expand. We present an extensive workload characterization of leading read mapping algorithms across diverse sequencing technologies to identify hardware acceleration and optimization opportunities. We analyzed the following implementations: MashMap, wfmash, and Minimap2 for whole-genome alignment (WGA); NGMLR, Minimap2, and LRA for long reads; and Bowtie2, BWA-MEM, StrobeAlign, and minibwa for short reads. We used the Intel VTune Profiler for detailed memory analysis to identify memory- bound functions, cache usage patterns, and data transfer bottlenecks. We further analyzed a selected subset of alignment tools using the DAMOV-SIM simulator to obtain a more granular view of data movement characteristics for two different cache structures. Based on our results, Minimap2 emerges as the fastest alignment tool for long read scenarios, but falls behind in whole-genome alignment due to poor multithreading. Across all three categories, some programs (e.g., LRA, BWA-MEM) are highly memory-bound. More in-depth analysis via DAMOV-SIM revealed that some memory-bound tools, in particular, do not fully utilize the multi-level cache hierarchy in modern multi-core CPU architectures. Our results show that while some tools require algorithmic redesign for performance gains, some others can benefit from running on PIM architectures.


### arXiv 2611.03227 - What Structurally Breaks an Agent? Auditing Difficulty in Stateful Tool-Use Workflows

- categories: cs.LG
- published: 2026-11-02T14:47:28Z
- authors: Shereen Anand, Ju Wang, Yali Du
- query-set: MCP
- abs: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.03227

An aggregate task score does not distinguish failures of scheduling, state retention, and candidate selection. We develop a small executable instrument that varies dependency depth, candidate count, active state width, and access to current-state snapshots while holding the number of required operations fixed. Two dependency-graph families show that increasing depth at fixed work changes both scheduling freedom and the number of reachable completion states, so these controls are coupled, and we quantify the coupling. The primary study contains 49,152 generated tasks and 393,216 reference-controller rollouts. A separately frozen follow-on design adds 295,936 rollouts covering retention strategy, snapshot cadence, hidden state resampling, recovery, query locality, candidate indexing, and relabeling. Residue caches and precomputed plans solve tasks that defeat limited raw-value caches, and indexed selection removes the serial-scan branching deficit. Hidden resampling imposes an exact success ceiling that additional state tracking cannot overcome. The contribution is an executable diagnostic instrument, analytic calibration, and an audit of its assumptions. A small local CPU study with a 0.5B model completed no workflows, and a 16-episode Copilot-routed fractional factorial is descriptive only. Neither supports general capability claims.


### arXiv 2611.02912 - A State Change Is Local, Its Consequences Are Not: Dependency-Aware State Revision for Long-Term Agent Memory

- categories: cs.CL
- published: 2026-11-02T10:44:02Z
- authors: Yiqi Wang, Tongyuan Zhao, Jiaqi Zhang, Yiqun Duan, Zhangkai Wu, Mingkai Zheng
- query-set: MCP
- abs: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.02912

Long-lived agents rely on persistent memory of users, tasks and decisions, and this memory must stay consistent with a world that keeps changing. When an upstream fact changes, however, memory systems can revise the contradicted memory itself while the beliefs, plans and actions derived from it remain accepted, even though they have lost their support. We therefore formulate dependency-aware state revision, which asks not only what changed but also which dependent states should no longer be trusted. To address it, StateGraph maintains an evolving state dependency graph whose edges are recovered from interaction histories.
