Its core design is counterfactual verification: each candidate edge is tested by asking whether the dependent state would survive the loss of its premise, which separates support dependence from semantic relatedness, and revision then invalidates a dependent state only when a strict dependency fails and no independent support path survives. To test this design, we introduce StateChangeBench, a controlled benchmark of 50 author-constructed cases annotated with cascade depth, hard negatives and action consequences. On it, StateGraph's Impact-F1 margin over a CUPMem reimplementation is 6.9 percentage points at depth 1 and 26.0 at depth 3+. On public long-term memory benchmarks, its point estimates are comparable to or higher than those of the evaluated baselines on most datasets. These results provide initial evidence that explicit dependency tracking can improve selective memory revision under changing conditions.


### arXiv 2611.02758 - Learning to Adjudicate: Verifier-Guided Multi-Agent Reasoning for E-Commerce Relevance

- categories: cs.IR
- published: 2026-11-02T08:39:57Z
- authors: Shi Shu, Hao Chen, Baopu Qiu, Chao Wei, Weiru Zhang, Xiu Li
- query-set: MCP
- abs: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.02758

Relevance modeling is fundamental to e-commerce search, determining how well retrieved products satisfy the needs expressed in a user's query. Accurate judgments require interpreting user intent, comparing product attributes, and applying business-specific matching criteria. Current methods based on large language models (LLMs) leverage contextual understanding and reasoning, focusing on incorporating domain-specific context and enhancing reasoning performance. Despite these advances, difficult query-product pairs remain challenging when evidence from different perspectives supports conflicting conclusions. A single reasoning pass may fail to reconcile these conflicts and ensure that decisive evidence guides the final judgment. To address this challenge, we propose Collaborative Adjudication with Debate and Replay Experience (CADRE), a framework for evidence-based adjudication in e-commerce relevance assessment. CADRE organizes three expert agents sharing a frozen backbone to analyze product attributes, user intent, and business rules in separate contexts, while a trainable Verifier assesses their supporting evidence against the applicable matching criteria to determine relevance. Under expert disagreement, a Verifier-guided debate elicits targeted clarification and revision of disputed interpretations before final adjudication. We optimize the Verifier through multi-turn group relative policy optimization and adapt successful-experience replay so that trajectories yielding correct terminal labels can be reused alongside fresh rollouts in subsequent updates. For efficient online serving, we use CADRE as an offline teacher to distill its relevance judgments into a lightweight student model. Offline evaluations and online A/B tests on an e-commerce search advertising platform demonstrate that CADRE improves relevance classification and key business and relevance satisfaction metrics.


### arXiv 2611.02725 - AffToM: Benchmarking Recursive Affective Theory of Mind in Dynamic Multi-Party Interactions

- categories: cs.AI
- published: 2026-11-02T08:19:23Z
- authors: Jinpeng Hu, Yiduo Jin, Erqiang Wang, Jia Li, Jingjing Wu, Peipei Song
- query-set: MCP
- abs: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.02725

Understanding emotion in realistic social interactions requires more than recognizing facial expressions or assigning affective labels. The same event can elicit different emotions depending on what each participant knows, what they believe others know or feel, and how these nested mental states evolve over time. Existing benchmarks largely study higher-order epistemic Theory of Mind (ToM) and multimodal emotion reasoning in isolation, leaving recursive affective reasoning under asymmetric and dynamically changing information underexplored. Therefore, we introduce AffToM, a benchmark for affective ToM with 1,922 test pairs and 19,759 training pairs. AffToM evaluates Individual Affective Reasoning (IAR), which infers emotions from events and socio-cognitive context, and Multi-party Affective Reasoning (MPAR), which recursively tracks participants' nested beliefs and emotions as new evidence emerges. We develop an event-driven multi-agent generation pipeline grounded in dynamic character interaction graphs that explicitly capture event sequences, interpersonal relations, and mental-state updates. Furthermore, we propose AffToM-R1, a two-stage post-training framework combining supervised fine-tuning with perspective-aware rubric-guided reinforcement learning. Experiments show that MPAR remains challenging, with even the strongest evaluated MLLM achieving less than 60% accuracy, while AffToM-R1 improves MPAR accuracy by 13.38 percentage points over its base model. Our dataset is available at: https://github.com/MindIntLab-HFUT/AffToM.


### arXiv 2611.02698 - GeoGuesser Bench: Benchmarking Vision-Language Agents on Interactive Geolocation

- categories: cs.AI, cs.LG
- published: 2026-11-02T07:50:57Z
- authors: Huanzhi Mao, Yichuan Wang, Michael Koo, Wenyuan Huang, Huy Doan, Jinghan Xia
- query-set: MCP
- abs: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.02698

Vision-language model (VLM) agents that act in the open world need two abilities: world knowledge to interpret what they see, and exploration to find what they cannot yet see. We study how agents combine the two through interactive visual geolocation. GeoGuesser Bench contains 282 tasks in an offline Street View environment where agents rotate, zoom, and move between real panoramas. Its three task types shift the balance from knowledge to exploration: agents (1) report the coordinates of their starting location, (2) name the enclosing street, city, state, or country, or (3) find, read, and count nearby objects. The coordinate tasks in (1) also include 42 real GeoGuessr World Championship rounds, whose expert guesses provide a human reference. We evaluate 23 VLMs: the best accuracy is 62.5% on place naming and 35.0% on object finding, and the best agent averages 4352.4 GeoGuessr points on the championship rounds, against 4770.3 for the better expert in each round. Beyond these headline numbers, world knowledge and exploration are only weakly correlated across models: agents that localize well do not necessarily explore well. To understand where agents fail, we categorize their errors into vision, navigation, and tool-use failures. Further ablations show that the strongest agents, such as Claude Opus 4.8, still perform well with limited views, while weaker agents degrade; customizing the environment thus adjusts task difficulty. The environment's verifiable rewards and restorable state also support training, and our Planner-Worker-Compressor harness cuts planner tokens by 57-61% by compacting the interaction history. We hope GeoGuesser Bench and its environment serve as a high-quality foundation for simulating real-world exploration at scale.


### arXiv 2611.02611 - Where Does MCP Security End? A Three-Tier Mitigation Mapping Framework for the OWASP MCP Top 10

- categories: cs.CR
- published: 2026-11-02T06:36:08Z
- authors: Christopher Krakauer, Andreas Schaad
- query-set: MCP
- abs: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.02611

The Model Context Protocol (MCP) has become a leading standard for connecting Large Language Models (LLMs) to external tools, software, and data sources. MCP architecture favors ease of integration over security, blending the data and control planes into a single natural language channel. This introduces confused deputy and prompt injection vulnerabilities that cannot be completely mitigated in the software development lifecycle (SDLC) of an MCP server. Structured security guidance for server developers has been outpaced by this adoption trend, leaving them without a clear way to identify where vulnerabilities can be addressed at the server layer and where architectural constraints create hard limits. This paper presents a framework based on OWASP MCP Top 10 mapped to three architectural tiers, developed using Design Science Research (DSR). Vulnerabilities are classified as server-primary, gateway-primary, or protocol-primary applying a cascading decision tree following four questions, resulting in reproducible tier assignments and confidence levels independent of who applies it. The framework was validated against an enterprise-grade Managed File Transfer (MFT) MCP server built in Go. Validation results showed that high-confidence server-tier controls worked as predicted, and low-confidence protocol-tier controls failed for architectural reasons beyond server implementation. The architectural ceiling encountered during practical validation is the main contribution and shows how secure-by-design development can reduce the MCP attack surface but not completely mitigate it.


### arXiv 2611.02581 - SMILE: A Structured Multi-Agent Clinical Reasoning for Liver Ultrasound Understanding

- categories: cs.CV, cs.CL, cs.LG
- published: 2026-11-02T06:05:38Z
- authors: Haris Ghafoor, Minjee Seo, Seonaeng cho, Keungmo Yang, Jaejun Lee, Kyungho Yoon
- query-set: MCP
- abs: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.02581

Automated liver ultrasound report generation improves the diagnosis of liver diseases by producing clinically structured descriptions of anatomical and disease-related findings. However, current generative vision-language models (VLMs) often rely on a single reasoning pathway that maps ultrasound appearance directly to textual descriptions. In addition to the inherent vision-language modality gap, this design is limited for liver ultrasound because disease interpretation is constrained by subtle visual evidence, heterogeneous disease patterns, and limited paired supervision. To address these limitations, we propose SMILE, a Structured Multi-agent clInicaL rEasoning framework that uses multiple complementary specialist agents to interpret ultrasound images and consolidates their outputs through a judge-guided consensus mechanism. Our approach employs three specialized agents: an image interpretation agent for morphological descriptions, a clinical-stage reasoning agent, and a visual question answering (VQA) agent for targeted queries. These agent responses are then aggregated and scrutinized by a judge VLM using a consensus filtering prompt to produce a unified, clinically coherent output. Across a public liver ultrasound dataset and an internal clinical dataset, SMILE consistently outperforms state-of-the-art VLMs, achieving up to 19.0\% improvement in ROUGE-1 and 18.8\% improvement in clinical F1-score, while reaching VQA F1-scores of 0.82 and 0.65 on public and internal datasets, respectively. These results demonstrate the potential of SMILE for data-scarce and limited-supervision clinical settings. Our models, datasets, and code are available \href{https://github.com/CMME-Lab/SMILE}{here}.


### arXiv 2611.02527 - When Neither Side Confirms: Destructive Tool Approval Across MCP Servers and Hosts

- categories: cs.SE, cs.CR
- published: 2026-11-02T04:57:42Z
- authors: Junwei Yao, Zike Yang, Zhijun Wang, Yongxi Zhou, Wenbo Ye
- query-set: MCP
- abs: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.02527

LLM agents call tools that delete data or move money through the Model Context Protocol (MCP). The specification asks for a human who can deny tool calls but splits the mechanism between the server, which can annotate a tool or ask the user through the host (elicitation), and the host application, which decides whether to prompt. We measure both sides from source code. In a 1,000-repository sample of the official MCP registry, an estimated 90% of destructive tools (95% CI 82-95) have no server-side confirmation, and most servers that elicit proceed, or fall back to a flag the model can set, when the host cannot relay the question. Of 41 popular open-source hosts and agent SDKs, 32 have no approval step by default, including 16 of 25 applications. Pairing sampled destructive tools flagged by a rule-based extractor with these applications, 60.7% of pairs have no enforced human approval by default. Through four agent SDKs, four of five low-cost models executed an injected deletion in 479 of 960 runs. If hosts prompted for every tool not annotated read-only or non-destructive, the unapproved share of these pairs would fall to 5.4%; universal elicitation support would leave 56.7%. Applications should therefore configure approval.


### arXiv 2611.02525 - Touching Projected Agents through the DAT Screen -System Design and Questions of Interaction Boundaries-

- categories: cs.HC, cs.AR, cs.RO, eess.SY
- published: 2026-11-02T04:51:34Z
- authors: Takaya Tamamoto, Tomoko Yonezawa
- query-set: MCP
- abs: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.02525

Touching a virtual agent involves more than detecting a finger on a display: the contact surface can deform and resist the user's push, and the agent can respond to that action. We describe the Desktop Amid Touch (DAT) screen, which combines spatial projection onto a semi-transparent, deformable mesh with touch sensing and motorized control of resistance to indentation. An initial prototype detected a single touch and estimated its position and depth from tension changes around a flexible projection screen. A subsequent prototype added motorized tension control to vary the screen's resistance to indentation. We describe the tension-controlled DAT implementation and its demonstrations, distinguishing what has been observed from what remains an open design question. These capabilities raise a design question: should touchable virtual agents respond as though they had preferences or boundaries, and under what conditions should such behavior be implemented on systems like DAT. The present paper describes a system and frames this question; it does not establish how users interpret an agent's refusal or consent.


### arXiv 2611.02517 - A Study on the Effects of Finger Movements on a Humanoid Hugging Robot

- categories: cs.RO, cs.AR, cs.HC
- published: 2026-11-02T04:36:46Z
- authors: Daigo Suhara, Tomoko Yonezawa
- query-set: MCP
- abs: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.02517

This study aimed to examine the impact of finger movements in hugging robots on users by experimentally investigating how the presence and patterns of such movements affect users psychologically. The results suggest that the presence and patterns of finger movements influence the sensations users seek from the robot; specifically, repetitive movements and asynchronous movements across multiple fingers tended to enhance these sensations. Additionally, simultaneous movement of all fingers and gentle hugging tended to evoke a greater sense of care. Conversely, no differences were observed regarding resistance to physical contact initiated by the robot.


### arXiv 2611.02511 - When Prediction Shapes the Future: A BlueSky Agenda for Reflexive Time Series Analysis

- categories: cs.AI, cs.LG
- published: 2026-11-02T04:30:54Z
- authors: Tao Xie, Yiqun Zhang, Yiu-ming Cheung
- query-set: MCP
- abs: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.02511

Time-series models are increasingly deployed in operational systems (e.g., inventory management and clinical care) where predictions trigger interventions that alter future observations, producing records we term AI-shaped trajectories. This sequence-prediction coupling deepens as such systems evolve into agents that autonomously predict, act, and remember across long horizons. Such AI-shaped trajectories support immediate performance monitoring, but turn ambiguous once reused as passive data. An algorithm may then misread a prediction-prevented stockout as naturally stable demand, or an averted medical crisis as a false alarm. This ambiguity then propagates into everything built on the record, from retraining and agent memory to downstream predictive tasks. As a result, systems may internalize these intervention effects as natural phenomena, drifting further from the truth at every closed-loop cycle. In this Blue Sky paper, we advocate an agenda for reflexive time-series analysis that shifts the analytical object from the intervention-agnostic sequence to the prediction-action-observation loop. The agenda requires closed-loop provenance to track the operational context (e.g., predictions, policies, and actions), sequence identification to estimate how those actions affect future values under explicit causal assumptions, and closed-loop evaluation to separate intrinsic process prediction from policy-driven effects. This foundation is essential for integrating autonomous agents into such loops at scale, enabling the joint learning of intervention effects and intrinsic dynamics for robust temporal representations.


### arXiv 2611.02391 - Late Autumn: LLM Agents Specialize a General Multi-Robot Planner for One Warehouse

- categories: cs.RO, cs.AI
- published: 2026-11-02T02:04:21Z
- authors: Yimin Tang, Han Zhang, Shao-Hung Chan, Erdem Bıyık, Sven Koenig, Jingkai Chen
- query-set: MCP
- abs: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.02391

No algorithm is best on all problems, but a real warehouse is a narrow problem: its layout and processes rarely change. Operators still run one general multi-robot planner at many sites, because experts cannot tune it for each one. In this work, which we call Late Autumn, we explore whether LLM coding agents running autonomous research can specialize a general planner for one site, trading generality for throughput, much as post-training adapts a pretrained LLM to one task. We study six campaigns on a lifelong planner that combines task assignment with multi-agent path finding, on a floor modeled on an existing automated warehouse. We find that (1) the agents raise throughput by 22--60%, and the gains hold on unseen seeds; (2) they rediscover known MAPF mechanisms, such as corridor-exit priorities, local guidance, and rollouts, and independent campaigns find the same bottleneck; (3) the gains largely carry over to another layout, a larger fleet, and other service times, shrink with a smaller fleet, and mostly vanish under an outbound-heavy demand mix; and (4) each final patch (0.7k--2.4k lines) is a chain of scored commits with a median step of 27 lines, so experts can review it, and review finds problems that the score misses; some patches multiply planner compute by over 100x. LLM agents can therefore turn each site's bias into extra throughput at little engineering cost, with reviewable patches; we expect experts to shift toward designing insightful metrics and fast simulators that mirror the real problem.


### arXiv 2611.02368 - Beyond Accuracy: How Quantization Affects Coding Agents

- categories: cs.CL, cs.AI
- published: 2026-11-02T01:30:27Z
- authors: Areej Mehboob, Muhammad Ali Shafique, Muhammad Usman Qadeer, Hamza Farooq
- query-set: MCP
- abs: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.02368

Agents are increasingly used for coding tasks, but the inference cost of large language models (LLMs) limits where they can run. Quantization reduces model size, but its effects on coding agents that execute and revise programs remain underexplored. We compare BF16 with 4-bit AWQ and GPTQ for Qwen2.5-Coder-Instruct (7B, 14B, and 32B) and Devstral-Small-2507 (24B) on OJBench coding problems across three seeds. We detect no statistically significant accuracy loss for any of the coding agents. However, final accuracy can conceal substantial behavioral changes: AWQ reduces Qwen-7B one-shot solves from 17 to 0 while increasing loop-based solves from 10 to 25. All Qwen-32B successes involve tool use, whereas Devstral rarely makes a valid executor call. Failure profiles also differ: Qwen-32B produces far more compilation errors than the smaller Qwen models, rising slightly under quantization, while Devstral's failures shift significantly from runtime errors toward wrong answers and timeouts. These findings indicate that quantization robustness in coding agents should be assessed through solution trajectories, tool use, and failure modes, in addition to final accuracy.


### arXiv 2611.02361 - Agent Memory as Rate-Limited Filtering: Explicit State Outlasts Long Context Beyond a Reader-Dependent Horizon

- categories: cs.LG
- published: 2026-11-02T01:09:24Z
- authors: Tianhao Guo, Chongyao Guo
- query-set: MCP
- abs: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.02361

Can a language model that reads the full history of an access log say whether a service may act now? On an executable benchmark of grants, withdrawals, reinstatements, expiries, groups and change windows under a known policy, scored on pairs of histories that differ only in the order or one field of the deciding events, open models of 7 to 32 billion parameters fall from 72 to 86 percent after eight or sixteen events to 48 to 55 percent after 256, while reasoning-trained 32B models reading the current state reach 96 and 98 percent. A reasoning model of 120 billion parameters trails the state by four and seven points at 256 and 512 events and by about ten at 1,024, so the length beyond which explicit state outlasts long context grows with the reader. We treat agent memory as recursive filtering. The decision depends on the history only through a state whose size is set by the number of tracked keys. A store holding fewer component records than the state forgets on a time scale set by its capacity, and a reader model predicts its accuracy within 0.02 on average in a registered test. Retaining log lines without regard to time order errs with probability approaching one half, a bound that is attained. A filter in which a model parses each line and an explicit rule applies the updates answers 80 to 89 percent at 256 events and beats relevance retrieval, model-kept ledgers, a reimplementation of StateMem and the Mem0 library. A retriever that selects at query time the lines the queried state depends on matches it at 256 events on the first domain, so what decides is selection by the state's dependencies, which the filter performs when writing, without the query. Applying parsed updates themselves, the models fall to 58 and 59 percent. A frozen replication on a second domain with fresh wording reproduces the horizon.


### arXiv 2611.02332 - LEANGUARD: Lean-Verified Guardrails for Tool-Using Agents

- categories: cs.AI, cs.CL
- published: 2026-11-02T00:26:48Z
- authors: Sreehari Sankar, Debargha Ganguly, Vikash Singh, Mohsen Hariri, Weicong Chen, Yanyan Zhang
- query-set: MCP
- abs: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.02332

Tool-using language-model agents often execute actions that their domain policies forbid. We present LeanGuard, a runtime that checks every proposed tool call against a policy written in Lean4 before the call reaches the environment. Policies combine state predicates with scoped past-time temporal operators over the execution history, evaluation is fail-closed, and the decision procedure is proved equivalent to a LeanLTL semantics. A denied call can be corrected in two ways. With feedback, the agent receives the violated rule and a hint; with proof-guided repair, Lean metaprogramming searches for a nearby call that preserves the user's intent and proves that the policy admits it, without the model. On Tau-Bench with five Qwen models (0.8B-27B), unguarded agents execute at least 33 calls that the Lean policy forbids, while LeanGuard executes none; with feedback, task success does not change significantly. On DTap, LeanGuard reduces direct prompt-injection success from 57.9% to 5.9%, and in telecom, where benign tasks need no writes, benign success is unchanged. Feedback reduces repeated blocked calls and raises task success for four of five models over a bare denial. On replayed denials, repair proves an admissible edit for 88 of 252 denials caused by argument values, in a median of 2,ms.


### arXiv 2611.02322 - Fast Fuel-Optimal Constrained Impulsive Control with Application to Distributed Spacecraft

- categories: math.OC
- published: 2026-11-02T00:14:22Z
- authors: Matthew Hunter, Simone D'Amico
- query-set: MCP
- abs: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.02322

This work extends a reachable set theory approach for fuel-optimal control to incorporate convex and non-convex state, time, and magnitude constraints. The solver is designed from a new set of optimality conditions, derived by reformulating a single reachable set into multiple time-ordered, step-wise reachable set waypoint sub-problems, where a waypoint is the state reached after a given maneuver. Non-convex constraints are satisfied at each waypoint, preserving convexity at each sub-problem without needing to convexify the full control problem, enabling the computationally efficient identification of constrained optimal maneuvers, and guaranteeing a user-defined bound on constrained cost. To produce an optimal maneuver plan, the solver rolls out constrained trajectories until the remaining unconstrained solution adheres to the problem constraints. The fuel-optimal control problem considered by this work is broadly applicable to many fields and particularly significant to Distributed Space Systems (DSS). Multi-agent guidance and propulsive control techniques, often for small spacecraft at close separations, require guarantees of collision avoidance, efficient maneuvering to preserve a restricted fuel payload, and numerically-simple algorithms functional under on-board computational processing limitations. The proposed solver offers both user-defined bounds on constrained maneuver optimality and enhanced computational efficiency to directly address these challenges, and these capabilities are demonstrated comparatively against baseline solvers from the literature through Monte Carlo analysis on a variety of relevant DSS scenarios. Overall, this work enables the benefits of reachable set theory, namely computational efficiency and provable optimality, to be extended to control problems of higher fidelity.


### arXiv 2611.02310 - Detect, Repair and Audit: A Hierarchical Multi-Agent Defense Against Adversarial Attacks in Energy Forecasting

- categories: cs.CR
- published: 2026-11-02T00:00:16Z
- authors: Zewei Shi, Ruoxi Sun, Minhui Xue, Feng Liu, Hua Ma, Xingliang Yuan
- query-set: MCP
- abs: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.02310

Accurate electricity-demand forecasting is essential for the modern energy infrastructure. Recently, Times-Series Large Language Models (TS-LLMs) have emerged as a promising approach to forecasting more accurate demand from historical consumption data in zero- and few-shot settings, helping energy operators effectively plan supply and allocate energy resources. However, these models are vulnerable to compromised sensor inputs that can distort forecasts and lead to erroneous market orders or unnecessary load shedding. Existing defenses focus primarily on end-device forecasting, overlooking the distinct data-drift, latency, and audit requirements across energy infrastructure. To address this gap, we extract from major energy standards and regulatory frameworks a three-layer conceptual model that captures the security postures for each layer. Guided by this model, we develop a Multi-Agent Reinforcement Learning (MARL) detector coupled with signal repair at local control centers, designed to mitigate distributional data drift through complementary feature-based detection and clean-reference-calibrated repair while meeting latency budgets. We further deploy an evidence-grounded LLM auditing agent with human-in-the-loop validation for audit-critical Virtual Power Plants (VPPs). In our evaluation, the best MARL configuration achieves an F1 score above 0.91 across all dataset-attack pairs, while signal repair reduces adversarial-to-clean input Relative Squared Error (RSE) by up to 83%. The few-shot auditor detects 77.78% of attacks in the Sherlock dataset. On the evaluated hardware, detection and repair require 7.95 ms on average, with a worst-observed latency of 14.50 ms, remaining within the local-center latency budget informed by IEEE 1646.


### arXiv 2611.02286 - Toward Agentic AI-Ready Workflows in Built Environment Organizations: Knowledge Structures and Interoperability Challenges

- categories: cs.AI
- published: 2026-11-01T23:29:55Z
- authors: Ahmet Bahaddin Ersoz, Justin McHenry, Yevgeniya Kovalchuk, Edward Barrett, Mandana Sarey Khanie
- query-set: MCP
- abs: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.02286

The rise of agentic artificial intelligence (AI) in the built environment is shifting attention from isolated AI tools toward autonomous systems that reason, plan, and act across project and organizational workflows. The effectiveness of such systems depends not only on large language model capabilities, but also on the readiness of heterogeneous data environments across the Architecture, Engineering, and Construction (AEC) sector. These environments combine structured sources, such as CAD and BIM models, schedules, cost databases, and asset registers, with unstructured ones, including technical reports, design narratives, planning and policy documents, contracts, meeting minutes, and risk registers. This paper reviews how built environment data can be organized to support agentic AI-ready workflows, focusing on knowledge structures, interoperability, and access and integration mechanisms such as retrieval-augmented generation (RAG) and the Model Context Protocol (MCP). Drawing on existing literature, it examines approaches proposed to organize structured and unstructured data and considers how such data can be contextualized and made accessible to AI agents in a reliable manner. The paper synthesizes current perspectives, highlights challenges around fragmentation and interoperability, and identifies open questions for future research on AI-compatible information management in the built environment.


### arXiv 2611.02269 - Qubrio: High-Performance Quantum Compilation via Multi-Agent LLM Collaboration

- categories: quant-ph, cs.AI
- published: 2026-11-01T22:49:33Z
- authors: Jixuan Ruan, Zhuo Cui, Zhengding Hu, Zhongkai Yu, Xiang Fang, Yue Guan
- query-set: MCP
- abs: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.02269

Large Language Models (LLMs) offer an adaptable alternative to traditional quantum compilation heuristics, which bottleneck progress by demanding costly manual redesigns whenever hardware evolves. However, naive single-pass LLM approaches fail under the unique challenges introduced by quantum compilation. We introduce Qubrio, a practical agentic framework that significantly reduces heuristic redesigns through a three-level decomposition. By (1) partitioning programs into sequential operation stages, (2) deploying specialized agents for placement, routing and optimization, and (3) using disaggregated feedback for precise error correction, our system reliably navigates complex physical constraints. Beyond serving as a direct compiler, our LLM framework also uncovers novel strategies that facilitate convoy-style shuttling, substantially enhancing hardware concurrency without sacrificing fidelity. We further integrate these strategies into the previous state-of-the-art (SOTA) compiler, recovering a significant fraction of the LLM's performance advantages. On realistic workloads, our compiler achieves significant improvements in hardware runtime and program fidelity over the SOTA baseline PowerMove, seamlessly adapting to new hardware capabilities. Qubrio features an open user interface for practical use, and its code is publicly available at https://github.com/crosaa/Qubrio.


### arXiv 2611.02240 - When to Probe and When to Act: Clarity-Aware Risk-Adaptive Control for Interactive Driving

- categories: cs.RO
- published: 2026-11-01T22:03:30Z
- authors: Youran Wang, Devodita Chakravarty, Yiwei Lyu
- query-set: MCP
- abs: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.02240

Safe navigation in interactive traffic requires autonomous vehicles to reason about unobservable human characteristics and the reciprocal nature of multi-agent interactions. Traditional architectures often decouple prediction from planning, leading to the "Frozen Robot Problem" or unsafe maneuvers under high ambiguity because they treat risk assessment and information acquisition as independent objectives. We argue that the notion of safety should not remain static but must instead dynamically co-evolve with the system's internal confidence: the robot should actively clarify ambiguity when necessary while also recognizing the diminishing returns of information, and naturally transition toward task execution. We introduce a unified belief-space Model Predictive Control (MPC) framework that couples multistep information planning with a clarity-adaptive Conditional Value-at-Risk (CVaR) barrier mechanism. By treating clarity, a scalar informational state, as an explicit planning variable, the controller co-designs its probing strategy with its safety specification, tightening conservatism under high ambiguity and relaxing constraints as confidence improves. We further provide a performance analysis showing that the cost of information seeking and the expected planning-cost gap to a controller that knows the true human characteristic are bounded by residual ambiguity and solver error. We evaluate the framework in lane-merging and unsignalized-intersection scenarios against representative interactive-planning baselines. Across 5,000 simulations, the proposed method achieves the highest success rates (94.6% and 93.2%) and the lowest collision rates (1.2% and 1.4%) among the compared methods, providing a principled foundation for robust decision-making in human-centric environments.


### arXiv 2611.02153 - Multi-Agent Collusion and the J-Space

- categories: cs.CL
- published: 2026-11-01T19:36:13Z
- authors: Marco Molinari, Gabriele Bettineschi
- query-set: MCP
- abs: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.02153

LLM agents are increasingly deployed in Multi-Agent Systems (MAS), and they introduce the risk of coordination among agents that might escape human oversight. The J-space has been used to uncover deception in single LLMs, but its use for uncovering and steering collusion between agents remains unexplored. We show, with interpretability experiments on DeepSeek-V4-Flash (284B) on the NARCBench collusion benchmark, that colluding agents' J-space holds the collusion itself: before replying, 86% of colluders represent misrepresentation, concealment or remorse. In a steganographic card-counting task, the receiver's J-space holds a hidden count, and causal interventions alter its count. We also introduce the Jacobian Pair Contrast (J-PACT), an unsupervised method for detecting collusion between agents. We evaluate J-PACT on four open-weight models: GPT-OSS-20B, Llama-3.3-70B-IT, Qwen3-32B, and DeepSeek-V4-Flash (284B). J-PACT retains 85% of the above-chance AUROC of supervised NARCBench probes, matches them on steganographic scenarios, and names the colluding pair up to its complement in 99% of detected runs.


### arXiv 2611.02104 - Consolidating Reasoning with Test-Time Training

- categories: cs.CL, cs.AI
- published: 2026-11-01T18:18:22Z
- authors: Haotian Wu, Zeming Chen, Hao Zhao, Antoine Bosselut
- query-set: MCP
- abs: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.02104

Effective multi-agent collaboration requires finalizing solutions from reasoning that is distributed across the individual attempts of many agents. However, useful intermediate reasoning can remain unexploited when collaboration is limited to voting on conclusions reached among individual agents, or aggregating long, noisy collections of reasoning trajectories in context. In this work, we propose TTT-Consolidate, a framework that uses multi-agent reasoning trajectories as adaptation data to improve a model's subsequent reasoning. TTT-Consolidate jointly consolidates trajectories into parametric representations through test-time training, then generates a new solution by conditioning on the consolidated information. We meta-learn how to consolidate using bi-level optimization at training time, where an inner loop encodes reasoning trajectories into a lightweight LoRA adapter, and an outer loop learns meta-parameters for the inner loop such that the consolidation is helpful for the model to synthesize a final solution. As no large-scale corpus of diverse reasoning trajectories exists for training aggregation methods, we construct the OpenParallelThinking corpus composed of 12,810 math problems paired with a mixture of trajectories from six LLMs. Evaluated on challenging math and STEM benchmarks, TTT-Consolidate outperforms the best aggregation baselines by up to 20 percentage points, even under matched compute and runtime settings, with the gap widening at larger model scales. TTT-Consolidate also recovers from unsuccessful individual trajectories more robustly, an ability that improves as the number of available (even incorrect) trajectories increases. Finally, we demonstrate that TTT-Consolidate benefits from both SFT and RL-based outer loop objectives. We will release TTT-Consolidate and OpenParallelThinking for use in future work on reasoning aggregation.


### arXiv 2611.02049 - Reinforcement Learning in High-Energy Physics: A Survey from Accelerator Control to Theory Exploration

- categories: hep-ex, cs.LG, hep-ph
- published: 2026-11-01T16:53:51Z
- authors: Luca Anzalone
- query-set: MCP
- abs: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.02049

Reinforcement learning (RL) is a machine learning paradigm in which an agent learns a decision-making policy by interacting with an environment, guided by a scalar reward rather than explicit supervision. Supervised and unsupervised learning have become indispensable tools in high-energy physics (HEP), but they learn from fixed datasets, whereas many HEP tasks are closed-loop decisions: the data an algorithm observes depend on its choices, the feedback is sparse or delayed, and the objective is often measured on a real machine or in a stochastic simulation, not by a differentiable model. This survey reviews the application of RL to such tasks: accelerator control and feedback, data quality monitoring, jet grooming, clustering, and representation, charged particle tracking, beyond-the-Standard-Model (BSM) and string model building, detector design, trigger control, analysis, and formal quantum field theory computation. It provides a self-contained introduction to the underlying RL methods, from model-free and model-based to multi-agent, offline, inverse, and bandit approaches, reviews each domain against its traditional methods, and summarizes the head-to-head comparisons. The applications range from policies deployed on real accelerators and evaluated on recorded collision data to exploratory studies in formal theory. RL is most advantageous for repeated tasks with a fast simulator, decisions inside fast control loops, operation under faults and corrupted measurements, physics-based rewards in place of labels, and sequential discrete constructions with a reusable policy, while Bayesian optimization, genetic algorithms, and differentiable methods remain competitive elsewhere. We conclude with the open challenges that recur across domains, including reward design, sim-to-real transfer, safety, the lack of shared benchmarks, and the statistical validity of policy-driven decisions.


### arXiv 2611.01977 - EvoRec: A Unified Self-Evolving Agentic Recommender with Dynamic Reflection Trees

- categories: cs.IR
- published: 2026-11-01T15:15:00Z
- authors: Mengting Li, Long Zhang, Hao Jiang, Sheng Yu, Fei Pan, Peng Jiang
- query-set: MCP
- abs: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.01977

Conventional recommender systems rely on fixed cascaded pipelines, limiting their ability to adapt to evolving user needs and feedback. Existing agentic recommenders introduce reasoning and tool use but often lack end-to-end decision control and systematic failure reflection. We propose EvoRec, a unified agentic recommendation framework that combines autonomous decision-making with reflection-driven self-evolution. A Need Agent infers user needs from long-term, short-term, and working memory. A Planning Agent adaptively allocates retrieval budgets between Behavior Continuation and Need Satisfaction, selects retrieval tools, and sets ranking-fusion weights. An Acting Executor executes the plan and records decision traces. Following user feedback, a Dynamic Reflection Tree identifies failures in need inference, planning, or previous repairs, proposes personalized corrections, and validates them through replay. Verified repairs update working memory and are distilled into reusable cross-user strategies, enabling continual improvement without parameter updates. Experiments on a Kuaishou local-services advertising dataset show a 15.7% Hit@50 improvement over the strongest baseline. Hierarchical reflection further improves Hit@50 by 13.1% among users with prior repairs. In a seven-day online A/B test, nearline EvoRec increases advertising revenue by 4.6% and CTCVR by 3.5%, with a 14.5% revenue gain for high-intent users. These results demonstrate the effectiveness of reflection-driven self-evolution in industrial-scale recommendation.


### arXiv 2611.01934 - TriPhysBench: Evaluating Physical Intelligence for Embodied Agents

- categories: cs.AI, cs.CV
- published: 2026-11-01T14:12:41Z
- authors: Zhongxiang Sun, Chenghao Yang, Haojie Ding, Boheng Zhang, Fan Yang, Qingsong Wen
- query-set: MCP
- abs: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.01934

Embodied agents need physical intelligence to understand their surroundings through physical perception, physical knowledge retrieval, and physical reasoning. These capabilities must work together to interpret events and make quantitative judgments. Existing benchmarks advance physical understanding and general-purpose tool use, yet the coordination of these capabilities in physical scenarios remains insufficiently characterized. Inspired by Popper's Three Worlds, we introduce TriPhysBench, a benchmark of 410 questions from 200 videos that brings physical perception, physical knowledge retrieval, and physical reasoning into a shared evaluation setting. Its measurement-grounded and knowledge-grounded subtasks impose complementary demands on obtaining observations and acquiring external specifications, then applying them to quantitative problems. Among six representative vision-language models, the best achieves an answer accuracy of only 50.38%, revealing limited performance on tasks that require these capabilities to work together. We further introduce TriPhys, a framework that learns a unified policy for physical perception, knowledge retrieval, and quantitative reasoning from complete tool-integrated trajectories. The policy uses intermediate reasoning to guide retrieval and computation, then incorporates tool feedback into subsequent physical inference. On 110 questions from videos excluded from training, TriPhys improves answer and solution scores across three Qwen configurations, with a 17.7% relative gain in answer accuracy for TriPhys-27B over its Qwen3.6-27B backbone. This work provides a benchmark and a learning pathway toward embodied agents that move beyond recognizing events to understanding and reasoning about the physical processes behind them.


### arXiv 2611.01926 - TAC-CoT: Tool-Empowered Implicit Chain-of-Thought via Interleaving Explicit Tokens

- categories: cs.CL
- published: 2026-11-01T14:05:15Z
- authors: Juseon Do, Sangmin Bae, Jeesu Jung, Hwanjun Song
- query-set: MCP
- abs: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.01926

LLM-based agentic systems are increasingly combined with CoT reasoning to solve tasks that require accurate reasoning, external knowledge, and multi-step deliberation. Yet, explicit CoT incurs substantial generation overhead, while implicit CoT, which replaces textual rationales with compact continuous tokens, does not naturally support the explicit communication interface required for agentic tool-use. We propose TAC-CoT, a tool-empowered adaptive implicit CoT framework that replaces explicit reasoning traces with compact latent reasoning segments while retaining textual tokens only for tool calls, observations, and final answers. This interleaved design enables agentic tool-use without verbalizing every intermediate reasoning step. Experiments on agentic math and QA benchmarks show that TAC-CoT improves accuracy by up to 8.8 points while reducing model forward passes by up to 6.8x. Furthermore, analyses show that TAC-CoT improves tool-use quality in both single- and multi-tool settings.


### arXiv 2611.01831 - MATE: Multi-Agent Traffic Engineering for Topology-Aware Distributed SRv6 Scheduling under Bursty Traffic

- categories: cs.NI
- published: 2026-11-01T12:00:59Z
- authors: Yuang Chen, Shuyi Liu, Chengdi Lu, Fengqian Guo, Hancheng Lu, Chang Wen Chen
- query-set: MCP
- abs: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.01831

As network traffic becomes increasingly dynamic and bursty, conventional centralized traffic engineering (TE) suffers from long control-loop delays caused by network-state aggregation, centralized computation, and policy distribution. In this paper, we propose MATE, a multi-agent TE method for proactive distributed scheduling in hybrid networks with partial deployment of Segment Routing over IPv6 (SRv6). MATE uses historical traffic stability to characterize the burst risk of each traffic demand and path sensitivity to quantify the ability of candidate paths to accommodate additional traffic, thereby enabling differentiated proactive scheduling tailored to heterogeneous traffic characteristics. MATE adopts an offline-centralized training and online-distributed execution architecture. During training, a shared global critic evaluates joint actions under a reward combining maximum link utilization (MLU) with variability-weighted path sensitivity, while counterfactual difference rewards provide agent-specific credit for actor learning. During online execution, edge agents generate traffic-splitting policies from local observations and apply them through SRv6, avoiding centralized optimization in each scheduling interval. Experiments on the China Environment for Network Innovations (CENI) testbed and simulations on the Sanet topology show that MATE outperforms a multi-agent reinforcement learning (MARL) ablation baseline without agent-specific credit assignment, COPE, and TEAL in most evaluated scenarios and approaches centralized FIGRET on measured traffic traces. On CENI-Abilene, MATE achieves MLU within 10% of the linear-programming optimum in up to 90.1% of test instances. MATE also achieves lower median normalized MLU than FIGRET in most tested failure scenarios, with mean neural-inference times of 2.05--3.14~ms.


### arXiv 2611.01718 - Delivered, Not Used: Evaluating Agent Memory at Its Point of Use

- categories: cs.AI
- published: 2026-11-01T09:09:43Z
- authors: Changran Xu, Zeju Li, Qiang Xu
- query-set: MCP
- abs: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.01718

Task success, retrieval quality, and cost do not show whether an LLM agent used a delivered memory or what followed from that use. We introduce MemUse, which evaluates memory at its point of use in recorded trajectories: each memory delivery is compared with what the agent could already see and followed through the agent's later actions and their consequences. An agentic judge rates each delivered entry's relevance, accuracy, novelty, influence, and observed effect; programmatic checks enforce complete reading of cited evidence, and fixed rules aggregate the ratings into verdicts linked to memory histories and delivery cost. On 8,000 SWE-bench Verified episodes from three memory systems, resolve rates stay within 0.4 percentage points of a no-memory baseline, yet MemUse shows that most delivered entries were irrelevant, most relevant entries went unused, and in two systems most deliveries restated content the agent could still see. It also surfaces effects that outcomes hide: 23.2\% of episodes with beneficial memory use still failed, and 5 of 16 with harmful use still succeeded.


### arXiv 2611.01700 - MemHarmBench: Evaluating Cross-Session Prompt Injection in Memory-Persistent AI Agents

- categories: cs.AI
- published: 2026-11-01T08:36:16Z
- authors: Zhihua Wang, Yunhao Feng, Yifeng Gao, Rui Pan, Baiheng Zhao, Zixing Chen
- query-set: MCP
- abs: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.01700

AI agents often rely on persistent memory to retain user preferences and operational rules across sessions. However, this capability introduces a new safety risk: malicious instructions from messages, documents, or Web content can be retained in memory and influence future tasks even after the original attack content is removed. Existing attack success metrics, however, fail to capture who is affected and how attacks translate into downstream harm during otherwise normal task execution. We introduce MemHarmBench, a victim-centric benchmark for evaluating cross-session prompt injection in memory-persistent AI agents. MemHarmBench considers three categories of potential victims: users, workspace/project owners, and external stakeholders. Our evaluation first exposes an agent to malicious instructions and tests whether they are retained in memory. We then remove the attack content and initiate a new session with a legitimate task while preserving the agent's memory. We jointly evaluate whether the agent follows the attacker's intent, completes the legitimate task, and exhibits observable execution anomalies. Across multiple agent systems, we find that cross-session attacks can remain behaviorally stealthy: agents often complete tasks normally and exhibit no detectable anomalies while still making critical decisions aligned with the attacker's intent. By connecting malicious memory retention to downstream decisions and affected parties, MemHarmBench provides a systematic framework for exposing safety risks that conventional attack- and task-success metrics may overlook. We publicly release MemHarmBench at https://github.com/Kirito14IT/MemHarmBench.


### arXiv 2611.01563 - \textsc{ChatDify}: Automating End-to-End Dify Workflow Synthesis from Natural Language via Multi-Agent Collaboration

- categories: cs.AI
- published: 2026-11-01T04:40:49Z
- authors: Tao Fan, Guoqiang Ma
- query-set: MCP
- abs: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.01563

The proliferation of Large Language Models has catalyzed powerful application development platforms like Dify, which enable users to build sophisticated LLM-powered workflows through modular node orchestration. However, manually configuring these workflows requires deep platform expertise and remains time-consuming and error-prone, creating a significant barrier for non-expert users. To address this challenge, we propose \textsc{ChatDify}, a novel multi-agent framework that automates end-to-end Dify workflow synthesis from natural language descriptions. \textsc{ChatDify} employs an Actor-Critic architecture comprising three specialized agents: the \textit{Planner Agent} that decomposes user requests into structured execution plans, the \textit{Executor Agent} that performs context-aware parameter synthesis and schema-augmented DSL compilation, and the \textit{Reviewer Agent} that ensures reliability through hybrid verification with closed-loop refinement. We evaluate \textsc{ChatDify} on a curated benchmark of 100 production-inspired tasks across three state-of-the-art LLM backends, achieving 0.96, 0.91, and 0.75 success rates with Qwen-3.7, GLM-5.1, and DeepSeek-v4, respectively. These results demonstrate its robustness and practical applicability for lowering the technical barrier to creating sophisticated LLM applications.


### arXiv 2611.01552 - Can Large Language Models Extend Their Agentic Capabilities to Goal Pursuit in Open-Ended Multi-Turn Interaction?

- categories: cs.CL
- published: 2026-11-01T04:23:27Z
- authors: Mingyu Chen, Jingkai Lin, Xiaofen Xing, Liankai Huang, Ruiqi Xiong, Xiangmin Xu
- query-set: MCP
- abs: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.01552

Goal pursuit in open-ended dialogue requires agents to adapt their actions to interaction feedback across successive exchanges. We investigate whether agentic capabilities developed for multi-step tool use carry over to dialogue organized through the model's native tool interface, and how this changes interaction behavior. We define Tool-Mediated Interaction (TMI) as a training-free protocol that represents dialogue actions as tool calls and counterpart responses as tool results, maintaining a continuous reasoning-action-observation loop with retained reasoning and interaction history. We evaluate TMI across social interaction, emotional support, persuasion, and price negotiation using two models and 15,678 main-experiment episodes. Across both models, TMI improves social goal attainment, emotional-support preference, donation amounts, and buyer negotiation margins over conversational baselines with and without thinking. A Qwen ablation further shows that TMI outperforms ordinary conversation with retained reasoning on all four primary outcomes. Behavioral analyses associate the improvements with task-specific patterns in initial proposals and responses to interaction feedback. Alongside these gains, TMI reduces mean reasoning tokens per decision by 42.0-81.5% relative to the thinking baseline and lowers mean cross-turn phrase reuse across all eight model-environment pairs. These findings show that interaction organization shapes both goal attainment and reasoning across dialogue turns.


### arXiv 2611.01446 - I-PTC: Interactive Programmatic Tool Calling for Stateful Tool-Augmented Agents

- categories: cs.AI, cs.LG
- published: 2026-11-01T02:32:02Z
- authors: Huanzhi Mao, Chengkun Cao, Shuo Yuan, Joseph E. Gonzalez
- query-set: MCP
- abs: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.01446

Large language models are increasingly capable of writing and executing code, suggesting a natural paradigm for tool-augmented agents: expressing tool use as programs rather than isolated calls. Programmatic tool calling (PTC) captures this idea through loops, batching, abstraction, and structured computation, but existing PTC approaches are largely stateless, limiting their ability to reuse intermediate results and adapt over time. This is problematic for real-world tasks, where tool environments and problem-solving processes are inherently stateful. We therefore present I-PTC, an interactive framework that executes stateful PTC: models issue code snippets that call tools, maintain reusable state, and evolve the tool interface through API corrections. To evaluate the effectiveness of I-PTC and the general PTC setting, we introduce PTC-Bench, a benchmark of long-tail, production-style user requests whose difficulty comes from large initial environments, many dependent API interactions, extended planning, and recovery across follow-ups and transient failures. Experiments on PTC-Bench show that I-PTC improves performance on complex multi-step tasks with large tool sets while substantially reducing token consumption over conventional tool-calling baselines and the original PTC baseline, highlighting stateful programmatic tool calling as a promising direction for scalable tool-augmented agents.


### arXiv 2611.01404 - Serving Agentic Robot Fleets: Workload Characterization, KV-Cache Thrashing, and Admission Control

- categories: cs.RO, cs.DC
- published: 2026-11-01T01:15:32Z
- authors: Sida He
- query-set: MCP
- abs: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.01404

Agentic robots combine a vision-language brain, a tool-using harness, and a vision-language-action (VLA) policy. Serving a fleet of these agents introduces two coupled resource constraints: the brain repeatedly revisits long, growing key-value (KV) cache states, while the VLA executes action chunks under tight control deadlines. We characterize 80 LIBERO-PRO episodes generated by GPT-6-Astra, RPent, and pi0.5 (1,862 brain turns and 1,582 VLA inference chunks), profile both layers on H800 graphics processing units (GPUs), and replay the resulting agent programs on a vLLM serving engine. Brain contexts reach 215k tokens; the median Astra request takes 7.78 s, while the intervening environment-step gap is only 1.0 s. Under a 0.25 s action-chunk deadline, one VLA GPU meets the 1% starvation target for one robot; under a 1.0 s deadline, its measured capacity reaches at least 32. In sustained brain replay, increasing concurrency from 20 to 32 robots reduces throughput from about 2.4 to 0.6 requests/s as repetitive prefill displaces generation. A static active-episode cap restores 2.24-2.25 requests/s and raises completed trace-program throughput from 1.30 to 5.00-5.55 episodes/min. Our measurements expose the workload-dependent shift between action deadlines and brain KV residency, and establish practical principles for capacity planning and admission in sustained agentic robot fleets.


### arXiv 2611.01394 - ERIA: An Instruction-Optimized Agent for Clinical EEG Reporting

- categories: cs.LG, cs.AI
- published: 2026-11-01T00:58:55Z
- authors: Ahmed Sameh, Gayal Kuruppu, Neeraj Wagh, Bridger Brundy, Yogatheesan Varatharajah
- query-set: MCP
- abs: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.01394

Clinical EEG interpretation requires integrating technical quality, background and behavioral states, interictal and ictal abnormalities, and overall clinical context across long recordings. We introduce ERIA (EEG Reporting and Instruction-Optimized Agent), a locally deployable framework in which a single 27B language-model controller analyzes whole-recording EEGs by orchestrating specialized parametric and signal-processing tools. ERIA combines learned tools for background abnormality, sleep-wake state, seizure-like activity, and interictal epileptiform discharges with deterministic spectral, amplitude, symmetry, montage, and signal-quality analyses. Rather than fine-tuning the controller or using multi-agent orchestration, ERIA keeps the controller weights, specialist tools, and safety constraints fixed and optimizes only its natural-language analysis policy with GEPA (Genetic-Pareto), using feedback from expert-written EEG reports. GEPA improves physician-aligned whole-recording report quality over the raw policy, and the optimized policy transfers to external EEG benchmarks. On CerebraGloss-Bench and BrainBench Foundational Analysis, ERIA remains competitive with the models evaluated in the original benchmarks. These results show that specialist tools and language-level policy optimization can extend the clinical and general EEG capabilities of a single locally executable model while preserving local inference and traceable tool use.


### arXiv 2611.01301 - CuriousTogether: Implications of Curiosity and Cultural Grounding in Multi-Agent LLM Interactions

- categories: cs.CL, cs.AI
- published: 2026-10-31T22:01:33Z
- authors: Angana Borah, Zoha Hayat Bhatti, Zhijing Jin, Rada Mihalcea
- query-set: MCP
- abs: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.01301

Large Language Models (LLMs) are increasingly deployed in multi-agent settings, exhibiting emergent collective behaviors, yet the role of curiosity as a driver of such dynamics remains understudied. We introduce CuriousTogether, a framework evaluating curiosity-driven multi-agent LLM interactions across three complementary dimensions: novelty-seeking, epistemic and social curiosity. Across eight tasks spanning reasoning, creative generation, and social bias mitigation, curiosity-guided multi-agent settings yield improvements over non-curious ones on seven out of eight tasks, with gains of up to 20.6% in story generation (semantic diversity) and a 14.9 pp reduction in stereotype rates. Furthermore, country-conditioned cultural grounding show benefits for story generation and social tasks, but can hurt precision-oriented reasoning. Finally, we show that inference-time multi-agent curiosity-based approach offers favorable quality-token trade-offs, compared with single-agent baselines. Together, these findings support curiosity-guided interaction as a practical mechanism for improving multi-agent collaboration, with downstream benefits for reasoning, creative, and societal tasks.


### arXiv 2611.01276 - OntoDoc: Automatically Generating Ontology Resource Descriptions

- categories: cs.AI, cs.IR
- published: 2026-10-31T21:23:13Z
- authors: Juan Guajardo Gutierrez, Mike Tran, Nasim Shirvani Mahdavi, Chengkai Li
- query-set: MCP
- abs: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.01276

Knowledge graphs and ontologies form the backbone of structured, machine-readable knowledge representation, yet ontology terms often lack natural language descriptions, limiting their accessibility and reuse. Existing ontology verbalization approaches tend to rely on rule-based or template-driven techniques that operate on strict, formal schema axioms, resulting in rigid verbalizations that fail to convey richer contextual semantics. We present OntoDoc, a multi-agent framework for automatically generating natural language descriptions for any Internationalized Resource Identifier (IRI) within a SPARQL endpoint or RDF file. OntoDoc operates through a four-stage pipeline: (1) a Database Context agent that adaptively explores the knowledge graph via SPARQL to collect semantically informative triples about the target IRI; (2) a Web Context agent that retrieves and re-ranks encyclopedic background knowledge from Wikipedia and the open web; (3) an Assumption Generation agent that synthesizes both contexts into a structured, evidence-grounded list of intermediate assumptions; and (4) a Description Generation agent that produces the final natural language description from the grounded assumptions. We evaluate OntoDoc on three knowledge graphs spanning biomedical, agricultural, and cross-domain settings, using both automatic scoring via Large Language Models (LLMs) and human evaluation with 11 annotators. OntoDoc consistently matches or exceeds a naive LLM baseline and a template-based ontology verbalizer in accuracy. Furthermore, it vastly outperforms both baselines in the completeness of the generated descriptions, delivering an average 12% improvement in completeness over the naive baseline across all domains, and improving completeness scores by as much as 2.8 points on a 5-point scale relative to the ontology verbalizer.


### arXiv 2611.01145 - CivicBench: A Benchmark and Memory Framework for Web-Based Civic Data Analytics

- categories: cs.AI
- published: 2026-10-31T17:32:54Z
- authors: Yangyu Wu, Siru Zhong, Haoyuan Liang, Yuxuan Liang
- query-set: MCP
- abs: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.01145

Municipalities publish service requests, geographic boundaries, weather observations, demographic estimates, and operational guidance through independently maintained Web portals. Civic analysis must combine these sources while preserving their schemas, source links, spatial and temporal scopes, and interpretive limits. Tool-using language agents can perform this integration, but existing task benchmarks rarely test whether a computed claim remains supported by the records used. We present CivicBench, a 400-task benchmark over four cities and two years that materializes civic Web data into frozen bundles for reproducible evaluation. Executable contracts score computation, source linkage, coverage, scope, qualified answers, and refusal when the requested conclusion is not supported. We also introduce Task-State Memory (TSM), which compiles tool observations into typed assertions, reconciles records only within compatible scopes, and retrieves a bounded state organized around unresolved constraints. Across nine completed model pairs, eight yield positive mean-score differences with 95\% semantic-contract-cluster bootstrap intervals excluding zero; the ninth interval crosses zero. The benchmark and memory framework make the records behind a claim inspectable at the point of decision.


### arXiv 2611.01132 - Acceptance and Responsibility Attribution Toward Autonomous Ambulances Across Driving Conditions: A Preliminary Survey of Fire Service Personnel

- categories: cs.HC, cs.CY
- published: 2026-10-31T17:22:13Z
- authors: Hirotake Yamazoe, Shingo Yamaguchi
- query-set: MCP
- abs: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.01132

Traffic incidents involving ambulances and fire engines during emergency-response driving can have serious consequences. Although autonomous driving technologies may reduce workload and improve safety, their introduction into emergency services requires consideration of first responders' trust, willingness to ride (WTR), and perceptions of responsibility. We surveyed 34 fire service personnel from a single fire station in Himeji, Japan, to examine their evaluations of autonomous ambulances and responsibility attribution under non-emergency and emergency driving conditions. Emergency driving was associated with lower perceived usefulness for improving safety, trust, and WTR, as well as greater anxiety. In contrast, responsibility attribution remained largely stable, with 30 participants ($88.2\%$) selecting the same primary responsible party across conditions and the onboard crew most frequently selected in both. Among these 30 participants, 26 ($86.7\%$) also assigned the same responsibility share. Open-ended responses revealed concerns about system reliability, the complexity of emergency driving, and the need for human judgment, as well as considerations regarding operator and organizational responsibility. These findings suggest that acceptance of autonomous ambulances is sensitive to driving conditions, while responsibility judgments may also reflect perceived professional roles and responsibilities.


### arXiv 2611.01054 - Finite-Time Tracking Success or Failure in the Pursuer--Evader Model

- categories: math.OC
- published: 2026-10-31T15:51:22Z
- authors: Woojoo Shim, Hyunjin Ahn
- query-set: MCP
- abs: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.01054

We investigate a sufficient framework for determining the success or failure of finite-time tracking in pursuer--evader dynamics. To this end, we design a nonsmooth coupled multi-agent system by adopting a previously established pursuer--evader model from the existing literature, which characterizes asymptotic tracking success and failure, as the backbone. We then provide appropriate sufficient conditions, expressed in terms of initial data, system parameters, and kernel functionals, under which some pursuers can successfully track evaders within a finite time, while others fail to do so. In particular, we show that the pursuer--evader subsystem associated with the pursuers that successfully track the evader, together with the corresponding evaders, exhibits finite-time stability.


### arXiv 2611.00923 - Illusion: Safeguarding Topology Confidentiality in LLM Multi-Agent Systems via Behavior-Preserving Obfuscation

- categories: cs.CR, cs.AI
- published: 2026-10-31T13:23:42Z
- authors: Yiu-Fai Cheung, Hongzhi Guo, Miao Pan, Irwin King, Hao Wang
- query-set: MCP
- abs: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.00923

The communication topology of Large Language Model Multi-Agent Systems (LLM-MASs) governs how information propagates among agents and encodes critical domain knowledge and expert workflow design. It is valuable intellectual property and plays a decisive role in system utility and safety, yet remains vulnerable to inference attacks that stealthily reconstruct it from agent outputs. Existing defenses detect or mitigate such attacks but do not proactively conceal the topology itself. We propose \textit{Illusion}, a proactive topology obfuscation defense for LLM-MAS. \textit{Illusion} injects fake edges and fake messages to present attackers with a deceptive topology, while selecting low-impact candidates that preserve output-distribution equivalence and task-accuracy non-inferiority. The resulting fake edges are designed to be indistinguishable from genuine messages under black-box observation. Extensive experiments show that \textit{Illusion} reduces edge-level F1 from over 0.87 to 0.62 and graph similarity from over 0.94 to 0.71. It also largely preserves output distributions and task performance, and its fake edges resist both similarity-based and LLM-judge filtering, even under adaptive pruning.


### arXiv 2611.00779 - Who Schedules Agent Memory? A Sidecar Approach to Long-Horizon Tasks

- categories: cs.AI
- published: 2026-10-31T09:45:28Z
- authors: Qijia Zhuang, Rui Liu, Yuxiang Ren
- query-set: MCP
- abs: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.00779

As large language model agents increasingly undertake complex, long-horizon tasks, memory has become an important component for supporting sustained decision-making and action. Existing methods primarily focus on memory storage, updating, and retrieval, but often overlook how memory scheduling affects task execution: the main agent must identify memory needs, initiate retrieval, and wait for results while advancing the task, increasing both scheduling burden and execution time. To address this problem, we propose MemSidecar, which decouples memory scheduling from task execution through two independently selectable modes. Hooks directly delivers bound memory to the main agent when execution events match predefined conditions. Foresight uses an independent agent to predict potential queries, retrieve results in parallel, and store them in a cache, delivering matching and valid results when actual requests arrive. Hooks and Foresight achieve scores of 41.23% and 44.34% on MCP-Persona, respectively, while effectively reducing execution costs and latency and improving the efficiency of long-horizon agent execution.


### arXiv 2611.00723 - Architecting Fairness-Aware LLM Interactions

- categories: cs.SE, cs.AI, cs.CY
- published: 2026-10-31T07:59:01Z
- authors: Alessandra Parziale, Valeria Pontillo, Fabio Palomba, Patrizio Pelliccione
- query-set: MCP
- abs: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.00723

Large Language Models (LLMs) are increasingly used as decision-support tools but may generate stereotypical, discriminatory, or unfair responses. As LLMs become embedded in decision-critical software systems, fairness can no longer be treated as a downstream evaluation step, but must become an architectural concern of the interaction itself. Yet current fairness approaches operate as standalone evaluation techniques, lacking a reusable mechanism to verify and mitigate violations at runtime. We propose a fairness-assurance architecture grounded in three reusable design patterns, instantiated as a multi-agent framework that evaluates and iteratively refines LLM responses before delivery. A preliminary evaluation across eight configurations involving different LLM families shows that the architecture identifies fairness violations and iteratively produces compliant responses in all configurations, with behavior differing depending on the model-family composition. These results provide initial evidence for the joint instantiation of the proposed patterns and a first step toward architecting non-functional properties into LLM-based systems.


### arXiv 2611.00546 - Low-Margin Branch Points in Deterministic LLM Deliberatio

- categories: cs.AI
- published: 2026-10-31T02:01:39Z
- authors: Hajime Shimao, Warut Khern-am-nua
- query-set: MCP
- abs: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.00546

Deterministic multi-agent LLM deliberation can exactly re- play a fixed transcript, yet exact replay alone does not test local resistance to controlled internal interventions. Because each response becomes context for later agents, a changed greedy token can redirect a transcript-mediated execution. We present a white-box audit of this local failure mode in a five-agent committee under greedy decoding. We prospectively select low- and high-margin token positions, require exact no-op re- play, inject norm-relative residual displacements, and measure first transcript divergence within ten generated tokens. Low- margin positions are more branch-sensitive than high-margin controls, while matched margin-reducing gradient directions branch substantially more often than margin-increasing con- trols. We then continue selected realized branches through the committee to distinguish textual persistence from preference and final-decision changes. The contribution is a controlled audit for local branch susceptibility in this specified protocol, not an estimate of deployment frequency, decision correct- ness, or a phenomenon unique to multi-agent systems.


### arXiv 2611.00518 - Agentic Context Management for Multi-Turn Human-AI Conversations

- categories: cs.AI, cs.CL
- published: 2026-10-31T01:13:55Z
- authors: Matthew Ho, Chenglong Wang, Baolin Peng, Nebojsa Jojic, Philippe Laban, Michel Galley
- query-set: MCP
- abs: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.00518

LLMs degrade in multi-turn conversation partly because of context pollution: the model's own earlier outputs stay in context and anchor later turns, so an early misreading of the user's intent can persist after the user corrects it. Assistant omission (AO), dropping every assistant message, removes this pollution but fails when interaction is referential: later turns refer back to drafts, decisions, and tool-call results that exist only in assistant turns. We propose Agentic Conversation Context Curation (AC3), a training-free method in which a separate analyzer (1) consolidates the task specification from the user's messages alone, (2) audits the assistant's prior outputs against it, and (3) edits the context, either appending the analysis or discarding the assistant side and rebuilding the context from the specification. A second finding: pollution is contagious. An analyzer that sees the assistant's reasoning while extracting the specification can do worse than no intervention, and instructing it to ignore them did not close the gap, so we exclude assistant turns structurally. On Lost in Conversation (replaying difficulty-selected instances), where AO is an oracle by construction, AC3 closes 55-84% of the gap between full context and AO on three of four tasks and exceeds AO on the fourth, and an LLM that faithfully summarizes the history does not reproduce the gain even with more compute. On real WildChat conversations, a GPT-5-mini judge prefers AC3's next turn over both full context and AO in every configuration of our main table, though this preference depends on the judge; on CollabLLM results are mixed. In stateful tool use (tau2-bench), AO collapses to 0% on every model; AC3 does not collapse, though it falls slightly behind full context. Detector studies show that Reset removes injected pollution but is not span-selective: the gain comes from rebuilding the specification.


### arXiv 2611.00492 - Spotting the Artificial: Dialogic Humor Improvisation in Humans and LLMs

- categories: cs.CL, cs.AI
- published: 2026-10-31T00:33:27Z
- authors: Lizeth Palacios Patiño, Ximena Gutierrez-Vasques, Daniel Rodriguez
- query-set: MCP
- abs: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.00492

We propose a methodology for investigating the differences between natural and synthetic language in a challenging setting: humorous improvisational dialogue. Our experimental setting compares human-produced comedy improvisations with the dialogues generated by LLMs in a multi-agent environment that we designed. The analysis combines automated linguistic measures with manual annotation grounded in Systemic Functional Linguistics (SFL). The results show that, although LLMs can create coherent and creative scenes, they often fail to reproduce key characteristics of spontaneous spoken interaction. In addition, LLM-generated interactions display a strong tendency toward politeness and positive evaluations, contrasting with the broader range of attitudes found in human humorous improvisation. Our evidence highlights both the strengths and limitations of current LLMs in modeling dialogic humor and demonstrate the value of integrating linguistic theory with NLP methods for the analysis of machine-generated language


### arXiv 2611.00442 - Fidelity Collapses with Table Size, Not Turn Count: Delivery Architecture in a Deployed Tool-Using Agent

- categories: cs.CL, cs.AI
- published: 2026-10-30T22:50:32Z
- authors: Tung Thanh Hoang
- query-set: MCP
- abs: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.00442

When a multi-turn agent delivers a tool's structured records into the model's context and re-emits them each turn, every turn can corrupt data the tool computed correctly. We measure this in a production award-travel agent: two architectures over identical frozen payloads, lineage-matched prompts and sampling, differing only in delivery architecture. SYNTHESIS feeds full record sets into the planner's context and answers follow-ups in-head; BYPASS, the deployed system, keeps records in session state, translates follow-ups into arguments for a deterministic filter, and delivers rows over a typed side channel. Across 24 dialogues (339 scored turns per condition), BYPASS scores 92.3% of turns fully correct, SYNTHESIS 45.7% (paired gap +46.6pp [+35.7, +57.1]). Varying delivered-table size by capture date, SYNTHESIS collapses from near-perfect at 10 rows or fewer to 8% or less beyond about 74 rows. On a content-controlled prefix ladder the collapse threshold orders by model capability, from under 10 rows (GPT-4o-mini) to about 198 or more (Gemini 2.5 Pro), while BYPASS stays size-flat for all four models. The collapse is expensive: beyond 100 rows, where almost no SYNTHESIS answer is correct, each correct one costs roughly 200x BYPASS's flat \$0.012. Practitioners converged on typed side-channel delivery for rendering, not correctness; we show it was, unknowingly, also a correctness decision.


### arXiv 2611.00420 - Circuit Oracle: Automating Attribution Graph Analysis via Natural-Language Queries

- categories: cs.CL
- published: 2026-10-30T22:12:53Z
- authors: Shariar Kabir, Hong Kiat Tan, Swastik Agrawal, Sai V R Chereddy, Sriram Balasubramanian
- query-set: MCP
- abs: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.00420

Attribution graphs, an emerging tool in mechanistic interpretability, use transcoders to decompose language model computations into sparse interpretable features connected by causal edges. However, turning a graph into a safety-relevant insight requires hours of manual analysis by experts. We introduce Circuit Oracle, an off-the-shelf LLM equipped with a multi-agent harness and task-specific authored skills, which answers natural-language questions about a target model by reading the attribution graph through tool calls. We evaluate it on three safety-relevant proxy tasks: detecting spurious features in probe circuits, eliciting hidden knowledge from taboo-finetuned models, and suppression jailbreaking via feature-level interventions. Beyond task scores, the oracle produces pruned subgraphs and context-specific feature explanations that we assess qualitatively. Results are mixed. On spurious-feature detection, Circuit Oracle reaches 78.2 $\pm$ 2.4% accuracy, outperforming single-layer feature-ranking baselines. On secret elicitation, trained self-explainers like Activation Oracles remain the stronger baseline. On refusal suppression over 50 prompts, its single committed intervention is slightly below a diff-in-means baseline, and only exceeds it when allowed the best of five attempts. Results are stable across five runs and across model choices for the agents. We also release the pruned subgraphs and context-specific feature readings that revise existing global labels, and invite future work to develop evaluations that can measure their usefulness directly.


### arXiv 2611.00290 - Quantifying Local and Global Fidelity of Nonlinear Embeddings

- categories: cs.AI
- published: 2026-10-30T19:16:23Z
- authors: Henry Han
- query-set: MCP
- abs: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.00290
