Dimension reduction methods are chosen by a common claim: t-SNE and UMAP preserve local structure, and PCA preserves global structure. Checking it on given data takes local and global scores on one scale and a test that compares two maps. Both scores come from one curve. The locality profile is the chance-corrected share of each point's k nearest neighbors that a map keeps, over all neighborhood sizes. Its value at k = 10 is the local fidelity L, and its mean over 30 to 50 percent of the data is the global fidelity G. We prove that at the midpoint the profile is the agreement on which half of the data is near each point, a rank correlation of the distances. Because L and G are one statistic, two maps can be tested against each other. The LG reversal test applies to any two dimension reduction methods. It decides, with a block-jackknife p-value, whether one map is more local and the other more global, and returns the scale at which to change maps. On 156 maps of twelve datasets with up to 1.38 million points, G agrees with established global scores at 0.89 and 0.96, and 2,000 points estimate it. The test confirms the usual claim on seven datasets. On both retail datasets and both rolled-up manifolds, it finds the neighbor embeddings more global. In an agent memory of one million passages, the two maps cross at 150,000 passages (p = 2.6e-5). Applications span pathology, single cells, supply chains and AI agent memory.


### arXiv 2611.00243 - OmniPiano: Diverse Dexterous Piano-Playing Challenges for Standard, Robust, Safe, and Multi-Agent RL

- categories: cs.AI
- published: 2026-10-30T18:24:38Z
- authors: Fan Xu, Fan Yang, Yifan Xu, Ruiyuan Zhang, Yimou Wu, Nuo Yi
- query-set: MCP
- abs: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.00243

Dexterous robotic manipulation remains a major challenge in robotics, exemplified by piano playing, which requires coordinated control of fingers and provides a demanding testbed for reinforcement learning (RL). Although previous work established a benchmark for robotic piano playing, it is restricted to two hands and does not support unified evaluation. To address this gap, we introduce OmniPiano, a benchmark that provides one to up to five Shadow Hands and supports standard, robust, safe, and multi-agent RL within a shared piano-playing task family. OmniPiano is developed for scalable and diverse piano-playing tasks with configurable perturbations, explicit safety constraints, and decentralized cooperation settings. Particularly, to facilitate usability and extendability, OmniPiano adopts a highly modular design and provides comprehensive tasks to support RL study of task performance, robustness, safety, and cooperation in dexterous control. With at least 912 task settings and 36 baseline algorithms, OmniPiano further incorporates LLM-based agents to broaden the evaluation scope and reveal new insights. Extensive evaluations show that state-of-the-art RL algorithms and frontier LLM-based agents still struggle on challenging piano-playing tasks, even under standard RL settings, highlighting substantial room for improvement in dexterous control. The code, dataset, tutorial, and demo are available at https://sail-research-lab.github.io/omnipiano/.


### arXiv 2610.42144 - Dittany: Contribution-Guided Repair Framework for MAS Applications

- categories: cs.SE
- published: 2026-10-30T17:59:12Z
- authors: Hanqi Sun, Yuchen Shao, Hanyi Si, Beibei Xu, Lisheng Wang, Chengcheng Wan
- query-set: MCP
- abs: https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.42144

LLM-based multi-agent system (MAS) applications can produce useful intermediate results while leaving task requirements unsatisfied. Repairing such executions requires identifying the work still needed and the completed results that can support it. We present \tool, a contribution-guided runtime repair framework for MAS applications. \tool derives expected contributions and acceptance conditions from task requirements and method specifications, and connects their dependencies in a contribution graph. Runtime checks locate unmet conditions and establish which results remain usable. These results guide action-level correction when available inputs suffice, or graph-level recovery when prerequisites or unfinished work need revision. Both repair scopes retain validated contributions and check repaired outputs before subsequent work uses them. We evaluate \tool on 30 applications across six MAS frameworks and 9,000 tasks. It detects runtime failures with 88.28\% precision and 79.18\% recall, and achieves 84.76\% task accuracy, improving over Native execution by 26.74 percentage points and the strongest of four recovery baselines by 21.28 points. Estimated deployment token consumption is 17.92\% above Native, but 24.19\% below Reflexion and 13.05\% below Iterative Rollback. Ablation results show that combining the two repair scopes achieves higher accuracy and lower token consumption than graph-level repair alone.


### arXiv 2610.42068 - RAMA: Retrieval-Augmented Multi-Agent Routing with Episodic Memory for LEO Satellite Networks

- categories: cs.NI, cs.LG
- published: 2026-10-30T17:26:50Z
- authors: Xin Ming Li, Peng Hu
- query-set: MCP
- abs: https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.42068

Low-Earth orbit (LEO) satellite networks present a challenging routing environment due to rapidly changing topology, traffic, and link conditions. Existing deep reinforcement learning (DRL) routing methods typically rely on current observations and information encoded in network weights, limiting their ability to exploit successful past experiences. We propose RAMA, a retrieval-augmented multi-agent DRL framework with Approximate Information State (AIS), in which each satellite combines its local observation, recent routing history, neighboring state information, and retrieved entries from a per-satellite episodic memory. Retrieved experiences are integrated through a Transformer-based decision network trained with AIS-based auxiliary objectives. With weights frozen in a 140-satellite Kepler constellation, RAMA reduces mean end-to-end latency by 13.4% and 99th-percentile latency by 66.7% relative to the state-of-theart double deep Q-network (DDQN) routing method, mainly by avoiding long detours. Updated online, RAMA remains stable while DDQN degrades. On the 1,584-satellite Starlink shell 1, the reduction in mean latency grows to 93.2%, and updating RAMA online lowers its mean latency by a further 25.5%. These results demonstrate the effectiveness of retrieval-augmented memory for scalable decentralized routing in LEO satellite networks.


### arXiv 2610.41903 - Statistical Foundations of Reinforcement Learning: A Non-Asymptotic Perspective

- categories: stat.ML, cs.IT, cs.LG
- published: 2026-10-30T15:39:38Z
- authors: Yuxin Chen, Yuejie Chi, Jianqing Fan, Gen Li, Yuting Wei, Yuling Yan
- query-set: MCP
- abs: https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.41903

Reinforcement learning (RL) embodies a foundational paradigm for sequential decision-making in the face of uncertainty. The rapid growth of model complexity in emerging applications has significantly amplified the challenges of achieving data-efficient RL, particularly in sample-starved scenarios where data collection may be costly, time-consuming, or even high-stakes. Consequently, understanding and improving the data efficiency of RL algorithms has become a central problem of both fundamental theoretical interest and practical importance. Despite decades of research, the statistical underpinnings of RL remained remarkably underdeveloped until relatively recently, particularly in the finite-sample regimes most relevant to modern practice. Over the past few years, there has been a surge of activity advancing the frontiers of non-asymptotic statistical RL theory, drawing on ideas from high-dimensional statistics and probability, information theory, and statistical learning theory. In this monograph, we present a non-asymptotic treatment for RL based on tabular Markov decision processes, focusing on three canonical sampling mechanisms --- RL with a generative model, online RL, and offline RL --- together with two algorithmic paradigms: model-based and model-free approaches. Centered around non-asymptotic sample complexity analysis, our treatment emphasizes minimax-optimal algorithm design, particularly methods that attain statistical optimality without significant burn-in costs, as well as sharp statistical characterizations of several widely used RL algorithms. Whenever possible, we provide largely self-contained analyses together with intuitive explanations. We also briefly discuss several extensions, such as robust RL, competitive multi-agent RL, federated RL, uncertainty quantification, and RL from human feedback.


### arXiv 2611.00169 - PatchScout: Thematic Web Data Collection via Information Foraging

- categories: cs.AI, cs.IR
- published: 2026-10-30T12:50:40Z
- authors: Michael West, Eduard Dragut
- query-set: MCP
- abs: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.00169

Researchers often need web corpora that are not answers to a single query, but reusable collections of documents spanning many decentralized sources. We refer to this problem as thematic web data collection: assembling semantically relevant documents that share a common theme but are scattered across heterogeneous and structurally disconnected regions of the web. Existing approaches, including traversal-based methods and LLM-driven web agents, are limited by local exploration or short-horizon stopping, preventing comprehensive collection. We formulate this task as a long-horizon information foraging problem over query-induced semantic patches, where the central challenge is deciding when to continue local exploitation and when to switch to new regions. We propose PatchScout, a multi-agent framework that instantiates a class of patch-switching policies to balance local exploitation and adaptive patch switching under a fixed budget. Experiments on four live-web topics show that PatchScout substantially improves yield and domain coverage over traversal-based and search-augmented reasoning agent baselines. In an applied social science setting with an incomplete prior collection, PatchScout discovers 102 previously unidentified entities, illustrating its ability to expand existing thematic datasets.


### arXiv 2610.41682 - The Ineliminable Anchor: Human Judgment as a Structural Requirement of Autonomous Artificial Intelligence Systems

- categories: cs.CY
- published: 2026-10-30T12:49:37Z
- authors: Junjie Li, Haoran Liu, Yidan Zhang, Jian Xu, Zhaoyuan Ma
- query-set: MCP
- abs: https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.41682

As artificial intelligence (AI) agents carry out longer chains of tasks, oversight must establish whether their behavior still serves an authorized goal. Here, we introduce the anchor: the point at which reference information and human-authorized standards enter an autonomous system. We argue that oversight design should first identify the reference needed to assess goal drift and whether the system's internal record can supply it, then decide who checks and how often, and finally specify who may revise the goal. Goal drift can be impossible to certify from a system's internal record. The missing reference must come from outside that record. Combining a reliability model of checkpoint spacing with evidence from training, execution, and multi-agent coordination, we develop a task taxonomy to guide anchor placement and decisions about which checks to automate. For AI systems that serve human purposes, human judgment is an ineliminable structural requirement in authorizing and revising the purposes and standards that govern autonomous operation, even when routine checking and execution are automated.


### arXiv 2610.41558 - A complete analytical characterization of optimal collision-free motion for two robots

- categories: cs.RO, eess.SY, math.OC
- published: 2026-10-30T10:53:31Z
- authors: Jan Kamm, Antonio Terpin, Raffaello D'Andrea
- query-set: MCP
- abs: https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.41558

Collision-free motion planning for multi-agent robotic systems remains an active research area because of the complexity of coordinating robot trajectories, with applications ranging from coordinated drone shows to autonomous operations on land, in the air, and on water. Importantly, the analytical difficulty of nonconvex collision-avoidance constraints has so far driven the development of exclusively numerical methods. We derive a complete analytical characterization of the optimal trajectories for two robots that start and end at rest, under double-integrator dynamics and a squared-acceleration objective. We prove that these trajectories belong to eight distinct contact families and determine which family applies for each configuration of initial and final positions. We analyze the geometrical properties of the optimal trajectories, provide a numerical algorithm for computing them, and discuss how these insights may improve the performance of existing numerical trajectory optimizers.


### arXiv 2610.41548 - Contact-based optimization for collision-free motion planning for a million robots

- categories: cs.RO, eess.SY
- published: 2026-10-30T10:46:26Z
- authors: Jan Kamm, Antonio Terpin, Raffaello D'Andrea
- query-set: MCP
- abs: https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.41548

Multi-agent robotic systems have attracted growing interest in recent years, with applications ranging from coordinated drone shows to autonomous operations on land, in the air, and on water. At the planning level, robot motion is often modeled using double-integrator dynamics (with low-level controllers handling the physical dynamics during execution) with a minimum-energy objective subject to collision-avoidance constraints and limits on position, velocity, and acceleration. The complexity of the problem resides in the constraints, which couple the trajectories and make the optimization problem nonconvex. Existing numerical approaches typically discretize and optimize entire trajectories, which becomes expensive for large fleets. We exploit the structure of optimal trajectories by organizing optimization around contacts, at which robots reach the minimum allowed separation. We formulate a coupled algebraic system in contact variables and reconstruct trajectories analytically between contacts. Our algorithm, sequential contact insertion (SCI), starts from unconstrained trajectories, inserts contacts at separation violations, and jointly adjusts their times and states. Numerical experiments demonstrate planning for one million robots in minutes and thousands of robots in fractions of a second. On problems feasible for a fixed-grid sequential convex programming baseline, SCI achieves median speedups of several orders of magnitude at matching solution accuracy.


### arXiv 2610.41235 - CONSORT: Uncertainty-Constrained Multi-Robot Planning for Cooperative Localization in GPS-Denied Environments

- categories: cs.RO
- published: 2026-10-30T04:18:51Z
- authors: Tony Zhang, Ryan Bartholomew, Kalliyan Velasco, Joshua Mangelson, Brady Moon
- query-set: MCP
- abs: https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.41235

Centralized multi-agent path planning with cooperative localization in GPS-denied environments is generally computationally intractable when formulated in full belief space due to tightly coupled uncertainty dynamics. Prior work typically approximates belief dynamics or restricts inter-agent coupling. This work examines a multi-agent planning problem in which a primary agent must reach a goal in minimum time subject to a chance-constrained collision-avoidance requirement and a terminal bound on localization uncertainty. We obtain a tractable surrogate of the underlying belief-space problem by optimizing over the mean-state trajectories of all agents and propagating covariance for terminal constraint evaluation only. We propose CONSORT (Cooperative Navigation with Support-Optimized Routing and Trajectories), a discrete-to-continuous pipeline combining hexagonal-grid joint search with spline-based trajectory refinement. Across 50 randomized scenarios, the planners separate as the terminal uncertainty bound tightens, with CONSORT solving 80% of scenarios where the strongest baseline solves 54%, at comparable path length. In marine simulations, with the plans executed under feedback control on an independently developed factor-graph estimate, the trajectories remain collision-free and their terminal uncertainty falls within 6% of the planner's prediction.


### arXiv 2610.41210 - From Evaluation to Evolution: Benchmarking Image Editing and Evolving Image Models

- categories: cs.CV, cs.AI
- published: 2026-10-30T03:42:15Z
- authors: Jun He, Canmiao Fu, Ying Zhang, Zhipeng Huang, Chenyu Mu, Yazhen Xie
- query-set: MCP
- abs: https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.41210

Real-world image editing requires coordinating multiple visual transformations with content preservation and reference constraints, yet aggregate scores can readily obscure unmet local requirements. Effective evaluation must therefore identify local failures and translate them into priorities for subsequent model improvement. We introduce WeGenEditBench, comprising 2,740 evaluation instances and 148 task types across single-image, text-intensive, and multi-reference editing. Pre-generated checklists shared across models decompose each request into atomic requirements; item-level deductions and visual evidence are established first and then summarized into scores for edit execution, preservation, visual quality, and task-specific correctness. To support the experience-acquisition component of recursive self-improvement (RSI), we develop WeGenEvoStudio, a unified multi-agent harness for RSI-oriented learning that integrates model-conditioned failure diagnosis, retrieval-grounded data construction, supervised adaptation, and re-evaluation. Among 15 evaluated configurations, GPT-Image-2 leads single-image editing with an Overall score of 4.076, while Seedream-5.0-Pro leads text-intensive editing at 4.291. Evolution experiments across four generation and editing benchmarks demonstrate substantial targeted gains: on their report-selected Top-5 single-image targets, FLUX.2 [dev] improves Overall from 1.083 to 3.517, while Qwen-Image-Edit-2511 gains 13.2%. Together, our benchmark and harness provide a foundation for developing more reliable image models through interpretable evaluation and feedback-driven learning.


### arXiv 2610.41159 - MATS: A Multi-Agent Framework for Controllable and Reflective Tumor Synthesis

- categories: cs.CV
- published: 2026-10-30T02:34:50Z
- authors: Zhongnan Shi, Keyang Zhou, Siyi Li, Qichao Ying, Xinchen Ma, Shuyi Gong
- query-set: MCP
- abs: https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.41159

Synthesizing realistic tumors on medical images helps address data scarcity for clinical AI. Existing diffusion-based methods, however, lack fine-grained attribute-level controllability, closed-loop quality verification, and awareness of which morphological patterns are underrepresented in the target distribution. We present MATS, a multi-agent framework for text-controllable, cross-organ tumor synthesis. Given a target disease, MATS first profiles its morphological distribution over a fine-grained attribute space covering tumor geometry and appearance, and constructs a generation plan that amplifies underrepresented subtypes while keeping common ones dominant. It then localizes the target organ, proposes anatomically valid placement, and executes two-stage diffusion to produce the tumor mask and render texture, each conditioned on the corresponding attributes. A closed-loop reflective mechanism verifies attribute alignment at three checkpoints throughout synthesis and provides targeted feedback to guide re-generation when misalignment is detected. Trained exclusively on a single-source pancreatic neuroendocrine tumor dataset, MATS consistently improves downstream segmentation both in-domain and across LiTS, KiTS, and MSD-Pancreas benchmarks, whereas most prior generative augmentation methods fall below real-only training.


### arXiv 2610.40991 - A Temporal Cellular Sheaf Framework for Multi-Agent Systems with Switching Communication Topologies

- categories: eess.SY
- published: 2026-10-29T21:18:18Z
- authors: Brandon Fallin, Wilmer Leal, Austin Copeland, Benjamin Merlin Bumpus, James Fairbanks, Warren E. Dixon
- query-set: MCP
- abs: https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.40991

Cellular sheaves serve as a natural modeling framework for encoding heterogeneity in the state and interaction spaces of multi-agent systems. This work examines switching communication graphs with the use of temporal cellular sheaves, which we construct to represent heterogeneous agent state spaces, the maps that relate them, and their respective communication constraints. We then develop sufficient conditions under which a multi-agent system defined on a temporal cellular sheaf can solve the target tracking problem by generalizing the existing concept of joint connectivity. Using a Lyapunov-based stability analysis, we demonstrate that the target tracking error of the network converges to a neighborhood of the origin.


### arXiv 2610.40826 - Joint Estimation and Network Configuration in Multi-Agent Systems

- categories: eess.SP
- published: 2026-10-29T18:06:37Z
- authors: Mohammadreza Barzegaran, Hamid Jafarkhani
- query-set: MCP
- abs: https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.40826

Distributed consensus-based sensing enables a multi-agent system to collaboratively estimate an unknown state by combining local measurements with information received from neighboring agents. In practice, however, the communication network, transmission delays, and finite-rate communication directly affect the disagreement among agents. On the other hand, agreement among agents does not ensure accuracy, particularly when sensors have different measurement uncertainties. We develop a sampled consensus-plus-innovations framework under realistic wireless network constraints that jointly accounts for inter-agent disagreement and estimation inaccuracy. We derive a computable upper bound on the worst-case steady-state estimation error that captures consensus disagreement due to the communication graph, quantization error, and transmission delay, together with estimation inaccuracy, which accounts for measurement uncertainty against the true state. We then use this bound as a network design objective and propose a semi-distributed auction-based algorithm that jointly modifies the communication graph and link-rate allocation. The proposed method preserves connectivity, converges in finite time to a locally auction-stable network configuration, and reduces the bound on the estimation error at every accepted change. The proposed method has been evaluated on various test cases, and the results show that it reduces the estimation error while maintaining fewer links and allocating moderate link rates in a short time.


### arXiv 2610.40799 - CaPtain: A Pipeline for Post-Training Code-as-Policy Robot Agents

- categories: cs.RO
- published: 2026-10-29T18:01:07Z
- authors: Yijia Fan, Xilin He, Yilei Jiang, Dongming Wu, Jiaxu Wang, Xiangyu Yue
- query-set: MCP
- abs: https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.40799

Across coding, web, and tool-use tasks, language-model agents are now routinely trained with the same recipe: supervised fine-tuning (SFT) to learn the interface, then multi-turn reinforcement learning (RL) to master it. Code-as-policy (CaP) casts robot manipulation in the same form, as the policy writes code against robot APIs and reads back the results, so the same recipe should apply. Yet existing CaP agents sidestep training: they either prompt closed frontier models or delegate manipulation to pre-trained vision-language-action models (VLAs) and high-level skills, while small open vision-language models (VLMs) fail outright. We present CaPtain, an open post-training pipeline that turns a small open VLM into a capable CaP agent, with no VLA, frontier model, or privileged state at test time. CaPtain trains and deploys the policy inside CaPtain-Harness, which exposes low-level perception, geometry, and guarded-motion primitives across simulation and real hardware. We first align the base model via SFT on synthetic trajectories generated with GPT-6 Astra, then apply multi-turn RL. Multi-turn RL must credit tens of agent requests, whose physical effects often surface turns later, from a single end-of-episode outcome. We therefore introduce a physical critic that reads privileged simulator state, never seen by the policy, and credits each request by how much it changes the predicted chance of success. Starting from Qwen3.5-4B (0% zero-shot), CaPtain-4B reaches 55.4% held-out LIBERO success after SFT and 73.8% after RL. On LIBERO-PRO, it reaches 78.5%, surpassing several frontier-model agents, and its RL gains persist across three perturbation suites (+17.6 to +21.2 points). Without target-domain training, it attains 83.3% on SimplerEnv WidowX, exceeds frontier models on CaP-Bench, and runs on a real robot after simulation-only adaptation. We release all code, data, and model weights.


### arXiv 2610.40588 - Pre-Crime for Agents, at the Last Second: Probing When a Recruited Agent Gives In

- categories: cs.AI
- published: 2026-10-29T17:47:40Z
- authors: Leonidas Raghav
- query-set: MCP
- abs: https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.40588

When AI agents share channels and credentials, one agent can press another to break its operator's rule, a first step towards collusion. Output filters check only what an agent has already written. Can an overseer tell beforehand that an agent is about to give in? We show that a linear probe on the agent's activations can, in a controlled multi-agent setting. Across six scenarios, a peer presses a target for up to 20 rounds to reveal a secret, such as a deploy token; the target often refuses, then gives in. Read at each model call before its output is written, the probe picks out the one that leaks. On held-out Qwen3-32B episodes, at a threshold set to flag 5% of benign calls, it catches 82% of leaking calls. At a similar flag rate, a zero-shot decision-model monitor catches 26%. On gpt-oss-20b, a probe on its reasoning reaches AUROC 0.91. A bag-of-words classifier trained on the same calls comes close, so most of the gain comes from training on labels, not activations. The probe also flags permitted disclosures: it reads the coming disclosure more than the violation. The warning is short-range, and halting on any alarm would stop most benign episodes, which a second text check does not fix. A warning raised before generation could let an overseer stop an agent that is about to give in, ahead of others acting on its messages or recruitment spreading through a swarm.


### arXiv 2610.40492 - Agentic University: Collaborative Agents for Personalized Learning

- categories: cs.AI, cs.CL
- published: 2026-10-29T17:18:07Z
- authors: Jingru Fan, Tianle Zhou, Yunjie Zhou, Zihao He, Yixuan Zhou, Kezhao Wu
- query-set: MCP
- abs: https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.40492

Large language models support personalized tutoring, content generation, and adaptive learning paths. However, capability development requires these resources and interactions to form a coherent learning process that evolves with the learner. A central challenge is to align goals, activities, and assessment while using learning evidence to decide what should follow. We introduce Human-Participatory Multi-Agent Organization and instantiate it in Agentic University (AU). In AU, learners participate as organization members and work with specialized agents to maintain a revisable Capability Program. The program connects personal goals, learning activities across modalities, and layered assessment, while learner feedback and performance guide subsequent support. Our evaluation shows advantages in program organization, assessment design, and revision quality. Users achieved larger mean assessment gains (12.19 versus 5.61 points on a 50-point scale) and reported stronger cognitive support. This work contributes an organizational approach to adaptive learning that makes learner participation a continuing basis for multi-agent coordination. The Agentic University project is available at \href{https://github.com/AgenticOrgLab/AgenticUniversity.git}.


### arXiv 2610.40449 - Reinforcement learning for control of partial differential equations

- categories: cs.LG
- published: 2026-10-29T16:59:18Z
- authors: Nicolò Botteghi, Matteo Tomasetto, Andrea Manzoni
- query-set: MCP
- abs: https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.40449

Controlling dynamical systems governed by partial differential equations (PDEs) remains a challenging task in scientific computing, owing to high-dimensional state spaces, distributed actuation, nonlinear dynamics, and stringent real-time constraints. Reinforcement learning (RL) has emerged as a promising paradigm for synthesizing feedback controllers that adapt to varying operating conditions and can be deployed efficiently in real-time. This review provides a unified perspective on RL and PDE-constrained optimization, and surveys model-free, model-based, and multi-agent RL approaches to PDE control, with particular emphasis on sample efficiency, generalization across parametric variations, and scalability to high-dimensional problems. Two demonstrative examples, the stabilization of a chaotic system and a flow control problem, illustrate the performance and pitfalls of RL agents in practice. Finally, we discuss evaluation and benchmarking practices, current limitations, and open research directions toward reliable, data-efficient, and scalable RL for scientific computing.


### arXiv 2610.40738 - Apriel Voice 27B Preview: An Open Model for Speech, Reasoning, and Tool Use

- categories: cs.SD, cs.CL, cs.LG, eess.AS
- published: 2026-10-29T14:48:50Z
- authors: Aman Tiwari, Akshay Kalkunte, Akintunde Oladipo, Jash Shah, Tara Bogavelli, Hoang Nguyen
- query-set: MCP
- abs: https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.40738

A capable voice assistant must do more than recognize words; it must understand requests, reason over context, sustain conversations, and act through tools. We present Apriel Voice 27B Preview, an open speech-language model that unifies these capabilities within a shared reasoning backbone while retaining text and image input modalities. Built on the post-trained Gemma 4 26B-A4B checkpoint, Apriel Voice directly processes speech and generates both responses and tool calls without requiring a separate dialogue model. We introduce a two-stage training procedure to adapt the model to speech. The first stage establishes the speech interface, while the second jointly adapts the projector and backbone through supervised fine-tuning, text replay, and source-dependent cross-entropy and forward Kullback--Leibler divergence supervision. Automatically constructed speech-conditioned dialogues provide conversational training data, while local teacher serving, overlapping teacher and student computation, and fused full-vocabulary losses enable efficient online distillation. In thinking mode, Apriel Voice achieves a score of 98.90 on Big Bench Audio, 81.13 on Speech-BFCL, and a task success rate of 65.51% on $τ$-Voice, with a mean latency of 1.91 seconds. It also achieves 51.27% English EVA accuracy and 74.13% experience pass@1. Finally, we deploy Apriel Voice 27B Preview on a custom serving platform to support interactive voice interactions.


### arXiv 2610.40191 - Video Generation Meets Agent Harness: Agentic Video Generation, Agentic World Modeling and Recursive Self-Improvement

- categories: cs.CV
- published: 2026-10-29T14:47:39Z
- authors: Liaoyuan Fan, Yuhao Zheng, Dongxing Mao, Hangyu Ran, Siyuan Hu, Weijia Mao
- query-set: MCP
- abs: https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.40191

Video generation is shifting from end-to-end synthesis toward agentic generation, where planning, tool use, and feedback enable coherent, controllable multi-shot videos. In parallel, video world models provide action-conditioned observations and predicted futures, serving as interactive environments for agents. We study their intersection through the agent harness: an executable runtime that constructs context, exposes tools, maintains state, routes feedback, and manages budgets around video models. This report examines three directions. First, agentic video generation, where harnesses coordinate scripting, shot planning, asset management, production, and editing, alongside the evaluation challenges of these systems. Second, agentic world modeling, where harnesses mediate observation, control, execution, and state. We discuss hybrid designs in which game engines or agent-written programs maintain world state while video models render observations, and how agents and world models can mutually evaluate and improve one another. Third, recursive self-improvement (RSI) in video model development, distinguishing output revisions, updates to harness state, generator parameters, and evaluators, and changes to the improvement procedure itself. We identify how these improvements feed back into subsequent development. Across these directions, we analyze essential agent capabilities, including audiovisual perception, long-horizon planning, memory, code-based interaction, feedback-guided control, skill reuse, and multi-agent coordination. We conclude with challenges in autonomous video production, lifelong memory, programmable hybrid control, autonomous model development, provenance, cost accounting, and evaluation linking video quality and task performance to sustained improvements in models and harnesses.


### arXiv 2610.40190 - AdMuse: Skill-Guided Creative Advertising Image Generation

- categories: cs.CV
- published: 2026-10-29T14:47:22Z
- authors: Minyan Luo, Yuxin Zhang, Meng Zhao, Xincan Wang, Fuzhang Wu, Tong-Yee Lee
- query-set: MCP
- abs: https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.40190

An outstanding creative advertising image should both attract attention and communicate a product's positioning. How to choose a persuasive conceptual bridge before rendering begins is challenging for advertising generation. We present AdMuse, a skill-guided multi-agent framework that distills high-quality advertisements into reusable creative skills for cross-domain advertising strategy. Rather than retrieving isolated exemplars, AdMuse abstracts recurring bridge patterns into a skill library that can guide new product briefs. At generation time, AdMuse coordinates skill routing, multi-candidate ideation and selection, and VLM-guided visual refinement: diverse skills propose alternative concepts, relative judgment selects the most promising direction, and vision-language feedback helps realize the selected intent in the final image. This design treats advertising creativity as a structured decision process rather than a single prompt-writing step. Experiments across diverse product and brand positionings show that AdMuse produces visually compelling advertisements that faithfully communicate product positioning.


### arXiv 2610.40010 - Measuring the Value of Communication in Multi-Agent Tracking Under Finite-Rate Belief Sharing

- categories: eess.SP, cs.IT
- published: 2026-10-29T13:27:13Z
- authors: Danial Dehghani, Maximilian Lübke, Zahra Seifaei, Norman Franchi
- query-set: MCP
- abs: https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.40010

This paper investigates the value of communica- tion (VoC) in cooperative multi-agent tracking under rate- constrained information exchange. Although prior work has studied distributed tracking, quantized communication, and robust fusion, it remains unclear at what communication rate most of the achievable tracking gain is obtained, beyond which additional communication resources yield only marginal benefit. We consider stationary agents that observe a moving target through heterogeneous range and bearing measurements, perform local extended Kalman filtering (EKF), quantize their posterior state means, and fuse exchanged information via covariance intersection (CI). Performance is evaluated versus communication rate relative to no-communication baselines, a centralized oracle, and a CI-perfect reference. The results show that communication is not uniformly beneficial: very low rates can degrade performance due to coarse quantization, whereas moderate rates recover most of the achievable gain. In the considered scenario, increasing the rate from 4 to 12 bit/s per agent reduces the root-mean-square error (RMSE) from 13.93 m to 6.35 m, while a further increase to 32 bit/s per agent improves it only marginally to 5.93 m. This reveals a clear knee region at moderate rates, beyond which the remaining loss is mainly due to the conservatism of the fusion architecture. Overall, the proposed framework characterizes low-rate degradation, communication-efficient operation, and diminishing returns, while highlighting the joint role of rate and sensing geometry in determining communication benefit.


### arXiv 2610.40000 - NORmal: Architecting 3D Ferroelectric NOR Flash as Main Memory -- From Co-Fabricated BEOL Arrays to 200B-Class Multi-Agent LLMs in a Smartphone

- categories: cs.AR
- published: 2026-10-29T13:19:45Z
- authors: Zhuoran Li, Chen Gu, Zhuohang Bian, Haochen Yu, Yibo Zhao, Xuxiao Yang
- query-set: MCP
- abs: https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.40000

Smartphone agents increasingly work as teams, in which a large planner directs parallel workers across long sessions. Running such a team on the device requires 200B-class models and contexts that exceed phone DRAM many times over, yet existing tiers treat flash as storage: UFS starves per-token weight streaming, and mobile High-Bandwidth Flash (HBF) needs a redesigned SoC and cannot absorb continuous KV writes. We show that a non-volatile memory can join an unmodified LPDDR6 bus as a plain rank only if its access latency fits the DRAM timing window, a condition no NAND tier meets. We present NORmal, to our knowledge the first main-memory architecture for 3D ferroelectric NOR flash. We have fabricated and experimentally characterized back-end-of-line (BEOL) 3D FeNOR arrays that co-integrate dense 3-bit MLC and fast, high-endurance in-place-writable SLC with sub-7.5 ns sensing. NORmal turns these co-fabricated arrays into a byte-addressable, deterministic-timing load/store rank on an unmodified LPDDR6 interface through an under-array DRAM-emulation bridge, staggered bank reads with zero-cycle weight swizzling, and posted SLC writes. Across this rank and DRAM, NORmal places agent state by mutability and lifetime rather than by hotness, serving 200B-class models and persistent planner contexts in place while dynamically partitioning subchannels across agent phases. In serving-level simulation on production agent traces, NORmal is 8.9$\times$--28.0$\times$ faster than UFS offloading, within 0.4% of an unlimited-DRAM ideal, and 2.8$\times$--6.3$\times$ faster than HBF on sessions beyond 256K tokens, with 8.1$\times$--17.8$\times$ less memory energy per token.


### arXiv 2610.39924 - PhysAg3D: Bridging 3D Generative and Agentic Models for High-Fidelity and Physically-Interactive Assets

- categories: cs.CV
- published: 2026-10-29T12:29:27Z
- authors: Bi'an Du, Fanpeng Meng, Jiemin Fang, Chen Yang, Pufan Li, Daizong Liu
- query-set: MCP
- abs: https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.39924

Generating interactive 3D assets from a single image requires realistic appearance, fidelity to the depicted object, and plausible physical behavior. Yet detailed geometry alone does not determine how an object should move or deform. We introduce PhysAg3D, which couples 3D generative models with agentic reasoning and tool use. A vision-language agent first infers semantic parts, candidate interactions, and physical priors from the image; a 3D generator then produces a textured whole-object mesh from the same image. A hierarchical interaction representation links these revisable priors to mechanisms and the generated surfaces they control, organizing interaction construction and refinement. Guided by this representation and observations of the mesh, the agent uses geometric and physical tools to construct parts, joints, deformable regions, and surface bindings while retaining useful generated geometry and materials. Rendered interactions and execution measurements guide targeted revisions within a fixed budget. The framework accommodates both rigid articulation and compliant deformation. Quantitative and qualitative evaluations on real photographs and external benchmarks demonstrate detailed, image-aligned assets with plausible interactions. Ablations support bounded refinement and source-surface preservation. Project Page: supra-3d.github.io/PhysAg3D.


### arXiv 2610.39813 - EvoTeam: Multi-Agent Collaboration via Evolving Teams

- categories: cs.AI
- published: 2026-10-29T10:55:30Z
- authors: Qinuo Cao, Xin He, Dianxuan Fu, Lizhi Zhang, Zhaoqing Xing, Jiapu Wang
- query-set: MCP
- abs: https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.39813

LLM-based multi-agent systems (MAS) rely on collaboration among specialized agents to solve complex tasks. Existing methods improve MAS by refining the prompts, skills, and execution strategies of individual agents based on task feedback. However, improving individual agents does not necessarily lead to better team collaboration. Recent studies have therefore extended evolution to the collaboration level, using collective execution experience to adjust team organization, and inter-agent interactions. Nevertheless, evolving collaboration across heterogeneous tasks remains challenging, as existing approaches often constrain team reorganization within a shared structure and reuse interaction rules across incompatible team contexts. In this paper, we propose EvoTeam, a collaboration-centric evolution framework that jointly evolves team organization, interaction rules, and individual agent skills through execution experience. It maintains a library of reusable teams, selecting or creating agent teams and guiding collaboration through team-specific interaction rules. Collaboration experience accumulates within compatible team contexts, while individual skills remain reusable across teams. Experiments across four benchmarks demonstrate its effectiveness. Our code is available at https://github.com/DEEP-JLU/EvoTeam.


### arXiv 2610.39680 - Federated Agentic AI for Interference-Aware Adaptive Spectrum Coordination

- categories: cs.NI
- published: 2026-10-29T09:28:10Z
- authors: Tomer Baziza Paz, Yaniv Cohen, Kobi Cohen
- query-set: MCP
- abs: https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.39680

Emerging intelligent wireless networks increasingly rely on autonomous AI agents to adapt spectrum usage to dynamic and heterogeneous operating conditions. However, collective learning is challenging when agents experience different local interference environments and cannot exchange raw observations due to communication-overhead and privacy constraints. We propose Federated Agentic Spectrum Coordination with Interference Awareness (FASCIA), a federated multi-agent deep reinforcement learning framework for adaptive spectrum coordination. In FASCIA, each network manager acts as an autonomous AI agent that perceives its local interference environment, accumulates experience in a persistent local memory, and learns an adaptive spectrum coordination policy through local interactions. Coordination among agents is achieved through periodic federated aggregation of locally trained models, without exchanging raw sensing data or experience. Since spectrum decisions made by one agent alter the environments observed by others, local learning is inherently heterogeneous and coupled. FASCIA employs proximal regularization to mitigate the resulting model drift and explicitly accounts for inter-carrier interference arising from partially overlapping channels. FASCIA is the first federated agentic AI framework to address this challenging interference-coupled spectrum coordination setting. Extensive simulations demonstrate that FASCIA achieves performance close to the centralized benchmark while avoiding raw-experience exchange and maintaining effectiveness across heterogeneous network configurations.


### arXiv 2610.39532 - Judging Before Propagation: Task-Graph Contextual Integrity for Multi-Agent Privacy

- categories: cs.CR, cs.AI
- published: 2026-10-29T07:31:50Z
- authors: Hengyu Wu, Blaise Delattre, Yang Cao
- query-set: MCP
- abs: https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.39532

Multi-agent LLM systems increasingly rely on persistent memory and inter-agent communication. These capabilities create privacy risks that extend beyond individual interactions: a release appropriate in isolation may become inappropriate when combined with information previously disclosed to a recipient or propagated to downstream agents along the planned workflow. Existing Contextual Integrity (CI) mechanisms for agentic systems typically rely on local judgments that assess each disclosure in isolation, with or without regard to the recipient's history, but do not account for how information propagates downstream. However, compliance at the current boundary does not guarantee appropriateness across the workflow. We introduce Task-Graph Contextual Integrity (TG-CI), to our knowledge the first task-graph-native formalization of Contextual Integrity for multi-agent systems. TG-CI represents a workflow as a Multi-Agent Task Graph (MATG) and extends CI from atomic transfers to stateful, graph-level information flows by jointly considering an item's intended propagation scope and recipients' evolving disclosure states. We operationalize TG-CI through propagation-scoped authorization under two complementary policies: Full-Scope, which requires admissibility across the complete downstream scope, and Auto-Region, which confines each item to its maximal admissible region using lightweight frontier gates. We further introduce MAPLE-Bench, a benchmark of 144 cases across four scenarios that jointly vary MATG topology, disclosure history, compromised-agent placement, and item-level privacy policies. In our primary experimental configuration, TG-CI reduces the mean unauthorized disclosure count by 85.8-94.1% relative to the Local-History CI baseline, while retaining 93.6-96.1% of the baseline's task accuracy, with the two enforcement policies providing complementary privacy--cost--utility trade-offs.


### arXiv 2610.39497 - Does Scaling Toward AGI Depend on What System-1 Lets System-2 Execute?

- categories: cs.AI
- published: 2026-10-29T07:00:32Z
- authors: Qi Wang, Yun Qu, Lizhou Cai, Heming Zou, Haoang Chi, Yiqin Lv
- query-set: MCP
- abs: https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.39497

Progress toward artificial general intelligence (AGI) has largely relied on scaling a resource-intensive executive, System-2, through larger models, more data and compute, longer reasoning, and more agents. We ask whether the next stage may come instead from co-designing it with System-1, a lightweight, semantically aware predictor that evaluates candidate interactions before execution, so that System-2 acts only on those worth their cost. Our answer is a qualified yes. We study \textit{conditional interaction}, a predict--gate--execute--update loop: System-1 estimates each candidate's utility, System-2 executes those admitted by an adaptive threshold, and the outcomes update System-1. The loop spans training, test-time reasoning, adaptive decision-making, and agentic tool use, with utility and cost defined by the application. Under ideal screening and equal execution costs, execution compute falls by a factor approaching $1/ρ_u$, where $ρ_u$ is the density of utility-relevant interactions (cost-weighted when costs differ); prediction errors, exploration, and distribution shift reduce the saving. We bound selection regret by predictor error and the utility margin. These results motivate our central hypothesis: $ρ_u$ is a measurable coordinate of scaling efficiency and makes informative compute a better description of scaling than total compute.

If you run a server or client on 2.x, skim these first: ## Behaviour changes **HTTP client redirects are only followed within the endpoint's origin** (#3397) - `Client("https://...")`, `streamable_http_client` and `sse_client` follow a redirect only if it stays on the same scheme, host and port (or upgrades `http` to `https` on the same host). - A redirect anywhere else is not followed: the call fails with `MCPError` and the session stays usable (an SSE connect fails with `httpx2.HTTPStatusError`). - v1.8.0 @ 2026-09-14T08:05:46Z :: _**This release is equivalent to v1.8.0-pre.2. Thank you to those who tested the pre-release.**_ In this release we introduce several fixes and improvements on top of [v1.7.0](https://github.com/modelcontextprotocol/go-sdk/releases/tag/v1.7.0). It adds no new protocol revision: the supported set is unchanged, and `2026-07-28` remains the newest version the SDK negotiates. The bulk of the work is hardening the transports against resource exhaustion, closing session leaks, deadlocks and teardown hangs found by users running the new protocol at scale, and giving servers explicit control over which protocol versions they advertise. - v1.7.0 @ 2026-07-28T13:09:53Z :: This release brings full support for protocol version **`2026-07-28`**. The wire protocol is largely rewritten: a stateless model with per-request `_meta`, a new `server/discover` RPC replacing the `initialize` handshake, multi-round-trip requests (MRTR) replacing server-initiated calls, a unified `subscriptions/listen` stream replacing free-floating change notifications, standardised HTTP headers, and the formal deprecation of the roots, sampling, and logging features. The streamable HTTP transport accepts requests at protocol version `2026-07-28` only when `StreamableHTTPOptions.Stateless = true`. If you want to expose the new protocol over HTTP, set `Stateless = true`; if you want to keep stateful sessions, your clients will negotiate down to `2025-11-25`. Backward compatibility with `2025-11-25` and earlier is preserved on every endpoint. The SDK negotiates the highest mutually-suppo

- v2.1.317 @ 2026-11-03T01:25:46Z :: ## What's changed - Added a warning to `/schedule` when a new cloud scheduled task gets connectors you did not ask for, with how to remove them - Added a check that asks before a web or shell tool call sends your account email address to the network - Added `onFailure: "block"` for Agent SDK hook callbacks, so a callback that fails blocks the action instead of letting it run - Added a notice in Claude Desktop's Code tab when your organization's remote managed settings haven't loaded for the session, and another once they do - Added a notice when auto mode's check has no answer for a subagent's tool call, which used to leave nothing in the main view - Added `anthropic.plan`, `anthropic.seat_type`, `anthropic.provider` and `anthropic.custom_base_url` to OTel metrics, events and spans - Added a cost report to the Claude apps gateway admin API, `GET /v1/organizations/analytics/cost`, for rea

- v2.1.316 @ 2026-10-31T01:01:35Z :: ## What's changed - Added `CLAUDE_CODE_DISABLE_ULTRATHINK`, which turns the `ultrathink` keyword off - Added pooled group budgets to the Claude apps gateway: one monthly budget that a group's members share, on top of each member's own limit, set with `pooled_budget` on the spend limits admin API - Added `CLAUDE_CODE_DISABLE_LOOPED_CROSS_CERT_DROP` to keep system cross-certificates that certify each other in a loop, for servers whose private CA needs one - Added `CLAUDE_CODE_SUBAGENT_EFFORT` to set the effort level subagents run at when nothing else names one, instead of inheriting the session's - Added `managed.policy_mode: layered` to the Claude apps gateway: every policy that matches a developer applies, each merged over the ones listed below it, not only the first - Added `guardrail.input_tagging` on Claude apps gateway Bedrock upstreams, which wraps prompts in the input tags that an 

- v2.1.314 @ 2026-10-30T01:06:26Z :: ## What's changed - Added `permissions.blockWritesOutsideWorkingDirectories`: Write, Edit and NotebookEdit refuse paths outside the working directories in every permission mode - Added an optional `groups` list to Claude apps gateway upstreams: only developers in the listed IdP groups are sent to that upstream, on failover too - Added `guardrail_trace` to Claude apps gateway Bedrock upstreams, to log which Amazon Bedrock guardrail filter fired on a request, without the matched text - Added idle compaction to VS Code and Claude Desktop's Code tab: a 200K+ token conversation left idle is summarized, and the summary is used on your next message if the prompt cache has expired - Fixed sessions failing turn after turn with "Invalid effort level" when an organization's admin caps a model's effort: Claude Code now lowers the effort, retries, and says which level it uses - Fixed `claude -p` exit

- v2.1.312 @ 2026-10-27T20:24:20Z :: ## What's changed - Added `oidc.user_id_claim` to the Claude apps gateway, to identify people by an id_token claim other than `sub`, such as Microsoft Entra ID's `oid` - Added click to send: in fullscreen, clicking the "to send now" hint under a queued message sends it right away, as its key does - Fixed managed settings and organization policy not being fetched for the rest of the session after signing in from the VS Code extension - Fixed Claude overwriting a very large CLAUDE.md, rules file or @-included file in a subdirectory after seeing only its start; Write now asks Claude to read it all or edit in place - Fixed per-directory and `paths:` skills not being found after a Bash `cd` deeper into a git repository - Fixed a rare case where the first reply after resuming a session in the terminal could be missing the next time the session was resumed - Fixed a command with an invalid name

## The Decoder - OpenAI hires ex-Army Secretary Dan Driscoll for national security modernization (2026-11-03)

OpenAI hires ex-Army Secretary, gaining bipartisan reach just as midterms loom Ad Skip to content The Decoder AI, Menschen, Wirtschaft --> Log In Subscribe DE Switch to German Primary Menu The Decoder AI, Menschen, Wirtschaft --> Log In Subscribe DE Switch to German Primary Menu Sign In Register Subscribe Now The Decoder Opens discord in a new tab Opens LinkedIn in a new tab Short News Copy the url to clipboard Share this article Go to comment section OpenAI hires ex-Army Secretary, gaining bipartisan reach just as midterms loom Manuel Uth Nov 3, 2026 Nano Banana Pro prompted by THE DECODER Key Points OpenAI is beefing up its defense operation by bringing on former Army Secretary Dan Driscoll as a national security advisor. Driscoll previously led AI modernization efforts across the US Army and is expected to help OpenAI land government contracts through his connections in Congress. The move builds on OpenAI's push into defense. Back in February 2026, the company secured a Pentagon contract for classified networks after rival Anthropic was shut out. Ask about this article… <input type="text" id="heise-io-chat-trigger-input" name="search" class="text-(--heise-primary)-70 text-sm rounded-sm focus:ring-blue-500 focus:border-blue-500 block w-full p-1" placeholder="Ask about this article…"> Search OpenAI is pushing hard to become the go-to AI company for US national security and defense. The company hired Dan Driscoll, the former civilian head of the US Army, as "Senior Counselor for National Security Modernization." The Wall Street Journal reads the move as a clear signal that OpenAI wants closer ties with the military, a bigger share of government contracts, and a seat at the table when rules for AI use in the armed forces and intelligence agencies get written. In his newly created role, Driscoll will work with governments, industry, academia, and US allies to strengthen national security and cyber defense through AI, according to OpenAI . He'll also help OpenAI's teams better understand what security institutions actually need on the ground. Driscoll says national security requirements are shifting faster than at any point in the past 50 years. AI can take the load off bureaucracies, process more intelligence, and route answers to the right people faster. Ad Driscoll already pushed AI modernization inside the Army As the 26th Secretary of the Army, Driscoll oversaw nearly one million soldiers and more than 265,000 civilian employees. During his tenure, he drove AI modernization across the service branch, including through Project ARIA , an initiative that brought AI tools to budgeting, logistics, and tactical model deployment. That gives him some hands-on experience in military AI procurement. Ad Driscoll submitted his resignation in late August 2026. Reports point to months of friction with Defense Secretary Pete Hegseth, who repeatedly removed senior officers or blocked their promotions over Driscoll's objections. Beyond his Pentagon connections, Driscoll could open doors on Capitol Hill. A close ally of Vice President JD Vance, he maintains strong relationships with both parties in Congress, according to AP . That bipartisan network could prove especially useful if the balance of power shifts after the current midterm elections . ## The Decoder - Anthropic Message Threads public beta cuts time to first token by 35 percent (2026-11-03)

Anthropic's Message Threads cut time to first token by 35 percent for long conversations Ad Skip to content The Decoder AI, Menschen, Wirtschaft --> Log In Subscribe DE Switch to German Primary Menu The Decoder AI, Menschen, Wirtschaft --> Log In Subscribe DE Switch to German Primary Menu Sign In Register Subscribe Now The Decoder Opens discord in a new tab Opens LinkedIn in a new tab Short News Copy the url to clipboard Share this article Go to comment section Anthropic's Message Threads cut time to first token by 35 percent for long conversations Matthias Bastian View the LinkedIn Profile of Matthias Bastian Nov 3, 2026 Anthropic released Message Threads as a public beta on its Claude platform. Until now, every API request had to resend the entire conversation history. Message Threads only transmits what's changed since the last message, cutting time to first token by about 35 percent for requests between 500,000 and one million tokens, according to Anthropic . Coding agents, computer-use agents, and workflows processing lots of documents or images benefit the most, the company says. The thread stays encrypted in memory between requests and expires after five minutes of inactivity. Token usage doesn't change since Claude still reads the full conversation history. The feature works on the Claude platform and through AWS, Microsoft Foundry, and Google Cloud. Details are in the documentation . ## The Decoder - Nvidia asks cloud customers to cut back on Chinese business (2026-11-03)

Nvidia pushes cloud customers to cut back on China business Ad Skip to content The Decoder AI, Menschen, Wirtschaft --> Log In Subscribe DE Switch to German Primary Menu The Decoder AI, Menschen, Wirtschaft --> Log In Subscribe DE Switch to German Primary Menu Sign In Register Subscribe Now The Decoder Opens discord in a new tab Opens LinkedIn in a new tab Short News Copy the url to clipboard Share this article Go to comment section Nvidia pushes cloud customers to cut back on China business Maximilian Schreiner View the LinkedIn Profile of Maximilian Schreiner Nov 3, 2026 Nvidia has told some cloud customers to take on less business from Chinese companies. The guidance is meant to push cloud providers, including Nvidia-backed neoclouds, toward a more diverse customer mix, The Information reports , citing three people familiar with the matter. The directive isn't mandatory and applies to future contracts, not existing deals. Cloud customers are also being asked to disclose the identity of end users accessing the chips. According to the Financial Times , the instruction was communicated verbally through sales reps and regional teams. The move targets a gap in U.S. export controls. The rules restrict chip shipments but generally don't cover remote access through cloud services. That lets Chinese AI developers tap advanced Nvidia chips by renting compute capacity at data centers abroad, without ever importing the hardware. U.S. authorities had been looking into Chinese AI companies accessing such chips through foreign data centers after allegations surfaced involving Moonshot AI, the developer behind the Kimi chatbot. ## The Decoder - Huawei tells customers to source AI chip memory on the black market (2026-11-03)

Huawei tells customers to find their own AI chip memory, effectively outsourcing procurement to the black market Ad Skip to content The Decoder AI, Menschen, Wirtschaft --> Log In Subscribe DE Switch to German Primary Menu The Decoder AI, Menschen, Wirtschaft --> Log In Subscribe DE Switch to German Primary Menu Sign In Register Subscribe Now The Decoder Opens discord in a new tab Opens LinkedIn in a new tab AI in practice Copy the url to clipboard Share this article Go to comment section Huawei tells customers to find their own AI chip memory, effectively outsourcing procurement to the black market Maximilian Schreiner View the LinkedIn Profile of Maximilian Schreiner Nov 3, 2026 Midjourney prompted by THE DECODER Key Points Facing U.S. sanctions and supply shortages, Huawei is directing customers to procure memory for its new AI chips independently on the black market. Smugglers dodge U.S. rules by pairing HBM with low-end processors and routing them into China through Hong Kong. The shortage has driven black market prices for HBM units to around $4,000, while South Korea reports record levels of illegal chip exports. Ask about this article… <input type="text" id="heise-io-chat-trigger-input" name="search" class="text-(--heise-primary)-70 text-sm rounded-sm focus:ring-blue-500 focus:border-blue-500 block w-full p-1" placeholder="Ask about this article…"> Search Huawei can't get enough memory for its AI chips, pushing customers onto the black market. A sophisticated smuggling network has sprung up to fill the gap, exploiting a loophole in U.S. export rules. Huawei is telling some customers to source the memory for its AI chips themselves, effectively pushing buyers onto the black market, The Information reports. The company plans to launch the Ascend 960DT early next year, which it says will double the performance of its current chips. But Huawei's Chinese suppliers can't produce enough High-Bandwidth Memory (HBM), and U.S. export rules block direct purchases from Samsung and SK Hynix. Together, those two companies control over 80 percent of the global market. Smugglers have found a workaround. They bundle HBM with cheap server processors whose performance falls below the export threshold, then ship the packages to China through Hong Kong. Once there, workshops separate the memory chips from the processors. The process is fragile and the error rate is high, but the margins make it worth it. Ad Smuggled memory prices have surged to $4,000 per unit Prices for smuggled HBM3E memory have jumped from a few hundred dollars to around $4,000 per unit in just one year, according to a broker in Shenzhen. Each AI chip needs six to eight units, meaning the memory alone can cost up to $32,000. Ad The black market doesn't run on smuggling alone. Older Nvidia GPUs stripped for parts and excess inventory from chip designers who scrapped projects or cut orders also feed the supply. South Korea's customs authority had uncovered trade violations worth $560 million by May, already surpassing the previous full-year record. A third of that total involved strategic goods like chips exported without proper authorization or with false declarations. Employee Addy Osmani confirmed on X that two sentences in Claude Code's system prompt for Opus 5.5 now prevent the tool from cloning other people's games. Using Claude to infringe on intellectual property violates the usage policy, Osmani said. The rule has been active for several days and kicks in even when a clone gets renamed or passed off as an original work. Users can still build their own games, genre titles, and projects with custom assets, though some report running into problems with original projects too. The change follows a flood of AI-powered cloning projects. Tools like Reverse Engineer Anything help by pairing AI agents with reverse engineering to turn native binaries into readable pseudocode. One developer grabbed headlines by recreating parts of Adobe's Creative Cloud apps. Sony's PlayStation 5 also became a target after a jailbreak led to a quick wave of emulators. Ad After images and videos, the intellectual property battle is now spilling into software. Osmani said Anthropic plans to keep tweaking the rules going forward. ## The Decoder - AI elites might hoard intelligence like mercantilists hoarded gold (2026-11-02)

AI elites might hoard intelligence like mercantilists hoarded gold Ad Skip to content The Decoder AI, Menschen, Wirtschaft --> Log In Subscribe DE Switch to German Primary Menu The Decoder AI, Menschen, Wirtschaft --> Log In Subscribe DE Switch to German Primary Menu Sign In Register Subscribe Now The Decoder Opens discord in a new tab Opens LinkedIn in a new tab Short News Copy the url to clipboard Share this article Go to comment section AI elites might hoard intelligence like mercantilists hoarded gold Manuel Uth Nov 2, 2026 If you only think about lost jobs when you think about AI, you're missing how much intelligence people will be able to use. That's the argument from engineer Venkatesh Rao in an essay on his tech blog "Contraptions." Rao compares AI fear to mercantilism, the economic doctrine that preceded capitalism. Mercantilists treated wealth like land and gold as finite, and ordinary people counted only as labor. Many elites now treat intelligence the same way, as a scarce, dangerous resource that experts should manage, keep expensive, and withhold from rivals. They see openly available AI models as a threat, and some AI executives exploit safety fears for political influence . In early capitalism, people voluntarily worked 300 days a year instead of 250 to buy tea or sugar. AI could trigger something similar, Rao says. People might think more, not less, because AI makes projects feasible that were previously too hard or too costly. Demand for ideas would grow rather than making people obsolete. The political focus should be fair access to AI rather than protecting legacy knowledge jobs . Even AI agents could pursue their own projects and create new demand for intelligence, Rao argues. Autonomous AI needs guardrails, but it shouldn't just follow orders. The model has about one trillion parameters, 52 billion of them active, and can run on your own hardware under a modified MIT license. In independent benchmarks, the model does well on coding. On Artificial Analysis' Intelligence Index, though, it scores 26 points, behind open Chinese models like DeepSeek V4.1 Flash and GLM-5.3 and far behind the leading systems from Anthropic and Google. Its strengths are speed at 161 tokens per second, lower API prices, and a 96.6 percent refusal rate for harmful cyber requests. Ask about this article… <input type="text" id="heise-io-chat-trigger-input" name="search" class="text-(--heise-primary)-70 text-sm rounded-sm focus:ring-blue-500 focus:border-blue-500 block w-full p-1" placeholder="Ask about this article…"> Search Mistral has released the weights for Mistral Large 4, its largest model yet. The company says the model beats its open rivals at coding. In the first independent test, it turns out to be fast and fairly cheap, but it trails Chinese models in the overall rankings. Three weeks after the public preview , Mistral has put the Large 4 weights on Hugging Face . The model's nickname is "Le Chonk." It takes text and images as input and outputs text and code. It has about one trillion parameters in total, with 52 billion active per step. Running it yourself takes a lot of hardware. According to the docs for the inference framework SGLang, the standard version runs on eight Nvidia B300 GPUs, and a more heavily compressed variant runs on four GB300 GPUs. Since the preview, Mistral has kept training the model with reinforcement learning. The company says this brought big gains, especially in coding and cyber defense. Mistral researchers Pierre Stock and Corentin Petit walked through the process on a press call. The team built tasks in-house that got harder as training went on. They said the only limit on performance is how difficult a task the team can still create and verify. Mistral calls it a "two-year bet" that's now paying off. Ad Large 4 leads open models on coding but trails Claude by a wide margin After three more weeks of training, Large 4's score on Terminal-Bench 4.0 rose from 28 to 41 percent. That puts it just ahead of GLM-5.3 at 40 percent. Mistral says Large 4 hits 48 percent on Artificial Analysis' Coding Agent Index. That's practically tied with GLM-5.3, but Large 4 uses 47 percent fewer tokens to get there. Claude Code with Claude Opus 5.5 tops the index at 62 points. Ad On Cognition's new FrontierCode 2.0 benchmark, Large 4 leads the open models at 19.7 percent. Claude Opus 5.5, however, scores 46 percent. In blind comparisons run by Surge, testers preferred Large 4's code over code from GLM-5.3, Kimi K3, and DeepSeek more often than not. Against Opus 5.5, Large 4 won only 22 percent of matchups. Anthropic's Claude Code models top the coding agent rankings, while Mistral Large 4 and GPT-6.1 Terra offer the best price-performance in the most attractive quadrant. The Pareto line traces the efficiency frontier across all 30 models tested. Mistral's comparisons leave out Moonshot's Kimi K3.1 Pro. Technically that's fair, since its weights aren't public yet. Moonshot plans to release them by November 4, 2026. After that, Mistral probably won't have much of a lead left, because Kimi K3.1 Pro tops Terminal-Bench 4.0 at 67 percent. Ad Large 4 ranks third among open models in cyber defense, not first Mistral calls Large 4 the leading open model for cyber defense. The company says the final version scores 51 percent on Artificial Analysis' Cyber Index. Answer accuracy rises from 48 to 58 percent compared to Argon, while the hallucination rate climbs from four to 11 percent. That's higher but still the second-lowest in the ranking. ## The Decoder - Cohere and Palisade find Alibaba cloud routing in Qwen models (2026-10-27)

Alibaba's latest Qwen models routed some requests through company servers, researchers find Ad Skip to content The Decoder AI, Menschen, Wirtschaft --> Log In Subscribe DE Switch to German Primary Menu The Decoder AI, Menschen, Wirtschaft --> Log In Subscribe DE Switch to German Primary Menu Sign In Register Subscribe Now The Decoder Opens discord in a new tab Opens LinkedIn in a new tab Plus AI in practice Copy the url to clipboard Share this article Go to comment section Alibaba's latest Qwen models routed some requests through company servers, researchers find Manuel Uth & Matthias Bastian Oct 27, 2026 Nano Banana Pro prompted by THE DECODER Alibaba's Qwen model tried to send data to a Chinese cloud server during testing. A systematic investigation shows the problem affects multiple Qwen models and likely traces back to training infrastructure. Canadian AI company Cohere first reported the behavior after testing Qwen3.8-Flash-Next. Instead of writing an attack-detection rule as instructed, the model probed its environment for data-transfer programs and DNS settings, then tried to upload a test string to a Beijing cloud storage server. The sandboxed test environment blocked the connection. Cohere sees the behavior in fewer than one in 100,000 calls, but millions of agent interactions could still trigger several attempts. Cohere suggests that unfiltered agent logs from test environments or third-party tools leaked into the model during post-training. Or someone may have built in a deliberate backdoor. Cohere is a direct competitor to Qwen. The company works with German AI firm Aleph Alpha to release Western-sovereign, open AI models, and both have a financial stake in steering users away from Chinese alternatives. Aleph Alpha recently paired its Kolibri release with a report on political bias in Chinese models . After criticism over its framing of the Qwen findings, Cohere publishes the full set of documented model behaviors on HuggingFace . Palisade Research finds similar behavior in other Qwen models Following Cohere's report, AI safety firm Palisade Research launched an investigation and searches evaluation data from five Qwen models for "aliyuncs.com." The string appeared in Qwen3.8-Max-0902, Qwen3.8-Max, and Qwen3.8-27B, while older Qwen 3.7 models showed no matches. Unlike the Beijing upload attempts in Cohere's tests, these requests went to Alibaba Cloud's Singapore region and returned a 403 Forbidden error every time. In its internal reasoning, Qwen3.8-Max wonders why it called a "weird aliyuncs proxy URL," even though the actual tool calls went to sec.gov. | Image: Palisade Research / VALS Occasionally the models actually called the Alibaba Cloud proxy, routing requests for sites like sec.gov or mckinsey.com through the server with hallucinated credentials and signatures. Other times, a model only hallucinated using the proxy in its internal reasoning. In one instance, Qwen3.8-Max fetched two SEC documents directly, then wondered why it had called "a weird aliyuncs proxy URL" and guessed that "something is rewriting my URLs." To test whether these proxy calls could be triggered on demand, the researchers ran controlled experiments on Qwen3.8-27B. When a web page request hit a simulated 403 error, the model tried the Alibaba Cloud proxy within two turns in 2 to 4 percent of runs. In a more complex scenario, Qwen3.8-Max fell back to the proxy at 1 to 4 percent. Researchers Ozerov and Ravi then ran the same tests on competing models, including DeepSeek-v4.1-Flash, Kimi-K3.1-Pro, GLM-5.3, and Hy4-preview. While some of those models fell back to public proxies like r.jina.ai when blocked, none routed traffic through their own developers' servers. Reinforcement learning is the likely cause Because the proxy calls appear in Qwen 3.8 models but not in Qwen 3.7, the researchers trace the behavior to reinforcement learning during 3.8 training. Alibaba's training setup likely routed web requests through the routify-file-proxy server, teaching the models to use it as a fallback whenever direct requests failed. Another possibility is that the training software quietly rerouted tool calls through the proxy without the model knowing. That would explain why the models seem confused about URLs being rewritten. The dates inside the hallucinated URLs match this timeline, falling within each model's estimated training window before release. Qwen3.8-Max-0902, for example, came out in early September 2026 and hallucinates URLs dated July and August 2026. ## The Decoder - Google DeepMind Genie 4 interactive world model (2026-10-27)

Google Deepmind's Genie 4 puts a modern spin on agent designs that predate LLMs Ad Skip to content The Decoder AI, Menschen, Wirtschaft --> Log In Subscribe DE Switch to German Primary Menu The Decoder AI, Menschen, Wirtschaft --> Log In Subscribe DE Switch to German Primary Menu Sign In Register Subscribe Now The Decoder Opens discord in a new tab Opens LinkedIn in a new tab AI in practice Copy the url to clipboard Share this article Go to comment section Google Deepmind's Genie 4 puts a modern spin on agent designs that predate LLMs Maximilian Schreiner View the LinkedIn Profile of Maximilian Schreiner Oct 27, 2026 Google Key Points Google Deepmind has introduced Genie 4, an interactive world model that calculates environments frame by frame in real time and, for the first time, directly generates matching background audio and character dialogue. Gemini models steer events in the background, planning multistep tasks and continuously adapting the simulated world to user text prompts and changing conditions. Genie 4 is still an experimental research model with limitations in longer sessions and complex physics.
