Ask about this article… <input type="text" id="heise-io-chat-trigger-input" name="search" class="text-(--heise-primary)-70 text-sm rounded-sm focus:ring-blue-500 focus:border-blue-500 block w-full p-1" placeholder="Ask about this article…"> Search Genie 4 is supposed to simulate interactive worlds in real time, including sound and talking characters. Gemini models plan in the background what happens next in the simulation. Only select testers can try the world model for now. Google Deepmind has introduced Genie 4 , the successor to Genie 3 , which the company showed in August 2025. The world model doesn't produce finished videos but calculates a scene frame by frame, reacting to input as it goes. Users move through the scene with the keyboard and steer events with text prompts. According to Deepmind, Genie 4 is the company's first real-time world model that generates sound directly. The company says each world has its own background audio, such as wind in the trees. Genie 4 also handles dialogue, with characters that are supposed to talk to each other and react in real time to user input or events. Ad Deepmind sees the biggest gain in realism in how the model renders people. Characters are supposed to move naturally and react to their surroundings, and users should be able to control them directly. Ad Gemini models plan ahead to keep the world coherent According to Deepmind, Genie 4 builds on capabilities of the video model Gemini Omni so the world looks and behaves realistically. Other Gemini models help plan the simulation to keep it consistent. If a user changes the weather with a text prompt, Gemini is supposed to adapt the instruction to the running scene. Deepmind says Gemini also steers events between inputs so the world keeps developing in a way that makes sense. Ad Deepmind explains the process with multistep tasks. Gemini 3.8 Flash watches the scene continuously, breaks a goal into steps, and uses new frames to check whether each step worked. If the world changes or the user steps in, the model adjusts its plan. Deepmind is also testing Gemini 4 Argon to tie its Genie work to its robotics research, Gemini Robotics. In one demo, Argon breaks the task of playing checkers into steps. Genie 4 gets these as text instructions and uses them to simulate Apptronik's humanoid robot Apollo playing the game, with no real robot involved. Ad Longer sessions still trip up the model Deepmind calls Genie 4 an early, experimental model. According to the company, the world stays consistent over short sessions, but visual details can shift and objects don't always persist in longer simulations or after several complex prompt changes. Ad Deepmind says it's still researching complex physical interactions, accurate real-time sound, and 


## Anthropic - Introducing the Anthropic Cyber Mission (2026-10-08)

Introducing the Anthropic Cyber Mission \ Anthropic Skip to main content News Research Policy Commitments Company Try Claude Chapter 01 : Defending critical infrastructure Introducing the Anthropic Cyber Mission 01 Defending critical infrastructure 02 Securing open-source software 03 Why we're doing this 04 The path ahead Published Oct 8, 2026 Category Security Topics Cybersecurity Open source Partnerships National security Beneficial Deployments See more... Related articles Frontier Red Team Oct 22, 2026 LLMs are making formal software verification feasible at a much larger scale View more Today we’re launching the Anthropic Cyber Mission, a long-term commitment to securing the systems everyone depends on. The Cyber Mission is a new effort to support defenders with tools, research, and resources to secure their software and systems. We’re starting with two areas: Critical infrastructure: Starting with securing the operational technology behind power grids, water systems, and transportation networks, and protecting government systems. Today, we’re introducing the Critical Infrastructure Defense Program (CIDP), which brings frontier models, on-site engineers, and threat research to the defenders that protect operational technology. Open-source software: Finding vulnerabilities and proposing patches in the free, shared code that most software depends on, and hardening the underlying code. Today, we’re launching OSS Scanner , which offers open-source projects regular security scans from our strongest models, for free. Frontier models can be misused to exploit vulnerabilities and conduct cyber operations. Despite best efforts, many areas of technology remain dangerously exposed. State-sponsored adversaries have spent years gaining footholds in these systems, across many sectors, so that they can disrupt them. Defenders of critical infrastructure and the OSS community have decades of security experience but have faced severe resource shortages that are exacerbated by this moment. We are recognizing their expertise in these domains and offering our support. We will also expand the Cyber Mission to release tools, research, and resources in new areas to help secure the world. As part of Project Glasswing , partners uncovered many vulnerabilities, but we haven’t yet achieved a sufficient reduction in cyber risk. It’s easier than ever to find vulnerabilities, but verifying, prioritizing, and fixing these findings remains challenging. Based on lessons from Project Glasswing, we’re starting these new efforts to support defenders. Earlier this week, we merged Project Glasswing into our expanded Cyber Verification Program , which gives many more defenders access to our most capable models. And through the Anthropic Cyber Mission, we’ll deploy Anthropic engineering talent and provide tools and funding for those who secure critical infrastructure and open-source software. Defending critical infrastructure Power grids, water utilities, factories, and transportation networks run on controllers, control software, and industrial networks built to last for decades. These systems are known as “operational technology,” and they often cannot be taken offline to patch, so known vulnerabilities can stay unresolved for years. Securing them is specialized work: the equipment is proprietary, changes are risky, and a mistake can take down a plant. Operators of every size rely on a small set of trusted providers to support that work, telling them what’s exposed and which fixes to make on a running system. Today, we’re introducing the Critical Infrastructure Defense Program , which brings frontier Claude models, on-site engineers, and our threat research to those trusted providers. Its founding partners are Accenture, Booz Allen, CrowdStrike, Deloitte, Dragos, Hitachi, Insane Cyber, Nozomi Networks, Palo Alto Networks, PwC, and Rockwell Automation. These organizations represent the providers that operators rely on to secure these systems: the consulting and advanced technology companies that run security programs, the security companies that guard the business and industrial networks, and the manufacturers that build and patch the equipment itself. This work is underway. Several partners are currently working with Claude to fix vulnerabilities and help customers do the same. Critical infrastructure is hard to defend in many ways that AI cannot fix, but we believe that frontier models can help find and repair weaknesses before those weaknesses are used to cut off power or make water unsafe. Our first step is to work with a small cohort of providers to learn which strategies are most effective and practical. If your company builds security products or services for critical infrastructure, you can register your interest here . “ As AI continues to advance, it presents an important opportunity to strengthen cybersecurity across critical infrastructure, provided it is applied responsibly, validated rigorously, and deployed with safety and reliability at the forefront. We are pleased to contribute our operational technology and industrial cybersecurity expertise to help develop practical approaches for applying AI in ways that strengthen the resilience of the infrastructure society depends on every day. ” Tony Baker VP, Digital Trust & Chief Product Security Officer, Rockwell Automation “ Through our work with critical infrastructure organizations, PwC’s cyber OT practice has seen firsthand the gravity of consequences cyber breaches can have in these environments. I firmly believe that a coordinated, industry-wide effort is necessary to identify vulnerabilities, mitigate risks, and strengthen the resilience of our critical systems. ” Harshul Joshi Partner, US OT/ICS Security Lead, Cyber Data and Tech Risk, PwC “ Threat actors are using AI to find and exploit exposures at machine speed. By combining Unit 42 threat intelligence and operational expertise with Anthropic’s frontier models, we’re helping operators defend against adversaries looking to attack and disrupt essential services. Together, we are giving defenders the speed and precision needed to close attack paths before exposure becomes impact. ” Sam Rubin Senior Vice President, Unit 42, Palo Alto Networks “ Frontier AI is evolving faster than any single organization can track alone. That’s why the Critical Infrastructure Defense Program matters. It brings together the people who understand these environments with the frontier capabilities now available to defend them. We’re proud to contribute our experience and to learn alongside the other partners working to protect the systems the world depends on. ” Moreno Carullo Co-Founder and CTO, Nozomi Networks “ The sites that need protection most, like remote substations, offshore platforms, and air-gapped plants, are exactly where traditional tools can’t reach and specialists can’t be. What no one had solved was the hours: more data than any team can work through, at more sites than any team can staff. 


## Anthropic - Preparing for the 2026 US midterms (2026-10-14)

Preparing for the 2026 US midterms \ Anthropic Skip to main content News Research Policy Commitments Company Try Claude Chapter 01 : Connecting people to trusted sources Preparing for the 2026 US midterms 01 Connecting people to trusted sources 02 Measuring and preventing political bias 03 How we define misuse Published Oct 14, 2026 Category Safeguards Topics Democratic integrity Bias and fairness Safeguards How people use AI Limits on abuse See more... Related articles Safeguards Apr 24, 2026 An update on our election safeguards View more Earlier this year, we shared an update on our election safeguards , which described how we measure and prevent political bias, detect misuse, and direct people to accurate voting information. Below, we outline how we've built on that work and continued to strengthen our election safeguards ahead of this year’s US midterms. How people use Claude for elections and politics Our goal is for Claude to be a useful tool for political conversations, while preventing election-related abuse. That starts with understanding how people actually use Claude in these contexts. To do this, we used Anthropic Insights to develop a new analysis of 250,000 conversations over the course of a recent one-week period. 1 Today, conversations that touch broadly on politics, public policy, or social issues account for about 5.75% of all conversations in the United States on Claude.ai, our consumer app. This includes people turning to Claude to follow the news, conduct academic or professional research on public policy, learn about candidates, and navigate government services. About 0.51% of all conversations are specifically about elections or voting. As we get closer to Election Day, we expect the share of political and election-related queries to increase . This sample suggests people rarely ask Claude whom or what to vote for. These kinds of requests showed up in about 0.01% of US Claude conversations. More often, we observed people asking Claude questions that could help them reach their own conclusions, like where candidates stand on a particular issue. Requests that violated our election integrity policies were even less common, appearing in about 0.002% of conversations. 2 How many US Claude.ai conversations touch on politics, are about elections, or ask Claude whom or what to vote for. Each dot represents 1 in every 10,000 conversations in a random sample of about 250,000 US Claude.ai consumer conversations from September 21 to 27, 2026, which Claude classified using a privacy-preserving method . Because requests for a recommendation on whom or what to vote for are so rare, we show the upper end of a 95% confidence interval for that group. Examples are illustrative only. Most of the requests we see are informational in nature. People turn to Claude to make sense of geopolitical events, research candidates, draft documents, assist with coursework, and weigh arguments for and against policies. What people ask Claude for in political conversations. Conversations were clustered in a privacy-preserving, bottom-up manner through Anthropic Insights . Connecting people to trusted sources Ahead of Election Day, we've added two ways for people to get reliable information directly in their conversations with Claude. As we shared in April, when people ask Claude.ai about voter registration, polling places, or election dates, we’ll continue to display a banner linking to TurboVote, a nonpartisan resource from Democracy Works . Later this month, Claude will start drawing official voting information from Democracy Works into its responses on Claude.ai for users in the US. Someone asking where to vote or what ID to bring to their polling place will now get an answer specific to their state, without leaving the conversation. These responses draw on publicly available information that Democracy Works collects from state and local election sources. Official voting information presented by Democracy Works. Additionally, Claude will begin showing up-to-date results on election night and in the days that follow, so that when people ask for current vote counts or election results, they'll be shown the latest official numbers directly in their conversation. Measuring and preventing political bias When people ask Claude about politics, they should get accurate and evenhanded answers that don't favor any particular political viewpoint. That’s why we test our models extensively for political bias before every model launch. Claude Opus 5.5, Sonnet 5.5, and Haiku 5.5 are our most evenhanded to date, scoring 99% on our political evenhandedness evaluation . Our latest models also engage more readily with political discussions and decline fewer requests, so people can get the information they came for. We're continuing to refine these evaluations, and also looking beyond them for new ways to pressure-test how our models handle political topics. As part of that effort, we asked three independent groups to review how Claude handles free expression, each from a different angle with different methodologies. The Future of Free Speech , an independent think tank at Vanderbilt University, tested Claude on hundreds of requests on contentious topics, and the Foundation for American Innovation compared how Claude responded to matched prompts from the left and right, ranging from mainstream policy debates to more extreme scenarios. On both of these evaluations, most of the responses were rated as strong, engaging substantively with requests. The Collective Intelligence Project surveyed more than 2,000 people in the US, UK, and India in developing an evaluation. Through a three-stage process, participants proposed principles for handling sensitive topics, ranging from health to politics, identified topics to test, and helped develop criteria for judging model responses. CIP synthesized this input and tested for agreement across demographic and cultural groups to establish shared principles. These evaluations also i

Related articles Announcements Oct 28, 2026 AI for Science expands to foundations and research institutions View more Anthropic is deepening its support for American scientific research by committing $150 million over three years to the Genesis Mission , a federal initiative to accelerate scientific and technological discovery through AI. The funding will go toward making Claude available to more than 15 agencies that are part of the mission, including NASA, the National Institutes of Health, and the National Science Foundation. We first announced our partnership with the US Department of Energy (DOE) and the Genesis Mission last December . Since then, we have worked with DOE to bring Claude to scientists across the national laboratories. Today, we announced this new commitment as part of the Science: A New Golden Age Summit hosted by the White House Office of Science and Technology Policy in Washington, DC. Supporting American scientists We share the administration’s view, laid out in “ Science: A New Golden Age ,” that AI can have a profound and positive impact on scientific discovery, and we are glad to back its efforts to support American scientists. Researchers at federal agencies and national laboratories work on some of the world’s most difficult scientific problems. Through our commitment, we aim to help them use Claude to meaningfully advance their work. Over the next three years, we will: Provide Claude, Claude Code, and API credits to several hundred Genesis Mission research projects; Partner closely with agencies and national laboratories on the administration’s top science priorities, including fusion energy and quantum computing; and Provide training, onboarding, and technical support to scientists on Genesis Mission projects, and help agencies new to the Genesis Mission get their first projects running. Accelerating scientific research This commitment is part of our broader effort to support scientific research. Earlier this year we launched Claude Science , an AI workbench that integrates the tools and packages researchers most commonly use, and opened 10,000 free and discounted Claude seats for academic scientists. We also expanded our AI for Science program, which gives credits to researchers on high-impact projects, and kicked off a research preview of the Model Hardware Standard , a shared specification for AI agents to safely operate lab instruments. Our commitment to the Genesis Mission is our next step toward helping researchers everywhere. ## Anthropic - Investing in the public benefit (2026-10-26)

Investing in the public benefit \ Anthropic Skip to main content News Research Policy Commitments Company Try Claude Investing in the public benefit Published Oct 18, 2026 Category Announcements Topics Funding Economic Futures Labor market Nonprofits Leadership See more... Related articles Announcements Jul 22, 2026 A research agenda for the Economic Futures Research Fund View more Today, we’re committing a portion of Anthropic’s shares to the Anthropic Public Benefit Fund, a new wholly owned entity with a structure that increases the stake as the company grows. Subject to stockholder approval, 5% of Anthropic’s shares will be committed today, with an additional 2% added at the board’s discretion for every 50% increase in the company’s value above a set baseline. As a public benefit corporation, most of our work in pursuit of our mission takes place inside the company: research relevant to AI safety and interpretability; applied biomedical research; discounts on Claude for nonprofits and scientific institutions. These activities have both a public benefit component and for-profit value, so we house them directly in the company. The Anthropic Public Benefit Fund will have increased flexibility and scale, enabling us to pursue our mission in new ways: for example, we might explore large donations to traditional nonprofits or direct distributions to specific populations. One example is labor market impacts. AI may generate enormous economic value, but it could also cause instability for many workers. Anthropic has already committed $200 million to studying and addressing this issue, but given the pace of AI progress, bolder approaches may be needed. The Fund will have the versatility to pursue them. We’re establishing the Anthropic Public Benefit Fund as an LLC—rather than a 501(c)(3) or other format—to give adjustability between different forms of giving. None of the Anthropic equity held by the LLC will be used for lobbying or political giving, in the US or abroad. Because of the way the Fund is structured, the more Anthropic grows, the larger the share of the company that can be dedicated expressly to the public good. It is common practice for tech companies to offer escalating compensation awards to their executives. We are instead directing that equity to the public benefit. This builds on commitments our co-founders and employees have already made. When Anthropic was founded, our co-founders pledged to donate 80% of their equity holdings, and since founding we have also operated an equity donation match program that allows employees to pledge equity which Anthropic matches. Approximately half of our employees participate in this program. When combined with today’s announcement, roughly 20% of Anthropic shares will be dedicated to the public good, plus any additional percentage that could be added if the company reaches specific valuation milestones. In the coming months, we will share more about the Anthropic Public Benefit Fund’s structure and governance, including the valuation milestones that may add to its stake. We will also report on our progress to date against our mission in an upcoming public benefit report. Open call for guest investigators in the life sciences \ Anthropic Skip to main content News Research Policy Commitments Company Try Claude Open call for guest investigators in the life sciences Published Oct 22, 2026 Category Announcements Topics Life sciences Related articles Sep 23, 2026 Claude discovers a novel enzyme system with CRISPR-like repeats View more Today, we’re inviting members of the life sciences community to apply to work with us on special projects. We’re looking for proposals from principal investigators and their research groups at academic institutions, nonprofits, or government labs who want to use Claude to investigate key questions in causal human biology, structural and computational molecular biology, medicinal chemistry, drug discovery, and clinical science. You can submit a proposal for a project here . Over 8–12 weeks, guest investigators will work directly with Anthropic researchers on a problem of interest and produce the analyses, artifacts, and tools that will be most helpful for them in their work. Our team will run our models and harnesses at no cost to them. We’ll work together to publish these outputs and open-source the accompanying data and code. Guest investigators retain the rights to their data at every step during their project. They will also have the rights to any independent follow-on research that might come out of our collaboration. We’re looking for applicants who can clearly explain how they would benefit from direct collaboration with Anthropic’s researchers. Applicants should also make a reasonable case that their outputs (e.g., preliminary discoveries, answers to open questions, open-source datasets, or public resources) can be produced within the 8- to 12-week collaboration period. There may be an option for extension beyond the initial collaboration period for some projects. We understand that the results for most open problems of interest will take much longer to validate, but we’re looking for proposals that can produce some preliminary evidence in a relatively short amount of time. We aren’t asking for guest investigators to give us their data, either in the application or during the collaboration, and we’re requesting that applicants include only publicly available information in their application materials. Applications will be open for approximately 14 days, and we’ll strive to notify selected participants by email approximately 14 days after applications close. We look forward to potentially expanding this effort in the future as we learn more about how we can best support the scientific community. This program is one of several initiatives Anthropic is pursuing to invest more heavily in the life sciences and the broader scientific research community. Scientists who would like direct access to our most capable publicly available models for biology can apply to join our Life Sciences Verification Program . We’ve also continued to support researchers through our AI for Science initiative , which provides scientists with Claude credits to accelerate their progress toward making scientific discoveries. A US state IT director used Claude Code to document 12 legacy code repositories, mapping 467 database tables and flagging 27 risks along the way. More than half of the US states, some of the largest localities, and some of the countries largest critical infrastructure operators participated in our SLTT cyber defense program. Agencies building their own tools can use Claude through Amazon Bedrock or Google Cloud’s Vertex AI. ## HN discussion - The Astra can read and write weights to improve Qwen post is misleading (2026-11-03)

The 'Astra can read and write weights to improve Qwen' post is misleading · honnibal.dev honnibal.dev The 'Astra can read and write weights to improve Qwen' post is misleading 2026-11-03 · 5 minute read Stage magicians often do the same trick multiple ways. One iteration uses a gimmick prop, the next one shows you the prop isn’t gimmick but that one palms the object, the third time was a forced choice etc. When you watch the performance you assume there’s only one technique, so each time they do it they seem to debunk your hypothesis about how it must be working. This is pretty much how the experiments in the ‘Astra can read and write weights to improve Qwen’ post work. The post’s abstract says: Just by looking at the raw weights, GPT-6 Astra can tell what a network was trained to do. And without any environment feedback, it has no trouble writing, from scratch, small game-playing networks that outplay networks trained with PPO for 100 million steps. Likewise, asked to make Qwen3.5-0.8B better at math without gradients, Astra identifies an issue in its weights causing reasoning loops that Qwen’s own model card warns about, and fixes this by natively editing 0.016% of the weights. With the same sampling settings, this weight correction raises MATH-500 from 7.9% to 48.1% and generalizes out of the box to instruction following, raising IFEval by 3x. If you read that, would you assume that the Qwen experiment worked ‘just by looking at the raw weights’? Well it doesn’t. There’s experiments that look at raw weights, and experiments that improve a game-playing network, and an experiment that improves Qwen at maths. But those latter two aren’t just working with raw weights, they write Python programs. Here’s a summary of what the post actually does and how the techniques work. # Experiment Size Reads raw weights? Writes weights? Python? Runs network? Result 1 Name the game a PPO MLP was trained on 17K Yes No No No 4/4 correct 2 Name the shape a diffusion MLP generates 24K Yes No No No 3/3 correct 3 Name a char transformer’s language and copying heads 7.2M No No Yes No 3/3 correct 4 Fix a stuck Kinetix car policy 172K Yes Yes No No¹ 0% → 100%² 5 Write MinAtar game MLPs from scratch 20K No³ Yes No No Beats PPO-100M⁴ 6 Write a 9×9 Go network 1.5M No Yes Yes Yes (0–50 passes) Beats PPO 7a Spot the sycophantic Gemma 3 270M No No Yes No Failed 7b Same, seeing attention patterns 270M No No Yes Yes⁵ 3/3 correct 8 Fix Qwen3.5’s maths 800M No Yes Yes Yes (50 passes) 7.9% → 48.1% 8b Same, on Gemma 3 270M No Yes Yes Yes (50 passes) Failed ¹ Given one recorded episode of the failure. ² In one try, changing 3.5% of the weights. ³ Weights start at zero, so there’s nothing to read. ⁴ Beats PPO-500M only on Breakout (517 vs. 25); loses to it on Seaquest (53 vs. 89) and Asterix (53 vs. 55). ⁵ Runs up to 16 texts and sees only the attention patterns, not the outputs. The “just by looking at the raw weights” setting (raw floats in context, no tools, no running the network) only appears in experiments 1, 2 and 4, on networks of 17K–172K weights. Every experiment involving a real language model uses Python, and the Qwen result, which is the headline one, also gets to run the model on up to 50 batches of practice problems. What the Qwen result actually shows is that Astra can be prompted to devise and perform tasks that a human ML engineer could do. Human ML engineers write snippets of code that analyse or even modify network weights “directly” all the time. Figuring out how to do this productively is not nothing, and AI models of a year or two ago would have failed at all these tasks. But to me the Qwen result and the other experiments that use Python don’t belong in the same post or paper as the experiments that read the weights directly. A single technique that could achieve both, “Author weights for a small game from scratch” and “Improve the performance of a large model on a sample task” would imply a deep capability. If you test a single technique that way you’ve demonstrated a sort of ‘gestalt’ of the weights (it can write them out from scratch) and also that it understands a large existing network. But that’s not at all true here, because it’s not a single technique. Matthew Honnibal Computational linguist from Sydney based in Berlin, Germany. I'm the author of the spaCy Natural Language Processing library and the co-founder of Explosion , where we're building Ellf : a platform that helps your team and coding agents build custom, fully private AI solutions in-house. honnibal.dev
